Entropy, Lyapunov exponents, and rigidity of group actions
Aaron Brown with 4 appendices by Dominique Malicet, Davi Obata, Bruno Santiago and Michele Triestino, Sébastien Alvarez and Mario Roldán Edited by Michele Triestino

Abstract
. This text is an expanded series of lecture notes based on a 5-hour course given at the workshop entitled Workshop for young researchers: Groups acting on manifolds held in Teresópolis, Brazil in June 2016. The course introduced a number of classical tools in smooth ergodic theory—particularly Lyapunov exponents and metric entropy—as tools to study rigidity properties of group actions on manifolds. We do not present a comprehensive treatment of group actions or general rigidity programs. Rather, we focus on two rigidity results in higher-rank dynamics: the measure rigidity theorem for affine Anosov abelian actions on tori due to A. Katok and R. Spatzier and recent the work of the author with D. Fisher, S. Hurtado, F. Rodriguez Hertz, and Z. Wang on actions of lattices in higher-rank semisimple Lie groups on manifolds We give complete proofs of these results and present sufficient background in smooth ergodic theory needed for the proofs. A unifying theme in this text is the use of metric entropy and its relation to the geometry of conditional measures along foliations as a mechanism to verify invariance of measures. MSC2010: Primary 22F05, 22E40. Secondary 37D25, 37C85.



Preface

This collective work originates from the workshop for young researchers
Groups acting on manifolds
held in Teresópolis, June 20-24 2016. The structure of the text respects the format of the event: Aaron Brown was lecturing about rigidity of smooth group actions, but other talks were given by young researchers and devoted to explain background notions. We thank all the participants, whose active presence contributed to a very pleasant scientific experience. The event was sponsored by the French-Brazilian Network in Mathematics, CNPq, UFF and EDAI.

Smooth ergodic theory takes a prominent rôle in the modern theory of differentiable dynamical systems. Typically one is interested in studying iterations of one single map, or a flow; this can be done under many different aspects, one of the most successful being the description of statistical behaviors, or the ergodic properties. That is, given a probability measure which is invariant for the dynamics, one wants to describe the distribution of the orbits, the typical rates of contraction/expansion, etc. One of the difficulties, or the richness, is that a given system usually admits different invariant measures, and for each measure the statistical descriptions may differ in a significant way. The book
[
BDV
]
was giving a good account of the state of the art about a decade ago (see also
[
AA
,
Mane
]
as more classical references).

The study of dynamical systems can be enlarged to include also general
group actions
on manifolds. This was historically motivated by geometry and foliation theory, but since the appearance of hyperbolic dynamical systems, dynamicists found an own interest for it. A starting point for what will be discussed in this text, goes back to works of Hirsch, Pugh and Shub
[
HPS
,
PS
]
, where the notion of
Anosov action
first appeared.
Not all isomorphism classes of groups are adapted to usual dynamical tools, and one usually restricts the attention to abelian, or amenable groups, or at least to groups containing “large” abelian subgroups.
Compared to the previous discussion, a first relevant difference for group actions is that
invariant probability measures (for the full group!) in general do not abound. In the first part of this work, Aaron Brown presents a pioneering result by Katok and Spatzier
[
KS1
,
KS2
]
stating that, under suitable hypotheses, invariant ergodic probability measures for the action of a higher rank abelian group must be of
algebraic nature
; see Theorem
2.8
. Indeed, the main source of examples of Anosov actions is given by Lie groups, notably by discrete diagonalizable groups of matrices acting on homogeneous spaces. One usually refers to these kinds of results as
measure rigidity
.

The term
rigidity
here will also refer to a different, but closely related setting: the so-called
Zimmer program
. Before explaining it, let us make a preliminary digression. Instead of discrete groups, we first focus on Lie groups. The celebrated Montgomery–Zippin theorem
[
MZ
]
tells that the topological structure is intimately connected to geometry; one can go even further:

Conjecture

(Hilbert–Smith)
.

If a locally compact topological group
G
acts faithfully on
some connected
n
-manifold
M
, then
G
is a Lie group.

This conjecture has been validated only for dimension
n\leq 3

[
Pardon
]
(actually, the conjecture reduces to prove that a group of
p
-adic integers
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
admits no faithful actions on manifolds). One may further ask, given a Lie group
G
, what is the lowest dimension
n(G)
such that
G
admits a faithful action on a manifold of dimension
n
? For example, the group
\mathrm{PSL}(n,\mathbb{R})
acts on the real projective space
\mathbb{R}P^{n-1}
, which is of dimension
n-1
, but it cannot act on a lower dimensional space. In general, for a simple Lie group
G
, the optimal lower bound depends on the maximal parabolic subgroup of
G

[
Stuck
]
, but a satisfactory bound can be given in terms of the so-called
(real) rank
of
G
(this is by definition, the dimension of the largest abelian subalgebra
\mathfrak{A}
of
\mathrm{Lie}(G)
which satisfies that for every
a\in\mathfrak{A}
, the adjoint operator
ad(a):\mathrm{Lie}(G)\to\mathrm{Lie}(G)
is diagonalizable over
\mathbb{R}
, see also Section
10.2
). Since the fundamental work of Margulis
[
Margulis
]
, it is natural to consider the same kind of question for
lattices
(i.e. discrete, finite covolume subgroups) in simple Lie groups. The so-called
Margulis’s superrigidity
roughly states that every linear representation of a lattice of a simple Lie group of rank
\geq 2
extends to the ambient group (more precisely, modulo finite subgroups and up to some bounded error, see Theorem
12.3
), and therefore all linear representations of lattices are classified. Zimmer program is about the
nonlinear
analogue of superrigidity.

Conjecture

(Zimmer)
.

Let
G
be a simple Lie group with
\mathrm{rank}(G)\geq 2
and
\Gamma
a lattice of
G
. Let
M
be a closed
d
-dimensional manifold, and
\rho:\Gamma\to\mathrm{Diff}(M)
a homomorphism. If
d<\mathrm{rank}(G)
then
\rho
has finite image.

More generally, it is conjectured that a lattice
\Gamma\subset G
cannot act (with infinite image) on a closed manifold whose dimension is lower than the least dimension
n(G)
introduced above. For detailed discussions, we recommend the expository works by David Fisher
[
F1
,
F2
]
.
Very recently, Aaron Brown, in collaboration with David Fisher and Sebastian Hurtado, solved Zimmer’s conjecture
[
BFH
,
BFH-SL
]
(some additional hypotheses are required, see Theorem
11.6
). The third part of these notes discusses the main ingredients of their work. As for measure rigidity, the theory of nonuniformly hyperbolic dynamical systems takes the major part. The works by Ledrappier–Young
[
LY1
,
LY2
]
on the relationship between
Lyapunov exponents
and
entropy
are of notable relevance here. These are very deep works, but we hope the reader will find a suitable introduction here. The main notions and results are recalled throughout the text, especially in the second part and in the appendices by Bruno Santiago and myself, Davi Obata, Sébastien Alvarez and Mario Roldán. Another important ingredient of similar flavor is the work of Ratner on unipotent flows, but we do not treat it in detail, as several very good introductions are available (in primis
[
WitteRatner
]
).

One delicate aspect in Zimmer’s conjecture is about
regularity
: in
[
BFH
,
BFH-SL
]
the authors require the action to be by
C^{2}
diffeomorphisms, that is
\rho(\Gamma)\subset\mathrm{Diff}^{2}(M)
. This is a very mild condition, at least compared to the previous approaches appearing in the literature, which had strong requirements such as invariant volume or geometric structures, or the action to be by real-analytic diffeomorphisms or on low dimensional manifolds
[
CZ
,
Zimmer
,
FS
,
Ghys
,
WitteLine
,
BM
,
Polterovich
,
FH1
,
FH2
]
. We recommend the beautiful collection of contributions
[
FF
]
for more detailed discussions on these works (and much more!).

As this text is more focused on smooth ergodic theory and applications to rigidity properties, we will shortly mention the other aspects of Zimmer’s conjecture. These include the algebraic properties of Lie groups and their lattices, especially their
rigidity
properties. An essential ingredient of
[
BFH
,
BFH-SL
]
is the
strong property (T)
, introduced by V. Lafforgue
[
Lafforgue
]
and studied by de Laat–de la Salle
[
dldlS
,
dlS
]
which generalizes the more classical Kazhdan’s property (T)
[
BHV
]
and is also enjoyed by lattices in higher-rank simple Lie groups. Very roughly, strong property (T) is a machine to produce invariant vectors for representations as operators of Banach spaces, and is used by Brown–Fisher–Hurtado to reduce the nonlinear problem to a linear one, and then apply superrigidity.

Finally, in the fourth and last part, Aaron Brown discusses further rigidity results that rely on tools of smooth ergodic theory, and which are, in some sense, extensions of Katok–Spatzier
[
KS1
,
KS2
]
discussed in the first part. First, he reviews theorems by Kalinin, Katok and F. Rodriguez-Hertz
[
prKK
,
prKRH
,
prKKRH
]
about rigidity problems for non-uniformly hyperbolic
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
actions on general 3-manifolds. Secondly
Cartan flows
, which constitute an other important class of Anosov actions, are treated. Among these, a classical example is the action of the group
A\subset\mathrm{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})
of diagonal matrices with positive entries, on the homogeneous space
X=\mathrm{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})/\mathrm{SL}(3,\mathbb{Z})
, which has dimension 8. An analogue of Katok–Spatzier result in this setting is motivated by a conjecture by Margulis (see
[
prMargulis
]
) that
A
-invariant ergodic measures should be algebraic: this was solved by Einsiedler–Katok–Lindenstrauss
[
prEKL
]
for measures of positive entropy.

Sections that are not required for the rest of the text or that may be skipped on first reading are marked by an asterisk
\bigast
.

Michele Triestino (editor)

Michele Triestino

Institut de Mathématiques de Bourgogne (IMB, UMR 5584)

9 av. Alain Savary, 21000 Dijon, France

michele.triestino@u-bourgogne.fr

Introduction

1.
Groups acting on manifolds and rigidity programs

In the classical theory of dynamical systems, one typically studies actions of 1-parameter groups: Given a compact manifold
M
, a diffeomorphism
f\colon M\to M

generates an action of the group
\mathbb{Z}
; a smooth vector field
X
on
M
generates a flow
\phi^{t}\colon M\to M
or an action of the group
\mathbb{R}
.
However, one might consider groups more general than
\mathbb{Z}
or
\mathbb{R}
acting on a manifold
M
. Natural families of group actions arise in many geometric and algebraic settings and the study of group actions connects many areas of mathematics including geometric group theory, representation theory, Lie theory, geometry, and dynamical systems.

This text focuses on various
rigidity programs
for group actions. Roughly, such rigidity results aim to classify all actions or all invariant geometric structures (such as closed subsets, probability measures, etc.) under

(1)

suitable algebraic hypotheses on the acting group, and/or

(2)

suitable dynamical hypotheses on the action.

This text primarily takes the first approach: under certain algebraic conditions on the acting group, we establish certain rigidity properties of the action. Specifically, we will consider actions of various
higher-rank
discrete groups: higher-rank, torsion-free abelian groups
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
for
k\geq 2
or lattices
\Gamma
in higher-rank simple Lie groups such as
\Gamma=\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{Z})
for
n\geq 3
. At times we impose certain dynamical hypotheses as well. In particular, in Part
I
we consider certain families of algebraic Anosov actions and will discuss more general results on Anosov actions in this introduction.

Neither this introduction nor this text as a whole gives a comprehensive account of rigidity results for group actions on manifolds. (For instance, we do not discuss the vast literature and many recent results concerning group action on the circle
S^{1}
. See, however
[
MR2809110
]
.) Our goal is rather to give detailed proofs (in simplified examples) of a small number of rigidity theorems coming from higher-rank dynamics and to present the necessary background and constructions in smooth ergodic theory required for these proofs.
This introduction aims to give context to these results and give the reader some familiarity with broader rigidity programs in the literature.

1.1.
Smooth group actions

Let
M
be a compact manifold without boundary and denote by
\mathrm{Diff}^{r}(M)
the group of
C^{r}
diffeomorphisms
f\colon M\to M
. Recall that if
r\geq 1
is not an integer then, writing

r=k+\beta\quad\text{ for $k\in\mathbb{N}$ and $\beta\in(0,1)$},

we say that
f\colon M\to M
is
C^{r}
or is
C^{k+\beta}
if it is
C^{k}
and if the
k
th derivatives of
f
are
\beta
-Hölder continuous.

For
r\geq 1
, the set
\mathrm{Diff}^{r}(M)
has a group structure given by composition of maps.
Given a (typically countably infinite, finitely generated) discrete group
\Gamma
, a
{\boldsymbol{C^{r}}}
action
of
\Gamma
on
M
is a homomorphism

\alpha\colon\Gamma\to\mathrm{Diff}^{r}(M)

from the group
\Gamma
into the group
\mathrm{Diff}^{r}(M)
; that is, for each
\gamma\in\Gamma
the image
\alpha(\gamma)
is a
C^{r}
diffeomorphism
\alpha(\gamma)\colon M\to M
and for
x\in M
and
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in\Gamma
we have

\alpha(\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2})(x)=\alpha(\gamma_{1})\big(\alpha(\gamma_{2})(x)\big).

If the discrete group
\Gamma
is instead
replaced by
a Lie group
G
, we also require that the map
G\times M\to M
given by
(g,x)\mapsto\alpha(g)(x)
be
C^{r}
.

If
\mathrm{vol}
is some fixed smooth volume form on
M
(which we always normalize to be a probability measure) we write
\mathrm{Diff}^{r}_{\mathrm{vol}}(M)
for the group of
C^{r}
-diffeomorphisms preserving

\mathrm{vol}
. A classical result of Moser ensures the
\mathrm{vol}
is uniquely defined up to a smooth change of coordinates
[
Moser
]
.
A
volume-preserving
action is a homomorphism
\alpha\colon\Gamma\to\mathrm{Diff}^{r}_{\mathrm{vol}}(M)
for some volume form
\mathrm{vol}
.

As discussed above, actions of the group of integers
\mathbb{Z}
are generated by iteration of a single diffeomorphism
f\colon M\to M
and its inverse. For instance, given an integer
n>1
, the diffeomorphism
\alpha(n)\colon M\to M
is defined as the
n
th iterate of
f
: for
x\in M

\alpha(n)(x)=f^{n}(x):=\underbrace{f\circ f\circ\dots\circ f}_{n\text{ times}}(x).

Given a manifold
M
, any pair of diffeomorphisms
f,g\in\mathrm{Diff}(M)
naturally induces an action
\alpha\colon F_{2}\to\mathrm{Diff}(M)
of the free group on two generators
F_{2}=\langle a,b\rangle
which is uniquely defined by the conditions
\alpha(a)=f
and
\alpha(b)=g
.
If a pair of diffeomorphisms
f\colon M\to M
and
g\colon M\to M
commute, we naturally obtain a
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-action
\alpha\colon\mathbb{Z}^{2}\to\mathrm{Diff}(M)
given by

\alpha(n,m)(x)=f^{n}\circ g^{m}(x).

1.2.
Rigidity of Anosov diffeomorphisms

As a prototype for general rigidity results discussed below, we recall certain rigidity properties exhibited by Anosov diffeomorphisms
f\colon M\to M
.
We first recall the definition of an Anosov diffeomorphism.

Definition 1.1
.

A
C^{1}
diffeomorphism
f\colon M\to M
of a compact Riemannian manifold
M
is
Anosov
if there is a
Df
-invariant splitting of the tangent bundle
TM=E^{s}\oplus E^{u}
and constants
0<\kappa<1
and
C\geq 1
such that for every
x\in M
and every
n\in\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\|D_{x}f^{n}(v)\|\leq C\kappa^{n}\|v\|

for all
v\in E^{s}(x)

\displaystyle\|D_{x}f^{-n}(w)\|\leq C\kappa^{n}\|w\|

\displaystyle\quad\quad\text{ for all $w\in E^{u}(x)$}.

As a primary example, consider a matrix
A\in\mathrm{GL}(n,\mathbb{Z})
with all eigenvalues of modulus different from
1
. Then, with
\mathbb{T}^{n}:=\mathbb{R}^{n}/\mathbb{Z}^{n}
the
n
-torus, the induced toral automorphism
L_{A}\colon\mathbb{T}^{n}\to\mathbb{T}^{n}
given by

L_{A}(x+\mathbb{Z}^{n})=Ax+\mathbb{Z}^{n}

is Anosov. More generally, given
v\in\mathbb{T}^{n}
we have
f\colon\mathbb{T}^{n}\to\mathbb{T}^{n}
given by

f(x)=L_{A}(x)+v

is an affine Anosov map.
In dimension 2, a standard example of an Anosov diffeomorphism is given by
L_{A}\colon\mathbb{T}^{2}\to\mathbb{T}^{2}
where
A
is the matrix
A=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}2&1\\
1&1\end{array}\right).

As a prototype for
local rigidity
results, it is known (see
[
MR0224110
,
MR0238357
]
,
[
MR1326374
, Corollary 18.2.2]
) that Anosov maps are
structurally stable
: if
f
is Anosov and
g
is
C^{1}
close to
f
then
g
is also Anosov and there is a homeomorphism
h\colon\mathbb{T}^{n}\to\mathbb{T}^{n}
such that

(1.1)

h\circ g=f\circ h.

The map
h
is always Hölder continuous but in general need not be
C^{1}
even when
f
and
g
are
C^{\infty}
or real-analytic.
The map
h
in (
1.1
) is called a
topological conjugacy
between
f
and
g
.

All known examples of Anosov diffeomorphisms occur on finite factors of tori and nilmanifolds. From
[
MR0271990
,
MR0358865
]
we have a complete classification—a prototype
global rigidity
result—of Anosov diffeomorphisms on tori (as well as nilmanifolds) up to a continuous change of coordinates: If
f\colon\mathbb{T}^{n}\to\mathbb{T}^{n}
is Anosov, then
f
is homotopic to
L_{A}
for some

A\in\mathrm{GL}(n,\mathbb{Z})
with all eigenvalues of modulus different from
1
; moreover there is a homeomorphism
h\colon\mathbb{T}^{n}\to\mathbb{T}^{n}
such that

h\circ f=L_{A}\circ h.

Again, the topological conjugacy
h
is Hölder continuous but need not be
C^{1}
.
Conjecturally, all Anosov diffeomorphisms are, up to finite covers, topologically conjugate to affine maps on tori and nilmanifolds.

1.3.
Actions of higher-rank lattices and the Zimmer program

A principal family of discrete groups considered in this text are lattices
\Gamma
in (typically higher-rank, see Section
10.2
) simple Lie groups
G
. That is, we consider discrete subgroups
\Gamma\subset G
such that
G/\Gamma
has finite volume. Examples of such groups include
\Gamma=\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{Z})
where
G=\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
(which is higher-rank if
n\geq 3
). It is well known that the matrices

\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&2\\
0&1\end{array}\right)

and
\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
2&1\end{array}\right)

freely generate a finite-index subgroup of
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
and thus the free group
F_{2}
is a lattice subgroup of
G=\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
(which has rank
1
.) See Section
10.1
for background and additional details.

1.3.1.
Linear representations

To motivate the results and conjectures concerning smooth actions of such
\Gamma
, first consider the setting of linear representations
\rho\colon\Gamma\to\mathrm{GL}(d,\mathbb{R})
. A linear representation
\pi\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathrm{GL}(d,\mathbb{R})
of the group of integers is determined by a choice of a matrix
A\in\mathrm{GL}(d,\mathbb{R})
; similarly, a linear representation
\pi\colon F_{2}\to\mathrm{GL}(d,\mathbb{R})
of the free group
F_{2}
is determined by a choice of a pair of matrices
A,B\in\mathrm{GL}(d,\mathbb{R}).

These representations may be perturbed to non-conjugate representations
\tilde{\pi}
.

In contrast, for groups such as
\Gamma=\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{Z})
for
n\geq 3
(and other lattices
\Gamma
in higher-rank simple Lie groups), linear representations
\pi\colon\Gamma\to\mathrm{GL}(d,\mathbb{R})
are very rigid as demonstrated by various classical results including
[
Selberg
,
Weil-I
,
Margulis-nonuniformtwo
,
Mostow-book
,
MR0385005
,
MR1090825
]
.
For instance, for cocompact
\Gamma\subset\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
, local rigidity results in
[
Selberg
,
Weil-I
]
established that any representation
\pi\colon\Gamma\to\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
sufficiently close to the inclusion
\iota\colon\Gamma\to\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})

is conjugate to
\iota
.
A cohomological criteria for local rigidity of general linear representations
\pi\colon\Gamma\to\mathrm{GL}(d,\mathbb{R})
was given in
[
MR0169956
]
, further studied in
[
MR0153028
,
MR0173730
]
, and is known to hold for lattices in higher-rank simple Lie groups.
Margulis’s superrigidity theorem (see Theorem
12.3
below and
[
MR1090825
]
) establishes that every linear representation
\pi\colon\Gamma\to\mathrm{GL}(d,\mathbb{R})
extends to a representation
\overline{\pi}\colon\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})\to\mathrm{GL}(d,\mathbb{R})
up to a “compact error;” this effectively classifies all representations
\Gamma\to\mathrm{GL}(d,\mathbb{R})
up to conjugacy.

1.3.2.
Smooth actions of lattices

As in the case of linear representations, actions of
\mathbb{Z}
or
F_{2}
on a manifold
M
are determined by a choice of diffeomorphism
f\in\mathrm{Diff}^{r}(M)
or pair of diffeomorphisms
f,g\in\mathrm{Diff}^{r}(M)
.
Such actions may be perturbed to create new actions that are inequivalent under continuous change of coordinates. In particular, there is no possible classification of all actions of
\mathbb{Z}
or
F_{2}
on arbitrary manifolds
M
. The free group on two generators
F_{2}
and the group
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
(which contains a finite-index subgroup isomorphic to
F_{2}
) are isomorphic to lattices in the Lie group
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
. Both
F_{2}
and
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
admit many actions that are “non-algebraic” (i.e. not built from modifications of algebraic constructions) and the algebraic actions of such groups often display less rigidity then actions of higher-rank groups. For instance, there exists a 1-parameter family of deformations of the standard
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
-action on the
2
-torus
\mathbb{T}^{2}
such that no continuous change of coordinates conjugates the deformed actions to the original affine action. See Examples
10.11
and
10.12
for further discussion.

However, as in the case of linear representations, the situation is expected to be very different for actions by lattices in
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
for
n\geq 3
and other higher-rank simple Lie groups. In particular, the
Zimmer program
refers to a collection of conjectures and questions which roughly
aim to establish analogues of rigidity results for linear representations
\pi\colon\Gamma\to\mathrm{GL}(d,\mathbb{R})
in the context of smooth (often volume-preserving) actions

\alpha\colon\Gamma\to\mathrm{Diff}^{\infty}(M)

or “nonlinear representations.”
In particular, it is expected that all nonlinear actions
\alpha\colon\Gamma\to\mathrm{Diff}^{r}(M)
are, in some sense, of “an algebraic origin.”
We note that genuinely “non-algebraic” actions exist; see for instance the discussion in
Example

10.10
and
[
MR2807830
, Sections 9, 10]
. Thus, a complete classification of all actions of higher-rank lattices up to smooth conjugacy is impossible. However, it seems plausible that certain families of actions (Anosov, volume-preserving, low-dimensional, actions on specific manifolds, actions preserving a geometric structure, etc.) are classifiable and that all such actions are constructed from modifications of standard algebraic actions. See
10.3
for examples of standard algebraic actions.
We refer to the surveys
[
MR2369442
,
MR2807830
,
1711.07089
,
MR1648087
]
for further discussion on various notions of “algebraic actions,” the Zimmer program, and precise statements of related conjectures and results.

For volume-preserving actions, the primary evidence supporting conjectures in the Zimmer program is Zimmer’s superrigidity theorem for cocycles, Theorem
12.2
below. This extension of Margulis’s superrigidity theorem (for homomorphisms) shows that the derivative cocycle of any volume-preserving action
\alpha\colon\Gamma\to\mathrm{Diff}^{r}_{\mathrm{vol}}(M)
is—up to a compact error and measurable coordinate change—given by a linear representation
\Gamma\to\mathrm{GL}(d,\mathbb{R})
.

1.3.3.
Actions in low-dimensions

Precise conjectures in the Zimmer program are easiest to formulate for actions in low dimensions. See in particular
11.1
. For instance, if the dimension of
M
is sufficiently small,

Zimmer’s conjecture
states that all actions should have finite image (see
Definition

10.4
). See Conjectures
11.2
and
11.3
for precise statements of this conjecture. Early results establishing this conjecture in the setting of actions the circle appear in
[
MR1198459
,
MR1911660
,
MR1703323
]
and in the setting of volume-preserving (and more general measure-preserving) actions on surfaces in
[
MR1946555
,
MR2026546
,
MR2219247
]
. See also
[
MR1254981
]
and
[
MR1666834
]
for results on real-analytic actions and
[
MR2103473
,
MR3014483
,
MR3849285
]
for results on holomorphic and birational actions. There are also many results (usually in the
C^{0}
setting) for actions of specific lattices on manifolds where there are topological obstructions to the group acting; a partial list of such results includes
[
MR2807834
,
MR1470739
,
MR2163900
,
MR2493377
,
MR2745276
,
MR3150210
,
Ye1
,
Ye2
]
.
Part
III
of this text presents recent progress towards this conjecture made in
[
1608.04995
]
.

1.3.4.
Local rigidity

Beyond the finiteness of actions in low dimensions, there are a number of local rigidity conjectures that aim to classify perturbations of non-finite actions. We recall one common definition of local rigidity of a
C^{\infty}
group action:

Definition 1.2
.

An action
\alpha\colon\Gamma\to\mathrm{Diff}^{\infty}(M)
of a finitely generated group
\Gamma
is said to be
locally rigid
if, for any action
\tilde{\alpha}\colon\Gamma\to\mathrm{Diff}^{\infty}(M)
sufficiently
C^{1}
-close to
\alpha
, there exists a
C^{\infty}
diffeomorphism
h\colon M\to M
such that

(1.2)

h\circ\tilde{\alpha}(\gamma)\circ h^{-1}=\alpha(\gamma)\quad\quad\text{ for all $\gamma\in\Gamma$.}

In
Definition

1.2
, using that
\Gamma
is finitely generated, we define the
C^{1}
distance between
\alpha
and
\tilde{\alpha}
to be

\max\{d_{C^{1}}(\alpha(\gamma),\tilde{\alpha}(\gamma))\mid\gamma\in F\}

where
F\subset\Gamma
is a finite, symmetric generating subset.

Local rigidity results have been established for actions of higher-rank lattices in many settings. For instance, local rigidity is known to hold for isometric actions by
[
MR1779610
,
MR2198325
]
.
In the non-isometric setting, local rigidity has been established for affine Anosov actions.

Definition 1.3
.

We say an action
\alpha\colon\Gamma\to\mathrm{Diff}(M)
is
Anosov
if
\alpha(\gamma)
is an Anosov diffeomorphism for some
\gamma\in\Gamma
.

See Example
10.5
and
Remark

10.6
for examples of affine Anosov actions of lattices on tori.

For Anosov actions, note that while structural stability (
1.1
) holds for individual Anosov elements of an action, local rigidity requires that the map
h
in (
1.2
) intertwines the action of the entire group
\Gamma
; moreover, unlike in the case of a single Anosov map where
h
is typically only Hölder continuous, we ask that the map
h
in (
1.2
) be smooth.

There are a number of results establishing local rigidity of affine Anosov actions on tori and nilmanifolds including
[
MR1154597
,
MR1631740
,
MR1164591
,
MR1367273
,
MR1740993
,
MR1332408
]
. The full result on local rigidity of Anosov actions by higher-rank lattices was obtained in
[
MR1632177
, Theorem 15]
.
See also related rigidity results including
[
MR1154597
]
for results on deformation rigidity and
[
MR1154597
,
MR1338481
,
MR1058434
,
MR1415754
]
for various infinitesimal rigidity results.
Additionally, see
[
MR2521112
,
MR1826664
]
for local rigidity of closely related actions and
[
MR1421873
]
and
[
MR1632177
, Theorem 17]
for results on the local rigidity of projective actions by cocompact lattices.

1.3.5.
Global rigidity

Beyond the study of perturbations, there are a number of conjectures and results on the
global rigidity
of
smooth actions of higher-rank lattices.
Most global rigidity results in the literature focus on various families of Anosov actions. (Though, see
11.5
for a global rigidity conjecture that is not about Anosov actions.)
Such conjectures and results aim to classify all (typically volume-preserving) Anosov actions by showing they are smoothly conjugate to affine actions on (infra-)tori and nilmanifolds.
See for instance
[
MR1154597
,
MR1380646
,
MR1367273
,
MR1740993
,
MR1401783
,
MR1643954
,
MR1866848
,
MR1826664
]
for various global rigidity results for Anosov actions.

Recently,
[
BRHW1
]
gave a new mechanism to study rigidity of Anosov actions on tori; in particular, it is shown in
[
BRHW1
]
that all Anosov actions (satisfying a certain lifting condition which holds, for instance, when the lattice is cocompact) of higher-rank lattices are smoothly conjugate to affine actions, even when the action is not assumed to preserve a measure. This provides the most general global rigidity result for Anosov actions on tori and nilmanifolds.

1.4.
Actions of higher-rank abelian groups

In Part
I
, the discrete groups we consider are higher-rank abelian groups of the form
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
for
k\geq 2
. We focus on certain affine Anosov actions and aim to classify all invariant measures for such actions.

Recall that Anosov diffeomorphisms
f\colon\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{T}^{d}
on tori are classified up to continuous changes of coordinates. Such maps
f
leave invariant many closed subsets and probability measures on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
of intermediate dimension. (See Proposition
2.1
and nearby discussion.)
For Anosov actions (satisfying certain non-degeneracy conditions) of higher-rank abelian groups
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
, a number of rigidity results show that properties of higher-rank actions are strikingly different from actions of a single Anosov diffeomorphism.
We outline some of these results known to hold in this setting:

(1)

Local rigidity
results—in which perturbations of affine Anosov actions are smoothly conjugate to the original actions—have been established in
[
MR1164591
,
MR1307298
,
MR1632177
]
with the most general results appearing in
[
MR2342454
]
. A partial list of related local rigidity results in the setting of partially hyperbolic actions includes
[
MR2726100
,
MR2838045
,
MR2672298
,
MR2753946
,
MR3395259
,
1510.00848
]
.

(2)

Global rigidity
results—in which all Anosov actions on tori and nilmanifolds are shown to be smoothly conjugate to affine actions—have been established in
[
MR2240907
,
MR2372620
,
MR2322492
,
MR2318497
,
MR2776843
,
MR2983009
,
1801.04904
]
with the most complete result being
[
MR3260859
]
. Under strong dynamical hypotheses, a number of these results including
[
MR2240907
,
MR2372620
,
1801.04904
]
establish global rigidity results without any assumption on the underlying manifold.

(3)

Results classifying all invariant sets (such as showing all closed invariant sets are finite or all of
M
) including Furstenberg’s theorem (
[
MR0213508
]
, Appendix
A
, and Theorem
2.2
below) and
[
MR716835
]
.

(4)

Measure rigidity
results—in which all ergodic, invariant Borel probability measures with positive entropy are shown to be algebraic or smooth—have been established in a number of settings including Theorems
2.5
and
2.8
discussed below and in
[
MR1406432
,
MR2029471
,
MR1062766
]
. See also
[
MR2261075
,
MR2811602
]
for versions of these results in non-linear and non-uniformly hyperbolic settings (discussed in Section
20
) and
[
MR1989231
,
MR2191228
,
MR2247967
,
MR2366231
]
for related results for diagonal actions on homogeneous spaces (discussed in Section
21
.)

1.5.
Rigidity and classification of orbit closures and invariant measures

A direction which is not pursued in this text concerns actions of groups
\Gamma
with much less structure than those considered above. As a prototype, one should consider
\Gamma=F_{2}
, the free group on two generators. Instead of studying all actions of such groups, one might consider families of actions arising from geometric or algebraic constructions or actions satisfying certain dynamical properties.
The aim is then to classify certain dynamically defined objects, such as orbit closures and invariant (or stationary) measures, by showing that such objects are smooth or homogeneous.

Consider a discrete group
\Gamma
and an action
\alpha\colon\Gamma\to\mathrm{Diff}^{r}(M)
on a compact manifold
M
. Given
x\in M
the
orbit
of
x
is

O_{x}:=\{\alpha(\gamma)(x):\gamma\in\Gamma\}

and the
orbit closure
of
x
is
\overline{O_{x}}
, the closure of
O_{x}
in
M
. A probability measure
\mu
on
M
is
\Gamma
-invariant
if for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
and Borel measurable
B\subset M
we have

\mu(B)=\mu\big(\alpha(\gamma^{-1})(B)\big).

Given a probability measure
\nu
on the acting group
\Gamma
, we say that a probability measure
\mu
on
M
is
\nu
-stationary
if for all Borel measurable
B\subset M
we have

\mu(B)=\int_{\Gamma}\mu\big(\alpha(\gamma^{-1})(B)\big)\ d\nu(\gamma).

That is,
\mu
is
\nu
-stationary if it is “invariant on average.” While an action might not admit invariant measures (for instance if the group
\Gamma
is non-amenable), for any measure
\nu
on
\Gamma
there always exists at least one
\nu
-stationary measure (assuming
M
is compact and the action is
C^{0}
.)

For a diffeomorphism
f\colon M\to M
exhibiting strong hyperbolicity properties, there always exist orbit closures that are Cantor sets (of intermediate Hausdorff dimension) and singular invariant probability measures supported on these Cantor sets. This holds, for instance, if
f
is Anosov or preserves an invariant measure with no Lyapunov exponent equal to zero; see Proposition
2.1
and nearby discussion as well as
[
MR0442989
,
MR573822
]
.
Similarly, singular orbit closures and invariant or stationary measures may appear for actions of free groups.

However, there are a number of extremely influential results establishing homogeneity of orbit closures and invariant measures in certain homogeneous or affine settings.
An extremely important setting in which such a program was carried out is Raghunathan’s conjecture (see
[
MR629475
, pg. 358]
) on the homogeneity of orbit closures for unipotent flows on homogeneous spaces. Important special cases of this conjecture were established in many papers including
[
MR0393339
,
MR0578655
,
MR0407233
,
MR0447476
,
MR744294
,
MR629475
,
MR835804
]
. Classification of orbit closures was central to Margulis’s proof of the Oppenheim conjecture
[
MR882782
,
MR993328
]
and later results of Dani and Margulis
[
MR1016271
,
MR1032925
]
. The full conjecture on the homogeneity of all orbit closures and invariant measures for unipotent flows was established by Ratner in a series of papers
[
MR1054166
,
MR1075042
,
MR1135878
,
MR1262705
]
. Similar results in more general homogeneous spaces and using different techniques were obtained in
[
MR1253197
]
.

More recently, there have been a number of breakthroughs in the setting of homogeneous dynamics and Teichmüller dynamics where new techniques are developed to classify orbit closures and invariant and stationary measures for certain families of group actions.
In these settings, a number of common rigidity properties of an action
\alpha\colon\Gamma\to\mathrm{Diff}(M)
are established:

(1)

Stiffness of stationary measures:
all
\nu
-stationary measures are
\Gamma
-invariant (for a finitely supported measure
\nu
whose support generates
\Gamma
).

(2)

Rigidity of invariant measures:
all ergodic,

\Gamma
-invariant measures
are a volume on a ‘nice’ (e.g. homogeneous, affine, or smooth) submanifold.

(3)

Rigidity of orbit closures:
all orbit closures are ‘nice’ submanifolds.

In a homogeneous setting, one may consider the natural action (see Example
10.5
) of a subgroup
\Gamma
of
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{Z})
on the torus
\mathbb{T}^{n}
. In
[
MR2136018
,
MR2182271
,
MR2060998
]
closed invariant sets were classified under various hypotheses on the acting group.
Assuming certain algebraic properties of the group
\Gamma
, in
[
MR2726604
]
and
[
MR2831114
]
all stationary measures are shown to be either supported on a finite set or are the Lebesgue volume on
\mathbb{T}^{n}
and hence are
\Gamma
-invariant; moreover, every orbit is either finite or dense.
Similar results appear in
[
MR2831114
]
for groups of translations on homogeneous spaces and under weaker hypotheses (which allow for orbit closures to be finite unions of proper homogeneous submanifolds) in
[
MR3037785
,
BQIII
]
. See also
[
1708.04464
]
for an application of the method from
[
MR3037785
]
to a certain non-volume-preserving homogeneous action and the recent preprint
[
ELlong
]
that extends many of the above results with fewer algebraic conditions.

In Teichmüller dynamics, an affine but non-homogeneous action of
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
(the natural
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-action on a stratum
\mathcal{H}(\kappa)
in the moduli space of abelian differentials on a surface) is studied in the breakthrough work
[
MR3814652
]
. For the action of the upper-triangular subgroup
P\subset\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
and for certain measures
\nu
on
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
, the
P
-invariant and
\nu
-stationary measures are shown in
[
MR3814652
]
to be
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-invariant and to coincide with natural volume forms on affine submanifolds. This classification of
P
-invariant measures is used in
[
MR3418528
]
to show that
P
- and
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-orbit closures are affine submanifolds.

In inhomogeneous settings, there are a number of families of actions for which a classification of orbit closures and invariant measures is both expected and desired. Such a classification was attained for nonlinear group actions on surfaces (satisfying certain dynamical hypotheses) in
[
1506.06826
]
. Analogous results are expected to hold in higher-dimension.

1.6.
Common themes

We end this introduction by outlining two common themes that recur throughout this text.

1.6.1.
Entropy, exponents, and the geometry of conditional measures

The first major theme that runs throughout this text is the relationship between metric entropy, Lyapunov exponents, and the geometry of measures along foliations and orbits. The most basic relationship between these quantities is expressed in Lemmas
3.6
and
8.4
which, for
C^{2}
(or
C^{1+\beta}
) diffeomorphisms, characterizes measures with zero metric entropy precisely as those measures whose conditional measures along unstable Pesin manifolds are purely atomic.

For measures with positive entropy but failing to attain equality in the Margulis–Ruelle inequality (Theorem
8.6
(1)
below), the Ledrappier–Young entropy formula (Theorem
8.7
below) gives a very general relationship between the geometry of conditional measures on unstable manifolds (specifically, the transverse dimension relative to the stratification into fast unstable manifolds), Lyapunov exponents, and metric entropy.

Our principal interest is in measures which attain equality in the Margulis–Ruelle inequality. (At times we will also be interested in measures that attain the maximal value for entropy conditioned along some expanding foliation or orbit of a group; see Definition
8.3
and (
9.3
), page
9.3
.) For such measures, Ledrappier and Ledrappier–Young showed (see Theorem
9.3
, and (
8.3
), page
8.3
) that conditional measures along unstable manifolds are absolutely continuous with respect to the Riemannian volume. Moreover, Ledrappier explicitly computes the density function of the conditional measures; in the case that the foliation and its dynamics are homogeneous, this yields invariance of the measure along the foliation. See
Proposition

5.8
and
Theorem

9.5
.

Deriving invariance of a measure from entropy considerations underlies the “invariance principle” for linear cocycles in
[
MR850070
]
and its extension to
C^{1}
-cocycles in
[
MR2651382
]
. It is one of the key ideas in the classification theorem of Margulis and Tomanov
[
MR1253197
]
extending and giving some alternative arguments to Ratner’s measure classification theorem. See for example discussion in
[
MR2158954
, Section 5.6]
. Related entropy arguments are used in
[
MR3814652
]
.
The relationship between entropy and the geometry of conditional measures also plays a key role in
[
MR2191228
,
MR2247967
]
.

In this text, we use the relationship between entropy and geometry of conditional measures in our proofs of Theorem
2.8
and Theorem
15.1
(and its extension in Proposition
15.5
.)
In our proof of Theorem
2.8
, we use
Proposition

5.8
(as well as the fact that all foliations considered are one-dimensional) to simplify certain arguments from
[
MR1406432
]
. In the proofs of Theorem
15.1
and Proposition
15.5
, we use Theorem
9.5
to obtain invariance of certain measures under a group action by studying the entropy conditioned along the orbits of the group.

1.6.2.
Linear functionals and higher-rank dynamics

In the proofs of the rigidity results considered in this text, we always reduce part of the proof to studying dynamics of higher-rank groups of the form
\mathbb{R}^{k}
for
k\geq 2
. The proofs of Theorems
2.8
,
11.4
,
11.6
, and
15.1
all use similar tricks that rely on the fact that
\mathbb{R}^{k}
is higher-rank when
k\geq 2
. To each action of
\mathbb{R}^{k}
, we will associate certain dynamically defined linear functionals. In the proof of Theorem
2.8
, these are the Lyapunov exponents. In the proofs of Theorems
11.4
,
11.6
,
15.1
these are the fiberwise Lyapunov exponents and the roots of the Lie algebra (where
\mathbb{R}^{k}\simeq A
is the maximal split Cartan subgroup of diagonal matrices).

The higher-rank tricks we employ are all variations on the following trivial fact: if
\lambda\colon\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}
is a non-zero linear functional and if
k\geq 2
, then there exists
s_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
with
s_{0}\in\ker(\lambda)
and
s_{0}\neq 0
. In the proof of Theorem
2.8
, the selection of such a
s_{0}
ensures there exists nontrivial dynamics acting isometrically along a dynamical foliation (see Lemma
6.2
.)
In the proofs of Theorem
11.4
and
Theorem

15.1
, the higher-rank assumption and the low-dimensionality of the fiber ensures we may find a nontrivial
s_{0}
for which all fiberwise Lyapunov exponents vanish (see (
15.1
), page
15.1
.)
In the proof of
Theorem

11.6
, we use that if
\lambda,\beta\colon\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}
are non-proportional, non-zero linear functionals then we may select
s_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
such that
s_{0}\in\ker\beta
and
\lambda(s_{0})>0
. When
\lambda
is a fiberwise Lyapunov exponent (for some
\mathbb{R}^{k}
-invariant measure) and
\beta
is a root, this implies that
s_{0}
is centralized by a unipotent root subgroup and we can average (the measure) over this subgroup to obtain a new fiberwise Lyapunov exponent (for a new measure)
\lambda^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}
with
\lambda^{\prime}(s_{0})>0
. See Claim
18.1
and the proof of Proposition
17.2
in
18.4
.

Part I
Rudolph and Katok–Spatzier measure rigidity theorems

2.
Furstenberg’s conjecture; Theorems by Rudolph and Katok–Spatzier

2.1.
Furstenberg conjecture

Let
S^{1}=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
be the additive circle. Note that for
k\in\{2,3,4,\dots\}
the map

M_{k}\colon x\mapsto kx\mod 1

is an expanding map of
S^{1}
.
The following properties of
M_{k}
are well known. For instance, using that
M_{k}
is uniformly expanding for
k\geq 2
, one may pass to a symbolic extension and derive such properties using symbolic dynamics of the full
k
-shift.

Proposition 2.1
.

For
k\in\{2,3,4,\dots\}
there exist

(1)

uncountably many mutually disjoint, closed, invariant subsets
\Lambda\subset S^{1}
;

(2)

uncountably many ergodic,
M_{k}
-invariant Borel probability measures
\mu
with positive metric entropy
h_{\mu}(M_{k})
.

Analogous results hold for Anosov diffeomorphisms and Axiom A systems
[
MR0442989
]
and for any
C^{1+\beta}
-diffeomorphism of a surface with positive topological entropy
[
MR573822
]
.

Note that each map
M_{k}
generates an action of the semigroup
\mathbb{N}_{0}
on
S^{1}
. In
[
MR0213508
]
Furstenberg considered the action of the semigroup
\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}
generated by

x\mapsto 2x\mod 1,\quad x\mapsto 3x\mod 1.

Theorem 2.2

(Furstenberg’s theorem
[
MR0213508
]
. See Appendix
A
)
.

The only closed subsets of
S^{1}
that are invariant under both

x\mapsto 2x\mod 1,\quad x\mapsto 3x\mod 1

are finite subsets (of rational numbers) or all of
S^{1}
.

In
[
MR716835
]
, Berend extended Furstenberg’s result to subsets of tori invariant under certain abelian groups of automorphisms. (See also
[
MR2974218
]
for further discussion on higher-rank abelian actions of toral automorphisms and
[
MR2136018
,
MR2060998
,
MR2182271
,
MR2726604
,
MR2831114
,
MR3037785
,
BQIII
]
for results concerning actions by toral automorphisms of more general groups.)

Note that both generators
x\mapsto 2x\mod 1
and
x\mapsto 3x\mod 1
preserve the Lebesgue measure
m
on
S^{1}
. Thus,
m
is invariant under the action of the semigroup
\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}
generated by
M_{2}
and
M_{3}
. Also, for any rational point
p/q\mod 1\in\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}
, the orbit of
p/q
under the action of
\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}
is finite and there exists an
\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}
-invariant measure supported on finitely many points of this orbit.

From Theorem
2.2
and the above observations, it is natural to conjecture the following.

Conjecture 2.3

(Furstenberg’s conjecture)
.

The only ergodic, Borel probability measure on
S^{1}
that is invariant under both

x\mapsto 2x\mod 1,\quad x\mapsto 3x\mod 1

is either supported on a finite set (of rational numbers) or is the Lebesgue measure on
S^{1}
.

Remark 2.4
.

In Conjecture
2.3
, the word
ergodic
means ergodic for the semi-group action generated by
M_{2}
and
M_{3}
. That is, if
\mu
is an
M_{2}
- and
M_{3}
-invariant measure, then
\mu
is ergodic if any measurable set
D\subset\mathbb{T}^{3}
satisfying

M_{2}^{-1}(D)=D\quad\quad M_{3}^{-1}(D)=D

has either
\mu(D)=1
or
\mu(D)=0
. It is possible that
\mu
is ergodic for the
\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}
-action but not ergodic for either of the generators
M_{2}
or
M_{3}
.

2.2.
Rudolph’s theorem

Conjecture
2.3
remains open. Building on previous results (specifically
[
MR941238
]
and
[
MR1194793
]
), Rudolph obtained what is still the optimal partial resolution of Conjecture
2.3
.

To state the result, we refer to the definition of metric entropy
h_{\mu}(f)
for a
\mu
-preserving transformation
f
defined in Section
8.1
below.
If
f\colon X\to X
is a continuous transformation of a compact metric space and if
\mu
is an
f
-invariant measure supported on a finite set then the metric entropy
h_{\mu}(f)
is zero. The converse need not hold; indeed using symbolic dynamics one can build measures
\mu
on
S^{1}
that are ergodic and invariant under
M_{2}
, satisfy
h_{\mu}(M_{2})=0
, and have no atoms and hence have infinite support. Explicit examples of such measures include measures supported on infinite minimal subshifts with zero topological entropy such as Sturmian subshifts and Morse-Thue (and more general substitution) subshifts; see
[
MR1369092
, §13.7]
.

In
[
MR1062766
]
, Rudolph resolved Conjecture
2.3
except, possibly, for zero entropy measures with infinite support.

Theorem 2.5

(
[
MR1062766
]
)
.

The only ergodic Borel probability measure on
S^{1}
that is invariant under both

M_{2}\colon x\mapsto 2x\mod 1\quad\text{and}\quad M_{3}\colon x\mapsto 3x\mod 1

and satisfies

h_{\mu}(M_{2})>0\quad\text{or}\quad h_{\mu}(M_{3})>0

is the Lebesgue measure on
S^{1}
.

2.3.
Katok–Spatzier reformulation

A minor technical nuisance when studying Furstenberg’s conjecture is that the action is noninvertible. That is, the maps

x\mapsto 2x\mod 1,\quad x\mapsto 3x\mod 1

generate an action of the semigroup
\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}
rather than the action of a group.
By passing to the
natural extension solenoid
one can induce an action of the group
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
that contains the
\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}
-action as a topological factor.

One can view the natural extension solenoid as an analogue of 3-dimensional torus except that the solenoid has non-Archimedean directions. A. Katok proposed studying a related action on a more familiar space: the action of two commuting (hyperbolic) automorphisms of
\mathbb{T}^{3}
. One then naturally obtains a version of Furstenberg’s conjecture for
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
-actions by automorphisms of tori and solenoids of arbitrary dimension.
A generalization of Rudolph’s theorem under a number of hypotheses was established in this setting by Katok and Spatzier
[
MR1406432
,
MR1619571
]
.

We will focus on the following concrete example which demonstrates many of the ideas in the paper
[
MR1406432
]
.

Example 2.6
.

Let

(2.1)

A=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}3&2&1\\
2&2&1\\
1&1&1\\
\end{array}\right),\quad\quad B=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}2&1&1\\
1&2&0\\
1&0&1\\
\end{array}\right).

One verifies the following properties of
A
and
B
:

Claim 2.7
.

(1)

\det A=\det B=1
so
A
and
B
preserve the orientation on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and the integer lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{3}
;

(2)

A
has 3 distinct real eigenvalues

\chi_{A}^{1}>1>\chi_{A}^{2}>\chi_{A}^{3}>0;

(3)

B
has
3 distinct real eigenvalues

\chi_{B}^{1}>\chi_{B}^{3}>1>\chi_{B}^{2}>0;

(4)

A
and
B
commute:
AB=BA
;

(5)

A^{k}B^{\ell}=\mathrm{Id}
only when
k=\ell=0
.

As
A
and
B
commute and are diagonalizable over
\mathbb{R}
, they are jointly diagonalizable. The enumerations of the eigenvalues of
A
and
B
are chosen so that
\chi_{A}^{i}
and
\chi_{B}^{i}
correspond to the same joint eigenvector; see (
3.1
) below.

Since both
A
and
B
preserve the integer lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{3}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
, they induce diffeomorphisms

L_{A}\colon\mathbb{T}^{3}\to\mathbb{T}^{3},\quad\quad L_{B}\colon\mathbb{T}^{3}\to\mathbb{T}^{3}

where
\mathbb{T}^{3}
is the quotient group
\mathbb{T}^{3}=\mathbb{R}^{3}/\mathbb{Z}^{3}
and
L_{A}\colon\mathbb{T}^{3}\to\mathbb{T}^{3}
is the automorphism

L_{A}\colon(x+\mathbb{Z}^{3})\mapsto Ax+\mathbb{Z}^{3}.

Note that each of the diffeomorphisms
L_{A}\colon\mathbb{T}^{3}\to\mathbb{T}^{3}
and
L_{B}\colon\mathbb{T}^{3}\to\mathbb{T}^{3}
is an Anosov diffeomorphism.

The maps
L_{A}
and
L_{B}
generate a
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-action
\alpha\colon\mathbb{Z}^{2}\to\mathrm{Diff}(\mathbb{T}^{3})
on the 3-torus given by

\alpha\left(n_{1},n_{2}\right)(x)=L_{A}^{n_{1}}(L_{B}^{n_{2}}(x))=L_{B}^{n_{2}}(L_{A}^{n_{1}}(x))=L_{A^{n_{1}}B^{n_{2}}}(x).

Note that given any
\mathbb{Z}
-action, any homomorphism
\psi\colon\mathbb{Z}^{2}\to\mathbb{Z}
induces a “fake”
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-action where the kernel of
\psi
acts trivially. Claim
2.7
(
5
) ensures the action
\alpha
is not of this form; that is,
\alpha
is a “genuine”
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-action.

In
[
MR1406432
,
MR1619571
]
, Katok and Spatzier proved a generalization of Rudolph’s theorem for
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
-actions on tori and solenoids generated by automorphisms under a number of technical hypotheses. These hypotheses are satisfied by the action in Example
2.6
. We note that some of these hypotheses were later removed in
[
MR2029471
]
.
The main result from
[
MR1406432
]
applies to the action constructed in Example
2.6
and yields the following natural analogue of Rudolph’s Theorem, Theorem
2.5
.

Theorem 2.8
.

Let
L_{A},L_{B}\colon\mathbb{T}^{3}\to\mathbb{T}^{3}
be as in Example
2.6
. Then, the only ergodic, Borel probability measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{T}^{3}
that is invariant under both
L_{A}
and
L_{B}
and satisfies

h_{\mu}(L_{A})>0\quad\text{or}\quad h_{\mu}(L_{B})>0

is the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{T}^{3}
.

The rest of this part will be devoted to proving
Theorem

2.8
. For a more concise yet complete proof of this result, see
[
MR1858547
, Section 2.2]
.

Remark 2.9
.

To generalize the action constructed from
Example

2.6
, let
A\in\mathrm{GL}(d,\mathbb{Z})
be a matrix whose characteristic polynomial is irreducible over
\mathbb{Q}
and has
d
distinct real roots. It follows from Dirichlet’s unit theorem (see
[
MR1949111
, Proposition 3.7]
) that the centralizer of
A
in
\mathrm{GL}(d,\mathbb{Z})
contains
\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}
as a subgroup of finite index. Let
\alpha\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}\to\mathrm{Diff}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
be the induced action. See
[
MR1949111
]
, where such actions of
\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}
on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
are called
Cartan actions
, for further discussion.

The proof we present of
Theorem

2.8
adapts to show the following.

Theorem 2.10

(
[
MR1406432
]
)
.

For
d\geq 3
and any Cartan action
\alpha\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}\to\mathrm{Diff}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
as above, any ergodic,
\alpha
-invariant Borel probability measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
with

h_{\mu}(\alpha(\mathbf{n}))>0

for some
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}
is the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
.

3.
Reductions in the proof of Theorem
2.8

3.1.
Lyapunov exponent functionals

Let
A
and
B
be as in Example
2.6
. Since the eigenvalues of
A
and
B
are distinct real numbers,
A
and
B
are diagonalizable over
\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, since
A
and
B
commute, they are jointly diagonalizable; that is there is a
Q\in\mathrm{GL}(3,\mathbb{R})
such that

(3.1)

Q^{-1}AQ=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\chi^{1}_{A}&0&0\\
0&\chi^{2}_{A}&0\\
0&0&\chi^{3}_{A}\end{array}\right),\quad\quad Q^{-1}BQ=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\chi^{1}_{B}&0&0\\
0&\chi^{2}_{B}&0\\
0&0&\chi^{3}_{B}\end{array}\right).

For
1\leq j\leq 3
, let
E^{j}
denote the
j
th joint eigenspace of
A
and
B
(corresponding to
\chi_{A}^{j}
and
\chi_{B}^{j}
.)
As each
A
and
B
is irreducible over
\mathbb{Q}
, the eigenspaces
E^{j}
are totally irrational: if
\mathbf{v}\in E^{i}\smallsetminus\{0\}
has coordinates
\mathbf{v}=(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})
then
v_{1},v_{2}
, and
v_{3}
are linearly independent over
\mathbb{Q}
.

It is more convenient at times to work with the logarithm of the eigenvalues of
A
and
B
. For
j\in\{1,2,3\}
let

\lambda^{j}_{A}=\log(\chi_{A}^{j}),\quad\quad\lambda^{j}_{B}=\log(\chi_{B}^{j}).

Note that for any
(n_{1},n_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
we have

\displaystyle Q^{-1}A^{n_{1}}B^{n_{2}}Q

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}(\chi^{1}_{A})^{n_{1}}(\chi^{1}_{B})^{n_{2}}&0&0\\
0&(\chi^{2}_{A})^{n_{1}}(\chi^{2}_{B})^{n_{2}}&0\\
0&0&(\chi^{3}_{A})^{n_{1}}(\chi^{3}_{B})^{n_{2}}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}e^{n_{1}\lambda^{1}_{A}+n_{2}\lambda^{1}_{B}}&0&0\\
0&e^{n_{1}\lambda^{2}_{A}+n_{2}\lambda^{2}_{B}}&0\\
0&0&e^{n_{1}\lambda^{3}_{A}+n_{2}\lambda^{3}_{B}}\end{array}\right).

For any
(n_{1},n_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, the subspace
E^{j}
is an eigenspace for
A^{n_{1}}B^{n_{2}}
. Let
\chi^{j}(n_{1},n_{2})
be the eigenvalue of
A^{n_{1}}B^{n_{2}}
corresponding to the eigenspace
E^{j}
. We have

\log(\chi^{j}(n_{1},n_{2}))=n_{1}\lambda^{j}_{A}+n_{2}\lambda^{j}_{B}.

Thus, the map
\lambda^{j}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
given by

\lambda^{j}(n_{1},n_{2})=\log(\chi^{j}(n_{1},n_{2}))=n_{1}\lambda^{j}_{A}+n_{2}\lambda^{j}_{B}

is additive. In particular, each
\lambda^{j}
extends to a linear functional
\lambda^{j}\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
.

Definition 3.1
.

The linear functionals

\lambda^{j}\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
are called the
Lyapunov exponent functionals
for the action
\alpha
.

3.2.
Stable, unstable, and Lyapunov foliations of
\mathbb{T}^{3}

Note that
\mathbb{R}^{3}
acts by translation on
\mathbb{T}^{3}
as does any vector subspace
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
. For
1\leq j\leq 3
and any
x\in\mathbb{T}^{3}
let
W^{j}(x)
denote the orbit of
x
under translation by elements of the vector subspace
E^{j}
:

W^{j}(x)=\{x+v:v\in E^{j}\}.

The sets
W^{j}(x)
form a foliation of
\mathbb{T}^{3}
by lines. We call
W^{j}(x)
the
j
th
Lyapunov manifold
through
x
and call the corresponding foliation the
j
th Lyapunov foliation.

Note that if
x^{\prime}\in W^{j}(x)
with
x^{\prime}=x+v
for some
v\in E^{j}
then for any
(n_{1},n_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
we have
\alpha(n_{1},n_{2})(x^{\prime})\in W^{j}(\alpha(n_{1},n_{2})(x))
and

\alpha(n_{1},n_{2})(x^{\prime})=\alpha(n_{1},n_{2})(x)+e^{\lambda^{j}(n_{1},n_{2})}v.

In particular, the action by
\alpha(n_{1},n_{2})
dilates distances in
W^{j}
-leaves by exactly
e^{\lambda^{j}(n_{1},n_{2})}
.

Given
(0,0)\neq(n_{1},n_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, let

E^{s}_{(n_{1},n_{2})}=\bigoplus_{\lambda^{j}(n_{1},n_{2})<0}E^{j}
and
E^{u}_{(n_{1},n_{2})}=\bigoplus_{\lambda^{j}(n_{1},n_{2})>0}E^{j}

be the
stable
and
unstable
subspaces for the matrix
A^{n_{1}}B^{n_{2}}
.
For
x\in\mathbb{T}^{3}
we similarly define
W^{s}_{(n_{1},n_{2})}(x)
and
W^{u}_{(n_{1},n_{2})}(x)
to be the orbits of
x
under
E^{s}_{(n_{1},n_{2})}
and
E^{u}_{(n_{1},n_{2})}
, respectively. For
(n_{1},n_{2})\neq(0,0)
, the map

\alpha(n_{1},n_{2})=L_{A}^{n_{1}}L_{B}^{n_{2}}\colon\mathbb{T}^{3}\to\mathbb{T}^{3}

is Anosov and

W^{s}_{(n_{1},n_{2})}(x)
and
W^{u}_{(n_{1},n_{2})}(x)
are the stable and unstable manifolds through
x
for the Anosov diffeomorphism
\alpha(n_{1},n_{2}).

Observe

Claim 3.2
.

For any
(n_{1},n_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, any
x\in\mathbb{T}^{3}
, and any
(0,0)\neq(m_{1},m_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}

(1)

\alpha(n_{1},n_{2})(W^{j}(x))=W^{j}(\alpha(n_{1},n_{2})(x))
;

(2)

\alpha(n_{1},n_{2})(W^{u}_{(m_{1},m_{2})}(x))=W^{u}_{(m_{1},m_{2})}(\alpha(n_{1},n_{2})(x))
;

(3)

\alpha(n_{1},n_{2})(W^{s}_{(m_{1},m_{2})}(x))=W^{s}_{(m_{1},m_{2})}(\alpha(n_{1},n_{2})(x))
;

(4)

E^{u}_{(m_{1},m_{2})}
and
E^{s}_{(m_{1},m_{2})}
are positive-dimensional and have complementary dimension in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
;

(5)

the sets
W^{u}_{(m_{1},m_{2})}(x)
and
W^{s}_{(m_{1},m_{2})}(x)
are injectively immersed planes or lines that intersect transversally and have complementary dimension in
\mathbb{T}^{3}
.

Property
(1)
is clear as
E^{j}
is an eigenspace of
A^{n_{1}}B^{n_{2}}.
Properties
(2)
and
(3)
follow from the commutativity of
\alpha(n_{1},n_{2})
and
\alpha(m_{1},m_{2})
. Property
(4)
follows as
\lambda^{j}(m_{1},m_{2})\neq 0
for each
(0,0)\neq(m_{1},m_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and

\lambda^{1}(m_{1},m_{2})+\lambda^{2}(m_{1},m_{2})+\lambda^{3}(m_{1},m_{2})=0

for every
(m_{1},m_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.
Property
(5)
follows from
(4)
and that the spaces
E^{i}
are totally irrational.

Remark 3.3
.

If
\alpha\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}\to\mathrm{Diff}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
is as in
Theorem

2.10
then there are
d
Lyapunov exponent functionals
\lambda^{i}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}\to\mathbb{R}
,
1\leq i\leq d
. Moreover, these are in general position.
Analogous properties to those in
3.2
hold in this case. For instance, we claim
that for each
1\leq i\leq d
there is some
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}
with
\lambda^{i}(\mathbf{n})>0
and
\lambda^{j}(\mathbf{n})<0
for all
j\neq i
; in particular, for such
\mathbf{n}
,
E^{u}_{\mathbf{n}}
is 1-dimensional and
E^{s}_{\mathbf{n}}
is
(d-1)
-dimensional. We actually claim a stronger fact as in
3.2
(4)
: for any non-trivial partition

\{1,\dots,d\}=A\sqcup B,\quad A\neq\emptyset,\quad B\neq\emptyset

there exists
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}
such that

\lambda^{i}(\mathbf{n})<0\quad\text{ for all $i\in A$}

and

\lambda^{i}(\mathbf{n})>0\quad\text{ for all $i\in B$.}

This can be seen by observing there are
2^{d}-2
such partitions. Similarly,
d
hyperplanes in
\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
in general position divide
\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
into
2^{d}-2
connected components each of which corresponds to a different collection of signs.

Recall that the eigenspaces
E^{j}
are totally irrational. In particular, from the unique ergodicity of totally irrational flows on tori, we obtain the following.

Lemma 3.4
.

A Borel probability measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{T}^{3}
is the Lebesgue (Haar) measure if and only if there exists
1\leq j\leq 3
such that the measure
\mu
is invariant under the 1-parameter group of translations generated by
E^{j}
.

Thus, to prove Theorem
2.8
, it is enough to verify that any ergodic,
(L_{A},L_{B})
-invariant measure with positive entropy is invariant under translation by
E^{j}
for some
1\leq j\leq 3
.

3.3.
Conditional measures and leaf-wise measures

(See Appendix
B
,
[
1208.4550
]
,
Section

8.1.1
, and
[
MR2723325
, Section 5]
for additional details and references.)
Let
\mu
be a Borel probability measure on
\mathbb{T}^{3}
. In general, the partition of
(\mathbb{T}^{3},\mu)
into the
j
th Lyapunov manifolds
W^{j}
is not a measurable partition. (See Lemma
3.6
below for precise statement as well as Appendix
B
.)
Let
\xi
be a measurable partition (see
Section

8.1.1
and Definition
B.7
in Appendix
B
) of
\mathbb{T}^{3}

subordinate to
W^{j}
(see Definition
8.2
); that is

(1)

\xi
is a measurable partition of the measure space (
\mathbb{T}^{3},\mu)
;

(2)

\xi(x)\subset W^{j}(x)
for
\mu
-a.e.
x
;

(3)

\xi(x)
contains an open neighborhood of
x
(in the immersed topology) in
W^{j}(x)
for
\mu
-a.e.
x
;

(4)

\xi(x)
is precompact in the immersed topology of
W^{j}(x)
for
\mu
-a.e.
x
;

Let
\{\mu_{x}^{\xi}\}
denote a family of
conditional measures
of
\mu
relative to the partition
\xi
.
That is (see
Definition

8.1
and Definition
B.2
in Appendix
B.2
)

(1)

\mu_{x}^{\xi}
is a Borel probability measure on
\mathbb{T}^{3}
such that
\mu_{x}^{\xi}(\xi(x))=1
;

(2)

if
y\in\xi(x)
then
\mu_{y}^{\xi}=\mu_{x}^{\xi}
;

(3)

if
D\subset\mathbb{T}^{3}
is a Borel set then
x\mapsto\mu_{x}^{\xi}(D)
is measurable and

(4)

\mu(D)=\int\mu_{x}^{\xi}(D)\ d\mu(x)
.

Such a family
\{\mu_{x}^{\xi}\}
of probability measures exists and is unique modulo
\mu
-null sets (see
[
MR0047744
]
.)

Rather than studying conditional probability measures
\{\mu^{\xi}_{x}\}
discussed above that depend on the choice of partition
\xi
, it is more convenient to study a family of
leaf-wise measures
denoted by
\{\mu^{j}_{x}\}
along
W^{j}
. Each measure
\mu^{j}_{x}
in this family is a locally finite, Borel measure (with respect to the immersed topology) on
W^{j}(x)
but is typically an infinite measure. We discuss the properties of these measures and then outline their construction.

Given
x\in\mathbb{T}^{3}
, let

I^{j}_{x}:=\{x+v:v\in E^{j},|v|<1\}

denote the unit ball (i.e. interval) in
W^{j}(x)
centered at
x
.
Given two locally finite, Borel measures
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
on
W^{j}(x)
we say
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
are
proportional
, written
\eta_{1}\,\propto\,\eta_{2}
, if there is
c>0
with

\eta_{1}=c\,\eta_{2}.

Proposition 3.5

(Leaf-wise measures)
.

For almost every
x\in\mathbb{T}^{3}
there is a locally finite, Borel (in the immersed topology) measure
\mu^{j}_{x}
on
W^{j}(x)
such that

(1)

each
\mu^{j}_{x}
is normalized so that
\mu^{j}_{x}(I^{j}_{x})=1
;

(2)

the family
\{{\mu_{x}^{j}}{\restriction_{{I^{j}_{x}}}}\}
of probability measures on
\mathbb{T}^{3}
depends measurably on
x
;

(3)

for
x^{\prime}\in W^{j}(x)
we have
\mu^{j}_{x}\,\propto\,\mu^{j}_{x^{\prime}};

(4)

if
\mu
is
\alpha
-invariant then for any
(n_{1},n_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
we have

\alpha(n_{1},n_{2})_{*}(\mu^{j}_{x})\,\propto\,\mu^{j}_{\alpha(n_{1},n_{2})(x)};

(5)

given any measurable partition
\xi
subordinate to
W^{j}
, the conditional probability measure
\mu_{x}^{\xi}
at
x
is given by

\mu^{\xi}_{x}=\frac{1}{\mu^{j}_{x}(\xi(x))}{\mu^{j}_{x}}{\restriction_{{\xi(x)}}}.

Outline of construction.

To construct the family
\{\mu^{j}_{x}\}
of leaf-wise measures, consider a sequence
\{\xi^{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
of measurable partitions of
\mathbb{T}^{3}
such that

(1)

each
\xi^{k}
is subordinate to
W^{j}
,

(2)

for almost every
x
, we have
\xi^{k}(x)\subset\xi^{k+1}(x)
, and

(3)

for almost every
x
, we have
\bigcup_{k}\xi^{k}(x)=W^{j}(x).

From the uniqueness of conditional measures, for almost every
x
we have that
\mu^{\xi^{k}}_{x}
and
\mu^{\xi^{\ell}}_{x}
coincide on
\xi^{k}(x)\cap\xi^{\ell}(x)
up to normalization: if
\ell\geq k
then

\mu^{\xi^{k}}_{x}=\frac{1}{\mu^{\xi^{\ell}}_{x}(\xi^{k}(x))}{\mu^{\xi^{\ell}}_{x}}{\restriction_{{\xi^{k}(x)}}}.

For each
x
and every
k
sufficiently large so that

I_{x}^{j}\subset\xi^{k}(x),

let

\tilde{\mu}^{\xi^{k}}_{x}=\frac{1}{\mu^{\xi^{k}}_{x}(I_{x}^{j})}\mu^{\xi^{k}}_{x}.

Then, given any compact (in the immersed topology) subset
K\subset W^{j}(x)
, we have for any
k
and
\ell
such that
K\subset\xi^{k}(x)
and
K\subset\xi^{\ell}(x)
that

(3.2)

\tilde{\mu}^{\xi^{k}}_{x}(K)=\tilde{\mu}^{\xi^{\ell}}_{x}(K).

The measure
\mu^{j}_{x}
on
W^{j}(x)
is then defined to be the locally finite Borel measure defined by (
3.2
) for each compact
K\subset W^{j}(x)
.
Properties
(1)
,
(2)
, and
(5)
follow from construction and the properties of the families of conditional measures
\{\mu_{x}^{\xi_{k}}\}.
Property
(4)
follows from the invariance of
\mu
.

For Property
(3)
, note that if
x^{\prime}\in W^{j}(x)
then both
\mu^{j}_{x}
and
\mu^{j}_{x^{\prime}}
are locally finite Borel measures on the same space
W^{j}(x)=W^{j}(x^{\prime})
. Moreover, there is some
\ell
such that
x^{\prime}\in\xi^{\ell}(x)
. Since the conditional measures
\mu^{\xi^{\ell}}_{x}=\mu^{\xi^{\ell}}_{x^{\prime}}
coincide, it follows that the leaf-wise measures
\mu^{j}_{x}
and
\mu^{j}_{x^{\prime}}
are proportional; however, due to the choice of normalization we typically have
\mu^{j}_{x}\neq\mu^{j}_{x^{\prime}}
. ∎

See also Section 6 of
[
MR2648695
]
, especially Theorem 6.3, where the construction of the family of leaf-wise measures is presented in a more general setting.
We emphasize that the topology for which
\mu^{j}_{x}
is Borelian is the
immersed
topology on the submanifold
W^{j}(x)
rather than the topology inherited as a subset of
\mathbb{T}^{3}
; as measures on
\mathbb{T}^{3}
, the measures
\mu^{j}_{x}
are rather pathological whenever they are non-atomic (see
Lemma

3.6
below.)

For
(0,0)\neq(n_{1},n_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and for a.e.
x\in\mathbb{T}^{3}
we similarly construct locally finite, leaf-wise measures
\mu^{s}_{(n_{1},n_{2}),x}
and
\mu^{u}_{(n_{1},n_{2}),x}
on the leaf of the corresponding stable or unstable foliation through
x
.

Recall that an
atom
of a locally finite measure
\mu
on a space
X
is a point
x\in X
with
\mu(\{x\})>0
. A probability measure
\mu
on
X
is
an atom supported at
x
if
\mu(X\smallsetminus\{x\})=0
and
\mu(\{x\})=1
in which case we write
\mu=\delta_{x}
.
We have the following equivalences. (See Lemma
8.4
below for a proof of a more general statement.)

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
(n_{1},n_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\smallsetminus\{(0,0)\}
and let
\mu
be an ergodic,
\alpha(n_{1},n_{2})
-invariant measure on
\mathbb{T}^{3}
. The following are equivalent:

(1)

h_{\mu}(\alpha(n_{1},n_{2}))=0
;

(2)

for
\mu
-a.e.
x
, the measure
\mu^{u}_{(n_{1},n_{2}),x}
has at least one atom;

(3)

for
\mu
-a.e.
x
the measure
\mu^{u}_{(n_{1},n_{2}),x}=\delta_{x}
is a single atom supported at
x
;

(4)

the partition of
(\mathbb{T}^{3},\mu)
into full
W^{u}_{(n_{1},n_{2})}
-leaves is a measurable partition.

For every
x\in\mathbb{T}^{3}
, the subspace
E^{j}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
gives a coordinate system (inducing the immersed topology) on the embedded line
W^{j}(x)
. It is convenient to make these coordinates explicit: for
x\in\mathbb{T}^{3}
, define an identification
\Phi_{x}
between the vector space
E^{j}
and the immersed manifold
W^{j}(x)\subset\mathbb{T}^{3}
by

\Phi_{x}\colon E^{j}\to W^{j}(x),\quad\quad\Phi_{x}(v)=x+v.

Let
\nu^{j}_{x}
denote the locally finite Borel measure on
E^{j}
given by pull-back under
\Phi_{x}
; that is (see also Figure
2
, page
2
), let

\nu^{j}_{x}=(\Phi_{x}^{-1})_{*}(\mu_{x}^{j}).

Remark 3.7
.

The map
\Phi_{x}
describes
W^{j}(x)
as an immersed copy of
E^{j}
with
x
as the origin. Thus, for
x^{\prime}\in W^{j}(x)
with
x\neq x^{\prime}
we have
\Phi_{x}\neq\Phi_{x^{\prime}}
. However, writing
x^{\prime}=x+v
for some
v\in E^{j}
we have

\Phi_{x}(t)=\Phi_{x^{\prime}}(t-v).

What is the difference between
\mu^{j}_{x}
and
\nu^{j}_{x}
?
For each
x\in\mathbb{T}^{3}
, the measure
\mu^{j}_{x}
is a locally finite measure on the immersed curve
W^{j}(x)\subset\mathbb{T}^{3}
. For
x^{\prime}\notin W^{j}(x)
it is difficult to compare the measures
\mu^{j}_{x}
and
\mu^{j}_{x^{\prime}}
.
On the other hand, for each
x\in\mathbb{T}^{3}
, the measure
\nu^{j}_{x}
is a locally finite measure on the vector space
E^{j}\simeq\mathbb{R}
; in particular, it is much more convenient to work with the collection
\{\nu^{j}_{x}\}
as we can easily compare
\nu^{j}_{x}
and
\nu^{j}_{x^{\prime}}
for
x\neq x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T}^{3}
.

For
x^{\prime}\in W^{j}(x)
, recall that
\mu^{j}_{x}\,\propto\,\mu^{j}_{x^{\prime}}
as
W^{j}(x)=W^{j}(x^{\prime})
. However, for
x^{\prime}\in W^{j}(x)
we do not necessarily have that
\nu^{j}_{x}\,\propto\,\nu^{j}_{x^{\prime}}
.
The key step in the proof of Theorem
2.8
is to establish that
\nu^{j}_{x}\,\propto\,\nu^{j}_{x^{\prime}}
for (typical)
x^{\prime}\in W^{j}(x)
.

The following lemma characterizes measures on
\mathbb{T}^{3}
that are invariant under translations by
E^{j}
. Together with Lemma
3.4
, this reduces the proof of Theorem
2.8
to studying the geometry of the family of measures
\{\nu_{x}^{j}\}
.

Lemma 3.8
.

A probability measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{T}^{3}
is invariant under translations by
E^{j}
if and only if for
\mu
-a.e.
x
the measure
\nu^{j}_{x}
is proportional to the Lebesgue (Haar) measure on
E^{j}\simeq\mathbb{R}
.

4.
Interlude: Tools from smooth ergodic theory

To complete the proof of Theorem
2.8
, a number of additional tools from smooth ergodic theory are needed. These tools and facts, as well as many additional facts that will be used in Part
III
, are collected in Part
II
below.
For the proof of Theorem
2.8
, we encourage the reader to first consult

(1)

Section
8.6
, especially Propositions
8.12
and
8.13
(used in Section
6.4
), and

(2)

Section
9.1
, especially Theorems
9.3
and
9.5
(used in Section
5.1
and Section
6.3
.)

To understand these statements, the reader should refer to Section
7.1
, Section
7.4
(especially
7.4.1
), Section
8.1
, Section
8.3
, and Definition
8.5
.

5.
Entropy, translation, and geometry of leaf-wise measures

In this section, we present two key propositions,
Proposition

5.6
and
Proposition

5.8
below, that will be used in the proof of Theorem
2.8
.
To motivate these results, in the setting of Theorem
2.8
, let
\mu
be an ergodic,
\alpha
-invariant probability measure with positive entropy (for some element of the action).
By Lemmas
3.4
and
3.8
, the proof of the theorem reduces to showing that for almost every
x
, the measure
\nu^{j}_{x}
is proportional to the Lebesgue (Haar) measure
m
on
E^{j}\simeq\mathbb{R}
for some
j\in\{1,2,3\}.
This is equivalent to showing that the measure
\mu^{j}_{x}
is the Lebesgue (Haar) measure along the manifold
W^{j}(x)=x+E^{j}
.

We present here two key propositions that will give us such properties of the measures
\mu^{j}_{x}
and
\nu^{j}_{x}
. First, under suitable geometric hypotheses on a measure
\nu
on
\mathbb{R}
, we show in
Proposition

5.6
that
\nu
is of the form
\nu=\rho\,m
where
m
denotes the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}
and
\rho\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is a density function with
0<\rho(x)<\infty
for
m
-a.e.
x
. In Section
6
, we show for some
j\in\{1,2,3\}
that these geometric hypotheses hold for the measure
\nu^{j}_{x}
for
\mu
-a.e.
x
. Second, using an entropy computation due to Ledrappier (see Theorem
9.3
below), we show in
Proposition

5.8
that the density function
\rho
above is constant and, specifically, that
\mu^{j}_{x}
is the Lebesgue measure on
W^{j}(x)
for almost every
x
.

5.1.
Shearing measures on
\mathbb{R}

Consider a locally finite Borel measure
\nu
on
\mathbb{R}
. For
t\in\mathbb{R}
, denote by
T_{t}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
the translation

T_{t}(x)=x+t

and let
(T_{t})_{*}\nu
denote the measure defined by

(T_{t})_{*}\nu(B)=\nu(T_{-t}(B))=\nu(B-t).

Recall that two locally finite Borel measures
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
on
\mathbb{R}
are
proportional
, written
\nu_{1}\,\propto\,\nu_{2}
, if there is a constant
c>0
with

\nu_{1}=c\,\nu_{2}.

Two locally finite measures
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
on
\mathbb{R}
are
equivalent
if there is a measurable function
\rho
with
0<\rho(x)<\infty
for all
x
such that

\nu_{1}=\rho\,\nu_{2}

where
\rho\,\nu_{2}
indicates the measure defined as

(\rho\,\nu_{2})(E)=\int_{E}\rho(x)\ d\nu_{2}(x)

for any Borel
E
.

Given a locally finite Borel measure
\nu
on
\mathbb{R}
, let
G(\nu)\subset\mathbb{R}
denote the subgroup of translations satisfying

G(\nu)=\{t\in\mathbb{R}:(T_{t})_{*}\nu\,\propto\,\nu\}.

Example 5.1
.

Consider the Lebesgue measure
m
on
\mathbb{R}
. Then
G(m)=\mathbb{R}
; in fact for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
we have
(T_{t})_{*}m=m
.

Example 5.2
.

Consider
\nu
to be the measure on
\mathbb{R}
given by the density
e^{x}
; that is

d\nu(x)=e^{x}\,dm(x).

For
t\in\mathbb{R}
we have

d((T_{t})_{*}\nu)(x)=e^{x-t}dm(x-t)=e^{x-t}dm(x)=e^{-t}e^{x}dm(x)

so

(T_{t})_{*}\nu=e^{-t}\nu\,\propto\,\nu.

Again we have
G(\nu)=\mathbb{R}
.

Note that for a generic density function
\rho\colon\mathbb{R}\to(0,\infty)
, we expect
G(\rho\,m)
to be the trivial subgroup
G(\rho\,m)=\{0\}
.

Although not needed in our proof of Theorem
2.8
, one can show the following.

Claim 5.3
.

If
\nu
is a locally finite Borel measure on
\mathbb{R}
with
G(\nu)=\mathbb{R}
then there exist
C>0
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\nu=\rho\,m
where

\rho(x)=Ce^{\alpha t}.

Indeed, we show in the proof of
Proposition

5.6
below that, if
G(\nu)=\mathbb{R}
, then
\nu
is equivalent to
m
. The density function
\rho
is then a measurable function
\rho\colon\mathbb{R}\to(0,\infty)
such that for each
t\in\mathbb{R}
, the function

x\mapsto\frac{\rho(x)}{\rho(x-t)}

is a constant (in
x
) function
c_{t}
. As
c_{s+t}=c_{s}c_{t}
and as
t\mapsto c_{t}
is measurable, the claim follows.

Example 5.4
.

Consider the measure
\nu
on
\mathbb{R}
given by

\nu=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{n}\delta_{n}.

For
t\in\mathbb{R}
we have

((T_{t})_{*}\nu)(B)=\nu(\{x-t:x\in B\})=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{n}\delta_{n+t}(B)=e^{-t}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{n+t}\delta_{n+t}(B).

Thus

(1)

(T_{t})_{*}\nu
is mutually singular with
\nu
if
t\not\in\mathbb{Z}
, and

(2)

if
t\in\mathbb{Z}
then

(T_{t})_{*}\nu=e^{-t}\nu\,\propto\,\nu.

We thus have that
G(\nu)=\mathbb{Z}
is a discrete subgroup of
\mathbb{R}
.

We have the following elementary claim.

Claim 5.5
.

G(\nu)
is a closed subgroup of
\mathbb{R}
.

Recall the
support
of a measure
\nu
, written
\mathrm{supp}(\nu)
, is the smallest closed subset of full measure. Note that

G(\nu)
restricts to a continuous action on
\mathrm{supp}(\nu)
. In particular, as
G(\nu)
is a closed subgroup, if
G(\nu)
has a dense orbit in
\mathrm{supp}(\nu)
then
G(\nu)
acts transitively on
\mathrm{supp}(\nu)
.

We state our first key proposition of this section.

Proposition 5.6
.

Suppose
G(\nu)
acts transitively on the support of
\nu
. Then either

(1)

the support of
\nu
is a countable set and
G(\nu)
is discrete, or

(2)

\nu
is equivalent to the Lebesgue measure
m
and
G=\mathbb{R}
.

The assumption that
G(\nu)
acts transitively on the support of
\nu
in
Proposition

5.6
is a very strong hypothesis; for a typical measure on
\mathbb{R}
, we expect
G(\nu)=\{0\}
.

To prove
Proposition

5.6
, we recall the Lebesgue–Besicovitch differentiation and decomposition theorems:

Proposition 5.7

(c.f.
[
MR1333890
, Theorems 2.12, 2.17]
)
.

Let
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
be two locally finite Borel measures on
\mathbb{R}
. Let

\rho(x):=\lim_{r\to 0}\dfrac{\nu_{1}(B(x,r))}{\nu_{2}(B(x,r))}.

Then

(1)

the limit
\rho(x)
exists
\nu_{2}
-a.e. and defines a
\nu_{2}
-measurable function

\rho\colon\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty);

(2)

the set

S=\{x:\rho(x)=\infty\}

is
\nu_{1}
-measurable and
\nu_{2}
-null;

(3)

\nu_{1}
decomposes as

\nu_{1}=\rho\,\nu_{2}+{\nu_{1}}{\restriction_{{S}}}.

Proof of
Proposition

5.6
.

Since
G(\nu)\subset\mathbb{R}
is a closed subgroup, there are only two options: either

(1)

G(\nu)
is discrete in which case
Proposition

5.6
(1)
follows, or

(2)

G(\nu)=\mathbb{R}
.

We show that if
G(\nu)=\mathbb{R}
then
\nu
is equivalent to the Lebesgue measure
m
.

We first consider the assertion that
\nu\ll m
. Let
\rho
and
S
be as in Proposition
5.7
with
\nu_{1}=\nu
and
\nu_{2}=m
. If
\nu
is not absolutely continuous with respect to
m
then the singular set
S
has positive
\nu
-measure. Fix
x\in S
. Then
\rho(x)=\infty
.

Consider any
y\in\mathbb{R}
. Let
t=y-x
. By the definition of
G(\nu)
we have

(T_{t})_{*}\nu=c\nu

for some
c>0
so

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to 0}\dfrac{\nu(B(y,r))}{m(B(y,r))}

\displaystyle=\lim_{r\to 0}\dfrac{\left(c^{-1}(T_{t})_{*}\nu\right)(B(y,r))}{m(B(y,r))}

\displaystyle=\lim_{r\to 0}c^{-1}\dfrac{\nu(B(x,r))}{m(B(x,r))}

\displaystyle=\infty.

It follows that
\rho(y)=\infty
for every
y\in\mathbb{R}
. This contradicts that
S
is a
m
-null set. It follows that
\nu\ll m
.

The reverse absolute continuity
\nu\gg m
follows in the same manner and is left to the reader. ∎

5.2.
Invariance from entropy considerations

We return to the setting and notation of
Theorem

2.8
.

For each
x\in\mathbb{T}^{3}
, recall that
I_{x}^{i}
denotes the unit ball (i.e. interval) in
W^{i}(x)
centered at
x
. Let
m^{i}_{x}
denote the locally finite Lebesgue measure on the leaf
W^{i}(x)
normalized so that
m^{i}_{x}(I_{x})=1
. Note that for
x^{\prime}\in W^{i}(x)
we have
m^{i}_{x}=m^{i}_{x^{\prime}}
since
m^{i}_{x}
is invariant under translations by
E^{i}
.

Our second key proposition of this section shows that if the leaf-wise measures
\mu^{i}_{x}
are absolutely continuous with respect to
m^{i}_{x}
, then
\mu
is automatically invariant under translations by
E^{i}
.

Proposition 5.8
.

For any
i\in\{1,2,3\}
, fix
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
such that

(1)

\lambda^{i}(\mathbf{n})>0
, and

(2)

\lambda^{j}(\mathbf{n})<0
for both
j\neq i
.

Then, the following are equivalent:

(a)

h_{\mu}(\alpha(\mathbf{n}))=\lambda^{i}
;

(b)

for
\mu
-a.e.
x
, the measure
\mu^{i}_{x}
is absolutely continuous with respect to
m^{i}_{x}
;

(c)

for
\mu
-a.e.
x
, the measure
\mu^{i}_{x}
is equivalent to
m^{i}_{x}
;

(d)

for
\mu
-a.e.
x
, we have equality of measures
\mu^{i}_{x}=m^{i}_{x}
;

(e)

\nu_{x}^{i}
is the Lebesgue (Haar) measure on
E^{i}
for
\mu
-a.e.
x
.

Remark 5.9
.

We only prove the implication
(a)

\implies

(d)
of the proposition. Given
(a)

\implies

(d)
, the only other non-trivial implication is
(b)

\implies

(a)
. This implication follows, for instance, from
[
MR693976
]
(see
Theorem

8.6
(3)
below) and can be shown using calculations similar to those in the following proof.

Our proof essentially follows
[
MR743818
,
MR819556
]
though we make certain simplifications using that the dynamics along
W^{i}
-manifolds is affine.

Proof that
(a)

\implies

(d)
.

We introduce some notation. Fix
f=\alpha(\mathbf{n})
. Then
f
is a linear Anosov diffeomorphism of
\mathbb{T}^{3}
such that for every
x\in\mathbb{T}^{3}
, the unstable manifold through
x
is
W^{i}(x)
.

We may assume
\mu
is ergodic for
f
. Indeed, from the Margulis–Ruelle inequality (see Theorem
8.6
(1)
below) we have that

h_{\mu^{\prime}}(f)\leq\lambda^{i}(\mathbf{n})

for any
f
-invariant probability measure
\mu^{\prime}
. As entropy is convex (see (
8.1
), page
8.1
), it follows that
h_{\mu^{\prime}}(f)=\lambda^{i}(\mathbf{n})
for almost every ergodic component
\mu^{\prime}
of
\mu
(see
Definition

6.5
and Appendix
B.4
).

Given a measurable partition
\xi
of
\mathbb{T}^{n}
, write
f^{-1}\xi
for the partition

f^{-1}\xi:=\{f^{-1}(C)\mid C\in\xi\}.

Then the atom of the partition
f^{-1}\xi
containing
x
is

f^{-1}\xi(x)=f^{-1}(\xi(f(x))).

Recall that the
W^{i}
-manifolds are the unstable manifolds for
f
. Let

J^{u}(x)=\left|\frac{\partial f}{\partial E^{i}}(x)\right|

be the unstable Jacobian of
f
: for any precompact,
m^{i}_{x}
-measurable subset
C\subset W^{i}(x)
we have

m^{i}_{f(x)}(f(C))=\int_{C}J^{u}(x)\ dm^{i}_{x}.

As the dynamics of
f
is affine along
W^{i}
-leaves, we have that
J^{u}(x)
is constant in
x
. Explicitly, we have

J^{u}(x)=\chi^{i}(\mathbf{n})=e^{\lambda^{i}(\mathbf{n})}.

For the remainder, fix
\xi
to be a measurable partition of
(\mathbb{T}^{3},\mu)
