such that

(1)

\xi
is subordinate to the partition into
W^{i}
-manifolds (see
Section

3.3
and Definition
8.2
below), and

(2)

\xi
is
increasing
under
f
: for a.e.
x
we have
f^{-1}\xi(x)\subset\xi(x)
.

Using that there exists
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
such that the
W^{i}
leaves are the unstable manifolds for the Anosov diffeomorphism
\alpha(\mathbf{n})\colon\mathbb{T}^{3}\to\mathbb{T}^{3}
, a partition
\xi
with the above properties can be constructed, for instance, by taking
\xi
to be the unstable plaques of a Markov partition (see
[
Sinai1968
,
MR0442989
,
MR1326374
]
). See also the construction in
[
MR693976
]
which holds for general
C^{1+\beta}
diffeomorphisms.

Let
\{\mu^{\xi}_{x}\}
be a family of conditional measures for this partition.
Also let

m^{\xi}_{x}=\frac{1}{m^{i}_{x}(\xi(x))}{m^{i}_{x}}{\restriction_{{\xi(x)}}}

denote the normalized restriction of the Lebesgue measure
m^{i}_{x}
to the atom
\xi(x)
of this partition.
Note that we have
{m^{i}_{x}(\xi(x))}>0
for
\mu
-a.e.
x
since each atom
\xi(x)
contains a neighborhood of
x
in
W^{i}(x)
; in particular, the measure
m^{\xi}_{x}
is well-defined for
\mu
-a.e.
x
.

We have

(5.1)

\log\left(\int\frac{m^{\xi}_{x}(f^{-1}\xi(x))}{\mu^{\xi}_{x}(f^{-1}\xi(x))}\ d\mu(x)\right)\leq 0.

Indeed,

\displaystyle\log

\displaystyle\left(\int\frac{m^{\xi}_{x}(f^{-1}\xi(x))}{\mu^{\xi}_{x}(f^{-1}\xi(x))}\ d\mu(x)\right)

\displaystyle=\log\left(\int\int_{\xi(x)}\frac{m^{\xi}_{x}(f^{-1}\xi(y))}{\mu^{\xi}_{x}(f^{-1}\xi(y))}\ d\mu^{\xi}_{x}(y)d\mu(x)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\log 1=0

where the inequality follows as

\int_{\xi(x)}\frac{m^{\xi}_{x}(f^{-1}\xi(y))}{\mu^{\xi}_{x}(f^{-1}\xi(y))}\ d\mu^{\xi}_{x}(y)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\in{f^{-1}\xi}\\
\mu^{\xi}_{x}(C)>0\end{subarray}}m^{\xi}_{x}(C)\leq m^{\xi}_{x}(\xi(x))=1.

We claim that

(5.2)

\displaystyle\int\log

\displaystyle\left(\frac{m^{\xi}_{x}(f^{-1}\xi(x))}{\mu^{\xi}_{x}(f^{-1}\xi(x))}\right)\ d\mu(x)=0.

Indeed, write

\displaystyle\int\log

\displaystyle\left(\frac{m^{\xi}_{x}(f^{-1}\xi(x))}{\mu^{\xi}_{x}(f^{-1}\xi(x))}\right)\ d\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\int\log\left({m^{\xi}_{x}(f^{-1}\xi(x))}\right)\ d\mu(x)-\int\log\left({\mu^{\xi}_{x}(f^{-1}\xi(x))}\right)\ d\mu(x).

From the properties of
\xi
, we have (see
8.3
below)

-\int\log(\mu^{\xi}_{x}(f^{-1}\xi(x)))\ d\mu(x)=h_{\mu}(f^{-1}\xi\mid\xi)=h_{\mu}(f).

On the other hand, we claim that

(5.3)

\int\log\left({m^{\xi}_{x}(f^{-1}\xi(x))}\right)\ d\mu(x)=-\lambda(\mathbf{n}).

To establish (
5.3
), let

q(x):=m^{i}_{x}(\xi(x)).

As
f^{-1}\xi(x)\subset\xi(x)\subset f\xi(x)
we have

\frac{q(f(x))}{q(x)}=\frac{m^{i}_{f(x)}(\xi(f(x)))}{m^{i}_{x}(\xi(x))}\leq\frac{m^{i}_{f(x)}(f(\xi(x)))}{m^{i}_{x}(\xi(x))}=\frac{\int_{\xi(x)}J^{u}(x)\ dm^{i}_{x}}{m^{i}_{x}(\xi(x))}=\chi^{i}(\mathbf{n})

and

\frac{q(f(x))}{q(x)}=\frac{m^{i}_{f(x)}(\xi(f(x)))}{m^{i}_{x}(\xi(x))}\geq\frac{m^{i}_{f(x)}(\xi(f(x)))}{m^{i}_{x}(f^{-1}(\xi(f(x))))}=\frac{1}{\chi^{i}(\mathbf{n})}.

It follows that the function

\log\frac{q\circ f}{q}

is
L^{\infty}(\mu)
(in particular
L^{1}(\mu)
); from
[
MR693976
, Proposition 2.2]
we have that

\int\log\frac{q\circ f}{q}\ d\mu=0.

We then have that

\displaystyle\int\log\left({m^{\xi}_{x}(f^{-1}\xi(x))}\right)\,d\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\int\log\left(\frac{m^{i}_{x}(f^{-1}\xi(x))}{m^{i}_{x}(\xi(x))}\right)\,d\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\int\log\left(\frac{\frac{1}{\chi^{i}(\mathbf{n})}\,m^{i}_{f(x)}(\xi(f(x)))}{m^{i}_{x}(\xi(x))}\right)\,d\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\int-\log\chi^{i}(\mathbf{n})+\log\frac{q\circ f}{q}\,d\mu

\displaystyle=-\lambda^{i}(\mathbf{n})

and (
5.3
) follows.

As we assumed
h_{\mu}(f)=\lambda(\mathbf{n})
, equation (
5.2
) follows.
From the strict concavity of
\log
we have

\int\log\left(\frac{m^{\xi}_{x}(f^{-1}\xi(x))}{\mu^{\xi}_{x}(f^{-1}\xi(x))}\right)\ d\mu(x)\leq\log\left(\int\frac{m^{\xi}_{x}(f^{-1}\xi(x))}{\mu^{\xi}_{x}(f^{-1}\xi(x))}\ d\mu(x)\right)

with equality if and only if the function

x\mapsto\frac{m^{\xi}_{x}(f^{-1}\xi(x))}{\mu^{\xi}_{x}(f^{-1}\xi(x))}

is constant off a
\mu
-null set. From (
5.1
) and (
5.2
), it thus follows that

{\mu^{\xi}_{x}(f^{-1}\xi(x))}={m^{\xi}_{x}(f^{-1}\xi(x))}

for
\mu
-almost every
x
. In particular, if
C\subset\xi(x)
is a union of elements of
f^{-1}\xi
, then
\mu^{\xi}_{x}(C)=m^{\xi}_{x}(C).

We may repeat the above calculations with
f
replaced by
f^{n}
for
n\geq 1
and obtain that

{\mu^{\xi}_{x}(f^{-n}\xi(x))}={m^{\xi}_{x}(f^{-n}\xi(x))}

for
\mu
-a.e.
x
. As the partitions
\{f^{-n}\xi(x^{\prime})\mid x^{\prime}\in\xi(x)\}
generate the point partition on each
\xi(x)
, it follows for
\mu
-a.e.
x
that

\mu^{\xi}_{x}=m^{\xi}_{x}.

Replacing
\xi
with
f^{n}(\xi)
for each
n\geq 1
, we obtain
\mu^{f^{n}(\xi)}_{x}=m^{f^{n}(\xi)}_{x}

and the equality
\mu^{i}_{x}=m^{i}_{x}
for
\mu
-a.e.
x
follows. ∎

Remark 5.10
.

When
f\colon M\to M
is an Anosov diffeomorphism or, more generally, a non-uniformly hyperbolic
C^{1+\beta}
diffeomorphism we still have equivalence of
(a)
,
(b)
, and
(c)
in
Proposition

5.8
when the right-hand side of
(a)
is replaced by the sum of all positive Lyapunov exponents counted with multiplicity and the measures are conditional measures along unstable manifolds. See
Theorem

9.3
below and Appendix
D
, especially Section
D.2.4
, for details. The proof is nearly identical to the above except for the analogue of computation (
5.3
). Multiplying the measures
m^{\xi}_{x}
with an appropriate dynamically defined density (see (
D.6
) in Appendix
D
), a computation analogous to (
5.3
) still holds. See
[
MR819556
, Lemma 6.1.2]
.

The extra conclusion
(d)
in
Proposition

5.8
follows in our setting from the fact that the
W^{i}
-manifolds are orbits of a group action (namely translation by
E^{i}
on
\mathbb{T}^{3}
) and that
f
acts homogeneously between orbits. The density function guaranteed by
(c)
is then constant and equality in
(d)
holds by choice of normalization. See
Theorem

9.5
below for a more general framework in which the extra invariance in
(d)
follows.

Remark 5.11
.

In our proof of Theorem
2.8
below, we will apply
Proposition

5.6
above to conclude for some
j\in\{1,2,3\}
that the leaf-wise measures
\mu^{j}_{x}
along leaves of the
W^{j}
-foliation are absolutely continuous by showing the group
G(\nu_{x}^{j})
is not discrete and hence
G(\nu_{x}^{j})=\mathbb{R}
for a.e.
x.

We will then apply
Proposition

5.8
to conclude that the measures
\mu^{j}_{x}
are Lebesgue along the
W^{j}
-foliation and conclude that
\mu
is invariant under translation by
E^{j}
. This approach heavily uses that the foliations
W^{j}
are 1-dimensional.

Alternatively, one may follow
[
MR1406432
]
(and many related arguments including those in
[
MR2261075
,
MR2811602
,
1506.06826
]
) any apply dynamical arguments to show that the density function in
5.3
is constant. For instance, using that the dynamics expands unstable manifolds, one may show the curvature
\alpha^{2}
of the density function
\rho
in
5.3
decreases and obtain a contradiction by Poincaré recurrence unless
\alpha=0
and
\rho
is constant.
This approach can be adapted when leaves of
W^{j}
-foliations are higher dimensional. In this case, the group
G(\nu_{x}^{j})
is a closed subgroup of isometries (of some
\mathbb{R}^{n}
) that preserve the
\nu_{x}^{j}
up to proportionality. By classifying orbits of subgroups of the isometry group of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and using the dynamics along
W^{j}
-leaves, one then argues that
G(\nu_{x}^{j})
consists of translations that preserve
\nu_{x}^{j}
.

6.
Proof of Theorem
2.8

6.1.
Inducing from a
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-action to a
\mathbb{R}^{2}
-action

To prove Theorem
2.8
,
it is convenient to induce from the
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-action on the 3-manifold
\mathbb{T}^{3}
to a
\mathbb{R}^{2}
-action on a certain
5
-dimensional manifold which we denote by
N
.

We first outline a general construction of
N
. (See
14.1
below for details of a related construction.)
Let
\tilde{N}=\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{T}^{3}
and let
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
act on
\tilde{N}
on the right by

(\mathbf{s},x)\cdot\mathbf{n}=(\mathbf{s}+\mathbf{n},\alpha(-\mathbf{n})(x)).

Let
\mathbb{R}^{2}
act on
\tilde{N}
on the left by

\mathbf{t}\cdot(\mathbf{s},x)=(\mathbf{t}+\mathbf{s},x).

Let
N=\tilde{N}/\mathbb{Z}^{2}
be the quotient manifold under the right
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-action. As the left
\mathbb{R}^{2}
-action and right
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-action commute, we obtain a
\mathbb{R}^{2}
-action on
N
. The manifold
N
has a structure of a fiber-bundle with fibers diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{T}^{3}
. The
\mathbb{R}^{2}
-action on
N
permutes the 3-dimensional fibers and fibers over the natural
\mathbb{R}^{2}
-action on
\mathbb{T}^{2}=\mathbb{R}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}^{2}
by translations.
For each
j\in\{1,2,3\}
, there is a foliation
W^{j}
of
N
by injectively immersed curves; each curve
W^{j}(x)
is contained in the
\mathbb{T}^{3}
-fiber through
x
.
Moreover, there is a Riemannian metric on
N
such that, if
d^{j}_{x}(\cdot,\cdot)
denotes the induced distance in
W^{j}(x)
, then for any
y,z\in W^{j}(x)
and
\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}

d^{j}_{\mathbf{s}\cdot x}(\mathbf{s}\cdot y,\mathbf{s}\cdot z)=e^{\lambda^{j}(\mathbf{s})}d^{j}_{x}(y,z).

Given any Riemannian metric on
N
and any
\mathbb{R}^{2}
-invariant probability measure
\tilde{\mu}
on
N
we may define fiberwise Lyapunov exponents for the action of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
restricted to the fibers of
N
(see
7.3
and
14.2
). For any
\mathbb{R}^{2}
-invariant measure
\tilde{\mu}
, these exponents coincide with the exponents
\lambda^{1},\lambda^{2}
, and
\lambda^{3}
above.

The above construction of
N
works in full generality. However, in the context of
Theorem

2.8
, using that
A
and
B
are diagonalizable over
\mathbb{R}
and have positive eigenvalues it is possible to give a more algebraic construction of the suspension manifold
N
.
The algebraic construction has the advantage that the dynamical properties outlined above follow immediately. We state the properties of the suspension space and induced
\mathbb{R}^{2}
-action and outline the construction in
Section

6.4
.

Given
\mathbf{s}=(s_{1},s_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
define subspaces of
\mathbb{R}^{3}

E^{s}_{(s_{1},s_{2})}=\bigoplus_{\lambda^{j}(s_{1},s_{2})<0}E^{j}
and
E^{u}_{(s_{1},s_{2})}=\bigoplus_{\lambda^{j}(s_{1},s_{2})>0}E^{j}
.

Note that if
(s_{1},s_{2})\in\ker\lambda^{j}\smallsetminus\{0\}
for some
j\in\{1,2,3\}
then both
E^{s}_{(s_{1},s_{2})}
and
E^{u}_{(s_{1},s_{2})}
are 1-dimensional.

Proposition 6.1

(See
Section

6.4
)
.

There is a
5
-dimensional manifold
N
and an
\mathbb{R}^{2}
-action
\tilde{\alpha}\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathrm{Diff}(N)
with the following properties:

(1)

N
is a fiber-bundle over
\mathbb{T}^{2}
with fibers diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{T}^{3}
; moreover the action
\tilde{\alpha}
permutes the fibers.

(2)

For every
1\leq j\leq 3
the vector space
E^{j}
acts by addition on
N
. For every
x\in N
, the orbit
W^{j}(x)=\{x+v:v\in E^{i}\}
is contained in the
\mathbb{T}^{3}
fiber containing
x
and the leaves
W^{j}(x)
form a smooth foliation of
N
.
The
W^{j}
-leaves are permuted by the action
\tilde{\alpha}
.

(3)

For every
0\neq(s_{1},s_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
the vector spaces
E^{s}_{(s_{1},s_{2})}
and
E^{u}_{(s_{1},s_{2})}
similarly act by addition on
N
. The orbits
W^{s}(x)
and
W^{u}(x)
are contained in the fiber through
x
and correspond to the stable and unstable manifolds, respectively, for the partially hyperbolic diffeomorphism
\tilde{\alpha}(s_{1},s_{2})\colon N\to N
.

(4)

For all
(t_{1},t_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
x\in N
, the map

\tilde{\alpha}(t_{1},t_{2})\colon W^{j}(x)\to W^{j}(\tilde{\alpha}(t_{1},t_{2})(x))

dilates distances in
W^{j}
by exactly
e^{\lambda^{j}(t_{1},t_{2})}
. That is, for
y\in W^{j}(x)
writing
y=x+v
for some
v\in E^{j}
, we have

\tilde{\alpha}(t_{1},t_{2})(y)\in W^{j}(\tilde{\alpha}(t_{1},t_{2})(x))

and

\tilde{\alpha}(t_{1},t_{2})(y)=\tilde{\alpha}(t_{1},t_{2})(x)+e^{\lambda^{j}(t_{1},t_{2})}v.

Given
\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
let
W^{u}_{\mathbf{s}}(x)=E^{u}_{\mathbf{s}}(x)
be the unstable manifold through
x\in N
for the 1-parameter flow
\alpha(t\mathbf{s})
.
Similarly let
W^{s}_{\mathbf{s}}(x)=x+E^{s}_{\mathbf{s}}(x)
be the stable manifold through
x\in N
for the 1-parameter flow
\alpha(t\mathbf{s})
. The stable and unstable manifolds
W^{s}_{\mathbf{s}}(x)
and
W^{u}_{\mathbf{s}}(x)
are contained in the
\mathbb{T}^{3}
-fiber of
N
through
x
. Note that for
\mathbf{s}\neq(0,0)
, the leaves
W^{u}_{\mathbf{s}}(x)
and
W^{s}_{\mathbf{s}}(x)
are at least one-dimensional. Moreover,
if
\mathbf{s}
is not in a kernel of any
\lambda^{j}
(see Figure
1
) then
W^{u}_{\mathbf{s}}(x)
and
W^{s}_{\mathbf{s}}(x)
are of complementary dimension in the
\mathbb{T}^{3}
-fiber through
x
. If
\mathbf{s}\neq(0,0)
is contained in the kernel of
\lambda^{j}
then
W^{u}_{\mathbf{s}}(x)
and
W^{s}_{\mathbf{s}}(x)
are both 1-dimensional.

To begin the proof of
Theorem

2.8
, fix an ergodic,
\alpha
-invariant probability measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{T}^{3}
with positive entropy
h_{\mu}(\alpha(n_{1},n_{2}))>0
for some
(n_{1},n_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. Note that
\alpha(n_{1},n_{2})
has either 2-dimensional unstable or 1-dimensional unstable manifolds. Replacing
(n_{1},n_{2})
with
(-n_{1},-n_{2})
if necessary and recalling that

h_{\mu}(\alpha(n_{1},n_{2}))=h_{\mu}(\alpha(n_{1},n_{2})^{-1})=h_{\mu}(\alpha(-n_{1},-n_{2}))

we can assume that
\alpha(n_{1},n_{2})
has 1-dimensional unstable manifolds. Fix
1\leq i\leq 3
for the remainder of the proof such that
E^{i}=E^{u}_{(n_{1},n_{2})}
. We will show that
\mu
is invariant under translations by
E^{i}
.

We write
\tilde{\mu}
for the ergodic,
\tilde{\alpha}
-invariant measure on
N
corresponding to
\mu
.
To construct the measure
\tilde{\mu}
, first let
m^{2}
denote the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Then
m^{2}\times\mu
is a locally finite Borel measure on
\tilde{N}=\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{T}^{3}
that is invariant under the actions of both
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. Let
\tilde{\mu}
denote the image of
m^{2}\times\mu
restricted to the fundamental domain
[0,1]^{2}\times\mathbb{T}^{3}.

From Lemma
3.6
the leaf-wise measures
\mu_{x}^{i}
of
\mu
along
W^{i}
-leaves in
\mathbb{T}^{3}
are nonatomic. This holds if and only if the leaf-wise measures
\tilde{\mu}_{x}^{i}
of
\tilde{\mu}
along
W^{i}
-manifolds in
N
are nonatomic.
Moreover, ergodicity of
\mu
for the
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-action on
\mathbb{T}^{3}
implies that
\tilde{\mu}
is ergodic for the
\mathbb{R}^{2}
-action on
N
.

For
x\in N
we again parameterize
W^{i}(x)
by
E^{i}
via the map

\Phi_{x}\colon E^{i}\to W^{i}(x),\quad\Phi_{x}(v)=x+v

and let
\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}
given by

\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}=(\Phi_{x}^{-1})_{*}\tilde{\mu}_{x}^{i}

be the corresponding locally finite measure on
E^{i}\simeq\mathbb{R}
. Recall we fix a normalization of each
\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}
so that each
\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}
gives mass 1 to the unit ball (i.e. interval) in
E^{i}
. From the choice of
E^{i}
, the measures
\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}
are nonatomic for a.e.
x\in N
.

To prove
Theorem

2.8
, we will show that
G(\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x})=\mathbb{R}
for
\tilde{\mu}
-almost every
x\in N
. This will imply that
G(\nu^{i}_{x})=\mathbb{R}
and thus
\nu^{i}_{x}
is absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue for
\mu
-almost every
x\in\mathbb{T}^{3}
by
Proposition

5.6
. Applying
Proposition

5.8
then implies that
\mu
is invariant under translations by
E^{i}
, showing that
\mu
is the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{T}^{3}
.

6.2.
Restriction to the kernel of
\lambda^{i}

We now heavily use that our acting group
\mathbb{R}^{2}
is higher-rank. Recall that
\lambda^{1},\lambda^{2},\lambda^{3}
are linear functionals on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
; moreover, none of the functionals
\lambda^{1},\lambda^{2},\lambda^{3}
is the zero function and no pair is proportional. It follows that each functional
\lambda^{1},\lambda^{2},\lambda^{3}
has a 1-dimensional kernel and that all kernels are distinct. See Figure
1
.

Figure 1
.
Kernels of the Lyapunov exponent functionals in the acting group
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Signs indicate the signs of the Lyapunov exponents in corresponding half-cones. For instance,
++-
indicates the open half-cone of
\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
\lambda^{1}(\mathbf{s})>0,\lambda^{2}(\mathbf{s})>0
, and
\lambda^{3}(\mathbf{s})<0
. (Recall that the sum of the
\lambda^{i}
is zero.)

As the exponents
\lambda^{i}
are not proportional to functionals defined over
\mathbb{Q}
, none of the kernels is defined over
\mathbb{Q}
. In particular, there is no
0\neq\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
with
\mathbf{n}\in\ker\lambda^{j}
for any
j\in\{1,2,3\}
. This is the primary reason why we induce to an
\mathbb{R}^{2}
-action on
N
rather than studying the
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-action on
\mathbb{T}^{3}
.

Recall our fixed
i\in\{1,2,3\}
above such that the leaf-wise measure
\tilde{\mu}^{i}_{x}
is non-atomic for almost every
x\in N
. As the kernel
\ker\lambda^{i}
is 1-dimensional, we may fix
\mathbf{s}_{0}=(s_{1},s_{2})\in\ker\lambda^{i}\smallsetminus\{0\}
. For the remainder of the proof we will (almost) exclusively study the 1-parameter flow
\phi_{t}
inside
\ker\lambda^{i}
:

\phi_{t}\colon N\to N,\quad\quad\phi_{t}(x)=\tilde{\alpha}(t\mathbf{s}_{0})(x)=\tilde{\alpha}(ts_{1},ts_{2})(x).

From the fact that
\tilde{\mu}
is
\tilde{\alpha}
-invariant, the choice of
\mathbf{s}_{0}\in\ker\lambda^{i}
, and the choice of normalization of the family of leaf-wise measures
\{\tilde{\mu}^{i}_{x}\}
, we immediately obtain the following.

Lemma 6.2
.

For every
t\in\mathbb{R}
and almost every
x\in N

(1)

\phi_{t}\colon W^{i}(x)\to W^{i}(\phi_{t}(x))
is an isometry;

(2)

(\phi_{t})_{*}\tilde{\mu}^{i}_{x}=\tilde{\mu}^{i}_{\phi_{t}(x)}
;

(3)

\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}=\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{\phi_{t}(x)}
.

Proof.

Conclusion
(1)
follows from Proposition
6.1
(4)
and the choice of
\mathbf{s}_{0}\in\ker\lambda^{i}
so that
\tilde{\alpha}(\mathbf{s}_{0})
dilates distances in
W^{i}
by
e^{\lambda^{i}(\mathbf{s}_{0})}=1.

For Conclusion
(2)
, from the invariance of
\tilde{\mu}
we have

(\phi_{t})_{*}\tilde{\mu}^{i}_{x}=(\alpha(\mathbf{s}_{0}))_{*}\tilde{\mu}^{i}_{x}\,\propto\,\tilde{\mu}^{i}_{\phi_{t}(x)}.

On the other hand, since
\phi_{t}
is an isometry along
W^{i}
-leaves, we have

\phi_{t}(I^{i}_{x})=I^{i}_{\phi_{t}(x)}

where
I^{i}_{x}
is the unit ball (i.e. interval) in
W^{i}(x)
.
It follows that

(\phi_{t})_{*}\tilde{\mu}^{i}_{x}(I^{i}_{\phi_{t}(x)})=\tilde{\mu}^{i}_{x}(I^{i}_{x})=1

and thus, from our choice of normalization,

(\phi_{t})_{*}\tilde{\mu}^{i}_{x}=\tilde{\mu}^{i}_{\phi_{t}(x)}.

Conclusion
(3)
then follows from
(1)
,
(2)
, and definition of
\Phi^{i}_{x}
.
∎

Recalling Remark
3.7
, conclusion
(3)
of Lemma
6.2
is quite strong: the family of measures
\{\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}\}
on
E^{i}
is constant along orbits of the flow
\phi_{t}
.

6.3.
Conclusion of the proof assuming ergodicity of
\phi_{t}

Note that while
\tilde{\mu}
is assumed to be
\mathbb{R}^{2}
-ergodic, there is no reason that
\tilde{\mu}
is ergodic for the 1-parameter flow
\phi_{t}
.
We complete the proof of Theorem
2.8
assuming the measure
\tilde{\mu}
is ergodic for the 1-parameter flow
\phi_{t}
. Although this may not hold in general, we will explain how to correct this in the next section.

Recall the notation and conclusion of
Proposition

5.6
. The next lemma verifies that the measures
\tilde{\nu}_{x}^{i}
satisfy the hypotheses of
Proposition

5.6
.

Lemma 6.3
.

Assume the 1-parameter flow
\phi_{t}
acts ergodically on
(N,\tilde{\mu})
. Then for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in N
, the group
G(\tilde{\nu}_{x}^{i})
acts transitively on the support of
\tilde{\nu}_{x}^{i}
.

Proof of Theorem
2.8
assuming that
\phi_{t}
is ergodic.

Assume that Lemma
6.3
holds, from
Proposition

5.6
we conclude that either
\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}
is supported on a countable set (and thus the measure
\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}
has atoms) or the measure
\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}
is absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure on
E^{i}\simeq\mathbb{R}
. From our entropy assumptions (recall Lemma
3.6
), the measures
\tilde{\mu}^{i}_{x}
and thus
\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}
have no atoms for almost every
x\in N
and thus from
Proposition

5.6
we conclude that

\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}
is absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure on
E^{i}
.

It follows that the leaf-wise measures
\tilde{\mu}^{i}_{x}
are absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure on
W^{i}(x)
for almost ever
x\in N
. From the construction of
\tilde{\mu}
, it follows that the leaf-wise measures
\mu^{i}_{x}
are absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure on
W^{i}(x)
for almost every
x\in\mathbb{T}^{3}
. (Alternatively, an analogue of
(b)

\implies

(a)
in
Proposition

5.8
implies that
h_{\tilde{\mu}}(\tilde{\alpha}(n_{1},n_{2}))=\lambda^{i}(n_{1},n_{2})
which, from the structure of
\tilde{\mu}
, implies
h_{\mu}(\alpha(n_{1},n_{2}))=\lambda^{i}(n_{1},n_{2})
and thus
\mu^{i}_{x}
is absolutely continuous for
\mu
-almost every
x
.)
From
Proposition

5.8
(d)
, it follows for a.e.
x\in\mathbb{T}^{3}
that
\mu^{i}_{x}
coincides with the Lebesgue measure on
W^{i}(x)
normalized on
I^{i}_{x}
. From
Lemma

3.8
, it follows that
\mu
is invariant under translations by
E^{i}
and is hence the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{T}^{3}
by
Lemma

3.4
. ∎

We give the proof of Lemma
6.3
(still assuming that
\phi_{t}
is ergodic.)

Proof of Lemma
6.3
assuming ergodicity of
\phi_{t}
.

Recall from Lemma
6.2
(3)
that the parameterized collection
of measures
x\mapsto\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}
forms a
\phi_{t}
-invariant, measurable function.
1
1

1

There is a minor technical point we ignored here. Namely, we are using that the space of locally finite Borel measure on
\mathbb{R}
is a reasonable topological space (with the topology dual to compactly supported continuous functions) and that
x\mapsto\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}
is a measurable function from
(N,\tilde{\mu})
to the space of locally finite Borel measure on
\mathbb{R}
.
As we assume ergodicity of the flow
\phi_{t}
, it follows that the assignment
x\mapsto\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}
is constant
\tilde{\mu}
-a.s.
In particular, for
\mu
-almost every
x\in N
and
\tilde{\mu}^{i}_{x}
-almost every
x^{\prime}\in W^{i}(x)
we have

(6.1)

\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x^{\prime}}=\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}.

Take such
x
and
x^{\prime}
. Recall the parametrization
\Phi_{x}\colon E^{i}\to W^{i}(x)
. Let
v\in E^{i}
be such that
x^{\prime}=x+v
.
We observe (see Figures
2(a)
and
2(b)
) that

\Phi_{x}^{-1}\circ\Phi_{x^{\prime}}\colon E^{i}\to E^{i}

is the map

\Phi_{x}^{-1}\circ\Phi_{x^{\prime}}\colon t\mapsto t+v.

Recall that
\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x^{\prime}},\tilde{\mu}^{i}_{x^{\prime}},\tilde{\mu}^{i}_{x}
, and
\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}
are canonically defined by our choice of normalization. Since
x
and
x^{\prime}
are in the same unstable manifold, we have
\tilde{\mu}^{i}_{x^{\prime}}\,\propto\,\tilde{\mu}^{i}_{x}
so

(\Phi_{x^{\prime}})_{*}\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x^{\prime}}=\tilde{\mu}^{i}_{x^{\prime}}\,\propto\,\mu^{i}_{x}=(\Phi_{x})_{*}\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}.

and

(\Phi_{x}^{-1}\circ\Phi_{x^{\prime}})_{*}\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x^{\prime}}\,\propto\,\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}.

It follows that

(T_{v})_{*}\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}=(T_{v})_{*}\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x^{\prime}}\,\propto\,\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}.

Thus
v\in G(\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x})
. Since
x^{\prime}
was a
\tilde{\mu}^{i}_{x}
-typical point of
W^{i}(x)
it follows that
G(\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x})
has a dense orbit in the support of
\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}
and thus acts transitively on the support of
\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}
.
∎

(a)
Parametrizations
\Phi_{x}
and
\Phi_{x^{\prime}}

(b)

\Phi_{x}^{-1}\circ\Phi_{x^{\prime}}\colon t\mapsto t+v

Figure 2.
Proof of Lemma
6.3

Remark 6.4
.

Above, we showed that
\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x^{\prime}}=\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}
for
x^{\prime}=x+v
with
v\in E^{i}
but only obtained
(T_{v})_{*}\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}\,\propto\,\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}
rather than
(T_{v})_{*}\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}=\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}
. The coefficient of proportionality is due to the choice of normalization on
\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}
which is chosen so that
\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}\left(B^{E^{i}}_{1}(0)\right)=1
where
B^{E^{i}}_{1}(0)\subset E^{i}
is the unit ball (interval) in
E^{i}
centered at
0
. We have

(T_{v})_{*}\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}\left(B^{E^{i}}_{1}(0)\right)=\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}\left(B^{E^{i}}_{1}(v)\right)

but
do not (yet) know that
\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}\left(B^{E^{i}}_{1}(v)\right)=1.
However, we do know that

\displaystyle\left((T_{v})_{*}\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}\right)\left(B^{E^{i}}_{1}(v)\right)

\displaystyle=\left((\Phi_{x}^{-1}\circ\Phi_{x^{\prime}})_{*}\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x^{\prime}}\right)\left(B^{E^{i}}_{1}(v)\right)

\displaystyle=\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x^{\prime}}\left(\Phi_{x^{\prime}}^{-1}(\Phi_{x}(B^{E^{i}}_{1}(v)))\right)

\displaystyle=\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x^{\prime}}\left(B^{E^{i}}_{1}(0)\right)=1.

In particular, we have that
(T_{v})_{*}\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}=(T_{v})_{*}\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x^{\prime}}=(\Phi_{x}^{-1}\circ\Phi_{x^{\prime}})_{*}\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x^{\prime}}\,\propto\,\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}

with explicit coefficient of proportionality:

\displaystyle(T_{v})_{*}\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}=\dfrac{1}{\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}(B^{E^{i}}_{1}(v))}\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}.

6.4.
Overcoming lack of ergodicity: the
\pi
-partition trick

The proof of Lemma
6.3
seems to fail if the measure
\tilde{\mu}
is not
\phi_{t}
-ergodic. Indeed, we used that the assignment
x\mapsto\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}
was
\phi_{t}
-invariant to conclude that the assignment
x\mapsto\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}
was constant in order to conclude that
\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}=\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x^{\prime}}
for
\tilde{\mu}^{i}_{x}
-typical
x^{\prime}\in W^{i}(x)
in (
6.1
).

We recall the following constructions and definitions. See also Theorem
B.11
in Appendix
B.4
and
[
MR1086631
, Section 3.5]
.

Definition 6.5
.

Let
f\colon X\to X
be a Borel map of a metric space
X
preserving a Borel probability measure
\mu
. Then, there exists a measurable partition
\mathcal{E}
of
(X,\mu)
such that—writing
\{\mu^{\mathcal{E}}_{x}\}
for a family of conditional measures of
\mu
relative to
\mathcal{E}
(see
Definition

8.1
)—for
\mu
-a.e.
x
the measure
\mu^{\mathcal{E}}_{x}
is an ergodic,
f
-invariant Borel probability measure. The partition
\mathcal{E}
is called the
ergodic decomposition
or the
partition into ergodic components
of
\mu
with respect to
f
. The measures
\{\mu^{\mathcal{E}}_{x}\}
are called the
ergodic components
of
\mu
.

To illustrate the most extreme defect when ergodicity fails, for a typical
x
, it could be that the conditional measure along
W^{i}(x)
of the
\phi_{t}
-ergodic component
\tilde{\mu}^{\mathcal{E}}_{x}
of
\tilde{\mu}
containing
x
is an atom at the point
x
. Then, the only point
x^{\prime}\in W^{i}(x)
for which one could conclude that
\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}=\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x^{\prime}}
would be
x^{\prime}=x
.

We now correct the proof of Lemma
6.3
. This requires tools and notation discussed in Section
8.6
below which we encourage the reader to read first.

Examining the proof of Lemma
6.3
, we did not fully use that the assignment
x\mapsto\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}
was constant. Rather, we used that the assignment
x\mapsto\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}
was constant along the support of
\tilde{\mu}^{i}_{x}
in
W^{i}(x)
. From this we concluded that
\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x}=\tilde{\nu}^{i}_{x^{\prime}}
for
\tilde{\mu}
-typical
x
and
\tilde{\mu}^{i}_{x}
-typical
x^{\prime}
in
W^{i}(x)
.

Recall that if
f\colon N\to\mathbb{R}
is a
\phi_{t}
-invariant, measurable function then
f
is constant on almost every
\phi_{t}
-ergodic component of
\tilde{\mu}
.
Thus, the proof of Lemma
6.3
above works if we establish that almost every
\phi_{t}
-ergodic component of
\tilde{\mu}
is “saturated” by full
W^{i}
-manifolds. The precise statement appears in the following lemma, known as the “
\pi
-partition trick.” We refer to
8.6
and Appendix
C
for details of the
\pi
-partition and measurable hulls.
From entropy considerations in Lemma
3.6
, we have that the partition of
N
into full
W^{i}
-leaves is not measurable. We let
\Xi^{i}
denote the measurable hull of the partition of
(N,\tilde{\mu})
into full
W^{i}
-leaves (see Section
8.6.1
). Also see
Section

8.3.2
for the definition of the partial order on the space of partitions.

Lemma 6.6

(
\pi
-partition trick)
.

\Xi^{i}
is finer than the partition of
(N,\tilde{\mu})
into
\phi_{t}
-ergodic components.

Note that if the
W^{i}
-leaves were expanded (or contracted) by
\phi_{t}
, then the conclusion of
Lemma

6.6
would follow from Proposition
8.12

below. However, we chose
\phi_{t}
precisely so that it neither expands nor contracts
W^{i}
-leaves.

It follows from Lemma
6.6
that almost every
\phi_{t}
-ergodic component contains full
W^{i}
-leaves and hence the proof of Lemma
6.3
works by replacing
\tilde{\mu}
with a
\phi_{t}
-ergodic component of
\tilde{\mu}
.

We complete the proof of Theorem
2.8
by giving the proof of Lemma
6.6
.
For
\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
x\in N
recall that
W^{s}_{\mathbf{s}}(x)
and
W^{u}_{\mathbf{s}}(x)
denote the stable and unstable manifolds, respectively, for the 1-parameter flow
\alpha(t\mathbf{s})
.
We let
\Xi^{u}_{\mathbf{s}}
denote the measurable hull (see
8.6.1
below) of the partition of
(N,\tilde{\mu})
into full
W^{u}_{\mathbf{s}}
-leaves. Similarly

\Xi^{s}_{\mathbf{s}}
denotes the measurable hull of the partition of
(N,\tilde{\mu})
into full
W^{s}_{\mathbf{s}}
-leaves.

Given
\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, let
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbf{s}}
denote the measurable partition of
(N,\tilde{\mu})
into ergodic components of the 1-parameter flow
\alpha(t\mathbf{s})
. Similarly, let
\pi_{\mathbf{s}}
denote the Pinsker partition (see
8.6.2
below) for the 1-parameter flow
\alpha(t\mathbf{s})
on
(N,\tilde{\mu})
.
As stated in Proposition
8.12
below, for any
\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
we have

\mathcal{E}_{\mathbf{s}}\prec\Xi^{s}_{\mathbf{s}}.

From Proposition
8.13
below, we have for any
\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
that

\Xi^{u}_{\mathbf{s}}=\pi_{\mathbf{s}}=\Xi^{s}_{\mathbf{s}}.

(See
8.3.2
for definition of the partial order on space of partitions.)

With the above notation, the conclusion of Lemma
6.6
states, for our fixed
\mathbf{s}_{0}
, that

\mathcal{E}_{\mathbf{s}_{0}}\prec\Xi^{i}.

Given the abstract ergodic theoretic facts above, the proof of Lemma
6.6
is remarkably straightforward.

Figure 3
.
Choice of
s_{1}

Proof of Lemma
6.6
.

Recall we chose
\mathbf{s}_{0}\neq(0,0)
so that
\lambda^{i}(\mathbf{s}_{0})=0
and
\lambda^{j}(\mathbf{s}_{0})\neq 0
for each
j\neq i
. Pick
\mathbf{s}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
close to
\mathbf{s}_{0}
with the following properties (see Figure
3
):

(1)

\lambda^{j}(\mathbf{s}_{1})\neq 0
for every
1\leq j\leq 3
;

(2)

\lambda^{i}(\mathbf{s}_{1})<0
;

(3)

for each
j\neq i
, the numbers
\lambda^{j}(\mathbf{s}_{1})
and
\lambda^{j}(\mathbf{s}_{0})
have the same sign.

Since
\lambda^{i}(\mathbf{s}_{1})<0
, it follows that
W^{i}(x)\subset W^{s}_{\mathbf{s}_{1}}(x)
for every
x\in N
. This immediately implies

\Xi^{s}_{\mathbf{s}_{1}}\prec\Xi^{i}.

Also, note that
W^{u}_{\mathbf{s}_{1}}(x)=W^{u}_{\mathbf{s}_{0}}(x)
for all
x\in N
whence
\Xi^{u}_{\mathbf{s}_{0}}=\Xi^{u}_{\mathbf{s}_{1}}
.
We then obtain the following string of refinements and equalities:

\mathcal{E}_{\mathbf{s}_{0}}\prec\Xi^{s}_{\mathbf{s}_{0}}=\pi_{\mathbf{s}_{0}}=\Xi^{u}_{\mathbf{s}_{0}}=\Xi^{u}_{\mathbf{s}_{1}}=\pi_{\mathbf{s}_{1}}=\Xi^{s}_{\mathbf{s}_{1}}\prec\Xi^{i}.

In symbols, this is exactly what we needed to prove.
∎

Lemma

6.6
completes the proof of
Theorem

2.8
. We end this section with some technical remarks on the proof of
Lemma

6.6
.

Remark 6.7
.

In the proof of
Lemma

6.6
, to choose
\mathbf{s}_{1}
satisfying condition (
3
), we heavily used the fact that there is no
\lambda^{j}
with
\lambda^{j}=-c\lambda^{i}
for any
c>0
; that is, we are using that the action has no Lyapunov exponents that are negatively proportional to
\lambda^{i}
. Indeed, if
\lambda^{j}=-c\lambda^{i}
then, for any choice of
\mathbf{s}_{1}
such that
\lambda^{i}(\mathbf{s}_{1})<0
, the sign of
\lambda^{j}
changes from zero at
\mathbf{s}_{0}
to positive at
\mathbf{s}_{1}
. On the other hand, if
\lambda^{j}
and
\lambda^{i}
were positively proportional, we can adapt the proof by grouping all exponents positively proportional to
\lambda^{i}
together into a single
coarse Lyapunov exponent
(see
Section

7.4.2
) and then study the leaf-wise measures along higher-dimensional
coarse Lyapunov manifolds
(see
Section

7.4.3
.)

For an explicit example of a higher-rank action where the proof of
Lemma

6.6
(and consequently
Theorem

2.8
) fails, let

A=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}2&1\\
1&1\end{array}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad B=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}2&3\\
3&5\end{array}\right).

We have eigenvalues

\chi^{1}_{A}>1>\chi^{2}_{A},\quad\quad\chi^{1}_{B}>1>\chi^{2}_{B}>0

and both
L_{A}\colon\mathbb{T}^{2}\to\mathbb{T}^{2}
and

L_{B}\colon\mathbb{T}^{2}\to\mathbb{T}^{2}
are Anosov. Consider

L_{A}\times\mathrm{Id}\colon\mathbb{T}^{4}\to\mathbb{T}^{4},\quad\quad\mathrm{Id}\times L_{B}\colon\mathbb{T}^{4}\to\mathbb{T}^{4}.

Clearly
L_{A}\times\mathrm{Id}
and
\mathrm{Id}\times L_{B}
commute and so generate a
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-action by automorphisms of
\mathbb{T}^{4}
. Note that while the generators
L_{A}\times\mathrm{Id}
and
\mathrm{Id}\times L_{B}
are not Anosov, the
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-action contains Anosov diffeomorphisms such as
L_{A}\times L_{B}
.

Let
\mu_{1}
be any ergodic,
L_{A}
-invariant measure on
\mathbb{T}^{2}
and let
\mu_{2}
be any ergodic,
L_{B}
-invariant measure on
\mathbb{T}^{2}
. Note that we can pick
\mu_{1}
different from Lebesgue with
h_{\mu_{1}}(L_{A})>0
.
Then
\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}
is an ergodic,
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-invariant measure on
\mathbb{T}^{4}
that is not Lebesgue and has

h_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}(L_{A}\times\mathrm{Id})>0.

The conclusion of Theorem
2.8
thus fails for this
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-action on
\mathbb{T}^{4}
. The proof degenerates in a number of places.

(1)

The
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-action on
\mathbb{T}^{4}
has four Lyapunov exponent functionals:

(n_{1},n_{2})\mapsto n_{1}\lambda^{1}_{A},\quad n_{1}\lambda^{2}_{A},\quad n_{2}\lambda^{1}_{B},\quad n_{2}\lambda^{2}_{B}.

We note that the functionals

(n_{1},n_{2})\mapsto n_{1}\lambda^{1}_{A},\quad\quad(n_{1},n_{2})\mapsto n_{1}\lambda^{2}_{A}

are negatively proportional. Similarly

(n_{1},n_{2})\mapsto n_{2}\lambda^{1}_{B},\quad\quad(n_{1},n_{2})\mapsto n_{2}\lambda^{2}_{B}

are negatively proportional.
2
2

2

In the literature, negatively proportional exponents such as
(n_{1},n_{2})\mapsto n_{1}\lambda^{1}_{A}
and
(n_{1},n_{2})\mapsto n_{1}\lambda^{2}_{A}
are often referred to as a
symplectic pair
.

The presence of negatively proportional Lyapunov exponents makes it impossible to choose
\mathbf{s}_{1}
in the proof of Lemma
6.6
above with the desired properties. In fact, the conclusion of
6.6
is false for this example.

(2)

Consider the map
h\colon\mathbb{T}^{4}=\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}\to\mathbb{T}^{2}
given by
h(x,y)=x
. Then
h
semiconjugates the
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-action generated by
L_{A}\times\mathrm{Id}
and
\mathrm{Id}\times L_{B}
with the
\mathbb{Z}
-action generated by
L_{A}
. For any
L_{A}
-invariant measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{T}^{2}
, we can find a
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-invariant measure on
\mathbb{T}^{4}
projecting to
\mu
under
h
. In the language of
[
MR1406432
]
,
L_{A}
is a
rank-1 factor
.

To state the general version (
[
MR1406432
, Theorem 5.1]
) of Theorem
2.8
, Katok and Spatzier impose additional hypotheses on the
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-action to rule out the defects discussed above. For the action in Example
2.6
, neither of these defects occurs.

The primary obstruction to the rigidity in this example is the presence of rank-1 factors. For genuinely higher-rank actions with negatively proportional pairs, other tools developed in
[
MR2029471
]
can be used to overcome the failure of Lemma
6.6
.

\bigast
6.5.
Algebraic construction of the suspension space
N
.

We outline the construction in Proposition
6.1
. Recall our fixed commuting matrices
A
and
B
are jointly diagonalizable: there is
Q\in\mathrm{GL}(3,\mathbb{R})
with

Q^{-1}AQ=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}e^{\lambda^{1}_{A}}&0&0\\
0&e^{\lambda^{2}_{A}}&0\\
0&0&e^{\lambda^{3}_{A}}\end{array}\right),\quad\quad Q^{-1}BQ=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}e^{\lambda^{1}_{B}}&0&0\\
0&e^{\lambda^{2}_{B}}&0\\
0&0&e^{\lambda^{3}_{B}}\end{array}\right).

Also recall our Lyapunov exponent functionals
\lambda^{j}\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
, given by

\lambda^{j}(t_{1},t_{2})=t_{1}\lambda^{j}_{A}+t_{2}\lambda^{j}_{B}.

Given
\mathbf{t}=(t_{1},t_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
let
M^{\mathbf{t}}\in\mathrm{GL}(3,\mathbb{R})
be the interpolation matrix

M^{\mathbf{t}}=Q\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}e^{\lambda^{1}(t_{1},t_{2})}&0&0\\
0&e^{\lambda^{2}(t_{1},t_{2})}&0\\
0&0&e^{\lambda^{3}(t_{1},t_{2})}\end{array}\right)Q^{-1}.

Note that for
\mathbf{t}=(n_{1},n_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
,
M^{\mathbf{t}}=A^{n_{1}}B^{n_{2}}\in\mathrm{GL}(3,\mathbb{Z})
. However, for
\mathbf{t}\notin\mathbb{Z}^{3}
, we expect
M^{\mathbf{t}}\notin\mathrm{GL}(3,\mathbb{Z})
; in particular,
M^{\mathbf{t}}
does not define an action on the torus
\mathbb{T}^{3}
.

For
\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
define the “twisted” lattice subgroup
\Lambda_{\mathbf{t}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
by

\Lambda_{\mathbf{t}}=M^{\mathbf{t}}\mathbb{Z}^{3}.

Note that if
\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
then
\Lambda_{\mathbf{m}}
is the standard integer lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{3}
.
For
\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
define a “twisted torus”
T_{\mathbf{t}}
by

T_{\mathbf{t}}=\mathbb{R}^{3}/\Lambda_{\mathbf{t}}.

Note that if
\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
then
T_{\mathbf{m}}
is the standard torus
\mathbb{T}^{3}
; also if
\mathbf{t}^{\prime}=\mathbf{t}+\mathbf{m}
where
\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
then
\Lambda_{\mathbf{t}}=\Lambda_{\mathbf{t}^{\prime}}
whence
T_{\mathbf{t}}=T_{\mathbf{t}^{\prime}}
.

Consider
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Let
\mathbb{Z}^{3}
act on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
as follows: given
(\mathbf{t},x)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
and
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}
define

(\mathbf{t},x)\cdot\mathbf{n}=(\mathbf{t},x+M^{\mathbf{t}}\mathbf{n}).

Let
\tilde{N}
be the quotient of
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
by this action. Note that
\tilde{N}
is a fiber-bundle over
\mathbb{R}^{2}
whose fiber over
\mathbf{t}
is exactly the twisted torus
T_{\mathbf{t}}.

Consider the following
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-action on
\tilde{N}
: given
\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and
(\mathbf{t},x+\Lambda_{\mathbf{t}})\in\tilde{N}

(\mathbf{t},x+\Lambda_{\mathbf{t}})\cdot\mathbf{m}=(\mathbf{t}+\mathbf{m},x+\Lambda_{\mathbf{t}}).

Also consider the following
\mathbb{R}^{2}
-action on
\tilde{N}
: given
\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
(\mathbf{t},x+\Lambda_{\mathbf{t}})\in\tilde{N}

\mathbf{s}\cdot(\mathbf{t},x+\Lambda_{\mathbf{t}})=(\mathbf{s}+\mathbf{t},M^{\mathbf{s}}x+\Lambda_{\mathbf{t}+\mathbf{s}}).

Let
N
be the quotient manifold
\tilde{N}/\mathbb{Z}^{2}
where the quotient is by the
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-action described above. As the
\mathbb{R}^{2}
- and
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-actions on
\tilde{N}
commute, the
\mathbb{R}^{2}
-action on
\tilde{N}
descends to an
\mathbb{R}^{2}
-action on
N
which we denote by
\tilde{\alpha}
.

Note that
N
is a
5
-dimensional manifold which fibers over the torus
\mathbb{T}^{2}=\mathbb{R}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and has
3
-dimensional fibers where the fiber over
\mathbf{t}+\mathbb{Z}^{2}\in\mathbb{T}^{2}
is
T_{\mathbf{t}}.

Also note that
N
inherits a Riemannian metric from
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
Given
x\in N
the tangent space
T_{x}N
decomposes as

T_{x}N=\mathbb{R}^{2}\oplus E^{1}\oplus E^{2}\oplus E^{3}

where
E^{i}
are the joint eigenspaces of
A
and
B
enumerated as before.

Each
E^{i}
acts on
N
as follows: given
p=(\mathbf{t}+\mathbb{Z}^{2},x+\Lambda_{\mathbf{t}})\in N
and
v\in E^{i}

p+v=(\mathbf{t}+\mathbb{Z}^{2},x+\Lambda_{\mathbf{t}})+v=(\mathbf{t}+\mathbb{Z}^{2},x+v+\Lambda_{\mathbf{t}}).

Note that the orbit
W^{i}(p)=\{p+v:v\in E^{i}\}
is contained in the fiber through
p
.
Moreover, for any
p=(\mathbf{t}+\mathbb{Z}^{2},x+\Lambda_{\mathbf{t}})\in N
, any
p^{\prime}=p+v
for
v\in E^{i}
, and any
\mathbf{s}=(s_{1},s_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
we have

\tilde{\alpha}(\mathbf{s})(p^{\prime})=\tilde{\alpha}(\mathbf{s})(p)+e^{s_{1}\lambda^{i}_{A}+s_{2}\lambda^{i}_{B}}v=\tilde{\alpha}(\mathbf{s})(p)+e^{\lambda^{i}(\mathbf{s})}v.

Part II
Primer: smooth ergodic theory for
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-actions

We present background material and a number of tools from the theory of nonuniformly hyperbolic dynamics that will be used in Part
III
and explain a number of facts and constructions that were used in Part
I
. We will be particularly interested in the relation between entropy, conditional measures, and Lyapunov exponents for single diffeomorphisms and for actions of higher-rank abelian groups.

7.
Lyapunov exponents and Pesin manifolds

7.1.
Lyapunov exponents for diffeomorphisms

Let
f\colon M\to M
be a
C^{1}
diffeomorphism of a compact manifold
M
. Let
\mu
be an ergodic,
f
-invariant Borel probability measure. We recall Oseledec’s Theorem
[
MR0240280
]
; see also
[
MR1073779
,
MR0571089
]
.

Theorem 7.1

(Oseledec
[
MR0240280
]
)
.

There are

(1)

a measurable set
\Lambda
with
\mu(\Lambda)=1
;

(2)

numbers
\lambda^{1}>\lambda^{2}>\dots>\lambda^{p}
;

(3)

a
\mu
-measurable,
Df
-invariant splitting
T_{x}M=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{p}E^{i}(x)
defined for
x\in\Lambda

such that for every
x\in\Lambda

(a)

for every
v\in E^{i}(x)\smallsetminus\{0\}

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\pm\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\|D_{x}f^{n}(v)\|=\lambda^{i};

(b)

if
Jf
denotes the Jacobian determinant of
f
then

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\pm\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log|{J}f^{n}|=\sum_{i=1}^{p}m_{i}\lambda^{i}

where
m_{i}=\dim E^{i}(x)
;

(c)

for every
i\neq j

we have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\pm\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\Big(\sin\angle\Big(E^{i}(f^{n}(x)),E^{j}(f^{n}(x))\Big)\Big)=0.

The numbers
\lambda^{i}
are called the
Lyapunov exponents
of
f
with respect to
\mu
and the subspaces
E^{i}(x)
are called the
Oseledec’s subspaces
. Above,
m^{i}
denotes the almost-surely constant value of
\dim E^{i}(x)
, called the
multiplicity
of
\lambda^{i}
.

Given any
f
-invariant measure
\mu
on
M
(which may be nonergodic) the
average top Lyapunov exponent
of
f
with respect to
\mu
is

(7.1)

\lambda_{\mathrm{top}}(f,\mu)=\inf_{n\geq 1}\frac{1}{n}\int\log\|{D_{x}f^{n}}\|\ d\mu(x).

Since
\mu
is
f
-invariant, the sequence
n\mapsto\int\log\|{D_{x}f^{n}}\|\ d\mu(x)
is subadditive and the infimum in (
7.1
) can be replaced by a limit.

By the subadditive ergodic theorem, the functions

x\mapsto\frac{1}{n}\log\|{D_{x}f^{n}}\|

converge a.e. to an invariant, integrable function with integral
\lambda_{\mathrm{top}}(f,\mu)
; see
[
MR0121828
,
MR0254907
]
and
[
MR3289050
, Chapter 3]
.
If
\mu
is ergodic, we have in the notation of
Theorem

7.1
that
\lambda_{\mathrm{top}}(f,\mu)=\lambda^{1}
. If
\mu
is not ergodic, let
\{\mu^{\mathcal{E}}_{x}\}
denote the ergodic decomposition (see
Definition

6.5
) of
\mu
and let
\lambda^{1}_{x}>\lambda_{x}^{2}>\dots>\lambda^{p(x)}_{x}
denote the Lyapunov exponents of
f
with respect to the ergodic invariant measure
\mu^{\mathcal{E}}_{x}
. Then we have

\lambda_{\mathrm{top}}(f,\mu)=\int\lambda^{1}_{x}\ d\mu(x).

7.2.
Lyapunov exponents and (sub)exponential growth of derivatives

Let
M
be a compact manifold and equip
TM
with a background Riemannian metric and associated norm. Let
f\colon M\to M
be a
C^{1}
diffeomorphism. We say
f\colon M\to M
has
uniform subexponential growth of derivatives
if for all
\epsilon>0
there is a
C_{\epsilon}>0
such that

\|Df^{n}\|:=\sup_{x\in M}\|D_{x}f^{n}\|<C_{\epsilon}e^{\epsilon|n|}\quad\quad\text{for all $n\in\mathbb{Z}.$}

Note that we allow that
C_{\epsilon}\to\infty
as
\epsilon\to 0
.

Proposition 7.2
.

A diffeomorphism
f\colon M\to M
has uniform subexponential growth of derivatives if and only if for any
f
-invariant Borel probability measure
\mu
, all Lyapunov exponents of
f
with respect to
\mu
are zero.

That is,
f\colon M\to M
has uniform subexponential growth of derivatives if and only if
\lambda_{\mathrm{top}}(f,\mu)=\lambda_{\mathrm{top}}(f^{-1},\mu)=0
for every
f
-invariant Borel probability measure
\mu
.

Proof.

We show that vanishing of all Lyapunov exponents for all
f
-invariant probability measures implies that
f
has uniform subexponential growth of derivatives; the converse is clear.

Suppose that
f\colon M\to M
fails to have uniform subexponential growth of derivatives. Then there is an
\epsilon>0
and sequences of iterates
n_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}
with
|n_{j}|\to\infty
, base points
x_{j}\in M
, and unit vectors
v_{j}\in T_{x_{j}}M
such that

(7.2)

\|D_{x_{j}}f^{n_{j}}v_{j}\|\geq e^{\epsilon|n_{j}|}.

Replacing
f
with
f^{-1}
, we may assume without loss of generality that
n_{j}\to\infty.

Let
UM\subset TM
denote the unit-sphere bundle. We represent an element of
UM
by a pair
(x,v)
where
v\in T_{x}M
with
\|v\|=1
. Note that
UM
is compact. Note also that
Df\colon TM\to TM
induces a map
Uf\colon U\to U
given by the renormalized derivative:

Uf(x,v):=\left(f(x),\frac{D_{x}f(v)}{\|D_{x}f(v)\|}\right).

Define
\Phi\colon UM\to\mathbb{R}
as follows: given
(x,v)\in UM
, let

\Phi(x,v):=\log\|D_{x}f(v)\|.

By the chain rule, we have

\log\|D_{x}f^{n}(v)\|=\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\Phi(Uf^{j}(x,v)).

For each
j
, let
\nu^{j}
denote the empirical measure along the orbit segment

(x_{j},v_{j}),Uf(x_{j},v_{j}),\dots,Uf^{n_{j}-1}(x_{j},v_{j})

in
UM
given by

\nu^{j}=\frac{1}{n_{j}}\sum_{k=0}^{n_{j}-1}\delta_{Uf^{k}(x_{j},v_{j})}.

From (
7.2
) we have for every
j
that

\int\Phi\ d\nu^{j}\geq\epsilon.

Claim 7.3
.

Let
\nu
be any weak-
*
subsequential limit of
\{\nu^{j}\}
. Then

(a)

\nu
is
Uf
-invariant;

(b)

\displaystyle\int\Phi\ d\nu\geq\epsilon
.

Proof.

Conclusion
(a)
follows as in the proof of the Krylov-Bogolyubov theorem: if
\phi\colon M\to\mathbb{R}
is any (bounded) continuous function then

(7.3)

\lim_{j\to\infty}\left|\int\phi\ d\nu^{j}-\int\phi\circ f\ d\nu^{j}\right|\leq\lim_{j\to\infty}\frac{2\|\phi\|_{C^{0}}}{n_{j}}=0

showing that
\nu
is
f
-invariant.
Conclusion
(b)
follows from continuity of
\Phi
and weak-
*
convergence. ∎

From
7.3
(b)
, we may replace
\nu
with an ergodic component (see
Definition

6.5
)
\nu^{\prime}
of
\nu
such that
\int\Phi\ d\nu^{\prime}\geq\epsilon
.

Take
\mu
to be the push-forward of
\nu^{\prime}
under the natural projection
UM\to M
. Then
\mu
is an
f
-invariant, ergodic measure on
M
.
Let
\{\nu^{\prime}_{x}\}
denote a family of conditional measures of
\nu^{\prime}
for the partition of
UM
into fibers over
M
.
By the pointwise ergodic theorem, for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in M
and
\nu^{\prime}_{x}
-a.e.
v\in UM(x)
we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\|D_{x}f^{n}(v)\|=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\Phi(Uf^{j}(x,v))=\int\Phi\ d\nu^{\prime}\geq\epsilon.

On the other hand,

\displaystyle\lambda_{\mathrm{top}}(f,\mu)

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\int\log\|{D_{x}f^{n}}\|\ d\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\int\sup_{v\in UM(x)}\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\Phi(Uf^{j}(x,v))\ d\mu(x)

\displaystyle\geq\lim_{n\to\infty}\int\int\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\Phi(Uf^{j}(x,v))\ d\nu_{x}(v)\ d\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\int\Phi\ d\nu^{\prime}\geq\epsilon.

Above, the inequality follows from comparing the maximal growth with the average growth (averaged by
\nu_{x}^{\prime}
.)
It follows that the largest Lyapunov exponent of
f
with respect to
\mu
is at least
\epsilon>0
.
∎

7.3.
Lyapunov exponents for nonuniformly hyperbolic
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-actions

How does the theory of Lyapunov exponents change for actions of more general abelian groups? We state a version of Oseledec’s theorem for actions of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
which can easily be extended to actions of
\mathbb{R}^{\ell}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k}
. One should think of the following as a non-stationary version of the joint-diagonalizability or joint-Jordan-normal-form for commuting matrices as exploited in Section
3.1
.

Let
M
be a compact manifold, let
\alpha\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathrm{Diff}^{1}(M)
be a
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-action, and let
\mu
be an ergodic,
\alpha
-invariant measure.

Theorem 7.4

(Higher-rank Oseledec’s theorem (see
[
AWB-GLY-P1
]
))
.

There are

(1)

a measurable set
\Lambda
with
\mu(\Lambda)=1
;

(2)

linear functionals
\lambda^{1},\lambda^{2},\dots,\lambda^{p}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
;

(3)

a
\mu
-measurable,
D\alpha
-invariant splitting
T_{x}M=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{p}E^{i}(x)
defined for
x\in\Lambda

such that for every
x\in\Lambda

(a)

for every
v\in E^{i}(x)\smallsetminus\{0\}

\lim_{|n|\to\infty}\dfrac{\log\|D_{x}\alpha(n)(v)\|-\lambda^{i}(n)}{|n|}=0;

(b)

if
Jf
denotes the Jacobian determinant of
f
then

\displaystyle\lim_{|n|\to\infty}\frac{\log|{J}\alpha(n)|-\sum_{i=1}^{p}m^{i}\lambda^{i}(n)}{|n|};

(c)

for every
i\neq j

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{|n|}\log\Big(\sin\angle\Big(E^{i}(\alpha(n)(x)),E^{j}(\alpha(n)(x))\Big)\Big)=0.

The linear functionals
\lambda^{1},\lambda^{2},\dots,\lambda^{p}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
are called the
Lyapunov exponent functionals
or simply the
Lyapunov exponents
of
\mu
.
In
(b)
,
m^{i}
is the almost-surely constant value of
\dim E^{i}(x)
, called the
multiplicity
of
\lambda^{i}
.
Note that
(a)
implies convergence along rays: for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and
v\in E^{i}(x)\smallsetminus\{0\}

(7.4)

\lim_{k\to\infty}\dfrac{1}{k}\log\|D_{x}\alpha(kn)(v)\|=\lambda^{i}(n).

The convergence in
(a)
is taken along
any
sequence
n\to\infty
; this is stronger than (
7.4
) and is typically needed in applications.

7.4.
Unstable manifolds and coarse Lyapunov manifolds

7.4.1.
Unstable subspaces and unstable manifolds for a single diffeomorphism

Let
f\colon M\to M
be a
C^{1}
diffeomorphism of
M
and let
\mu
be an ergodic,
f
-invariant measure. Let
\lambda^{i}
be the Lyapunov exponents for
f
with respect to
\mu
. For
x\in\Lambda\subset M
, where
\Lambda
is as in
Theorem

7.1
, define

E^{u}(x):=\bigoplus_{\lambda^{i}>0}E^{i}(x)

to be the
unstable subspace
through
x
.
We have that

E^{u}(x):=\{v\in T_{x}M:\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\|D_{x}f^{-n}(v)\|<0\}.

We may similarly define
stable
and
neutral
(or center) subspaces through
x
, respectively, by

E^{s}(x):=\bigoplus_{\lambda^{i}<0}E^{i}(x)

and

E^{c}(x):=\bigoplus_{\lambda^{i}=0}E^{i}(x).

We now assume that
f\colon M\to M
is
C^{1+\beta}
for
\beta>0
.
Through
\mu
-almost every point
x
the set

W^{u}(x):=\left\{y:\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log(d(f^{-n}(x),f^{-n}(y)))<0\right\}

is a connected
C^{1+\beta}
injectively immersed manifold with
T_{x}W^{u}(x)=E^{u}(x)
(see
[
MR0458490
]
) called the
(global) unstable Pesin manifold of
f
through
x
. The collection of all
W^{u}(x)
forms a partition of (a full measure subset of)
M
; in general, this partition does not have the structure of a nice foliation. However, restricted to sets of large measure the partition into local unstable manifolds has the structure of a continuous lamination.
That is, for almost every
x\in M
and any
\epsilon>0
there is a neighborhood
U
of
x
such that, on a set
\Omega
of relative measure
(1-\epsilon)
in
U
, the local leaves of
W^{u}
-manifolds form a partition of
\Omega
by embedded
\dim(E^{u})
-dimensional balls that vary continuously in the
C^{1+\beta}
-topology.

Given the Lyapunov exponents
\lambda^{1}>\lambda^{2}>\dots>\lambda^{p}
of
\mu
, fix
j\in\{1,\cdots,p\}
such that
\lambda^{j}>0
. Then, for almost every
x
, the set

W^{j}(x):=\left\{y:\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log(d(f^{-n}(x),f^{-n}(y)))\leq-\lambda^{j}\right\}

is again a connected,
C^{1+\beta}
injectively immersed manifold with

T_{x}W^{j}(x)=\bigoplus_{\lambda^{i}\geq\lambda^{j}}E^{i}(x)

called the
(global)
j
th unstable manifold
through
x
. We remark that, in general, the intermediate unstable distributions,
E^{i}(x)
for
\lambda^{i}>0
, do not integrate to invariant family of immersed submanifolds.

7.4.2.
Coarse Lyapunov exponents and subspaces

Let
\alpha\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathrm{Diff}^{1}(M)
be an action and let
\mu
be an ergodic,
\alpha
-invariant probability measure. We introduce objects that play the role of unstable subspaces and unstable manifolds for the
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-action
\alpha
.

Given Lyapunov exponents
\lambda^{1},\lambda^{2},\dots,\lambda^{p}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
we say
\lambda^{i}
and
\lambda^{j}
are
positively proportional
if there is a
c>0
with

\lambda^{i}=c\lambda^{j}.

Note that this defines an equivalence relation on the linear functionals

\lambda^{1},\lambda^{2},\dots,\lambda^{p}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}.

The positive proportionality classes are called
coarse Lyapunov exponents
.
For a
\mathbb{Z}
-action generated by a single diffeomorphism
f
, the coarse Lyapunov exponents are simply the collections of positive, zero, and negative Lyapunov exponents.

Let
\chi=\{\lambda^{i}\}
be a coarse Lyapunov exponent. While the size of
\chi(n)
is not well defined, the sign of
\chi(n)
is well defined. Write

E^{\chi}(x)=\oplus_{\lambda^{i}\in\chi}E^{i}(x)

called the corresponding
coarse Lyapunov subspace.

7.4.3.
Coarse Lyapunov manifolds for
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-actions

Analogous to the existence and properties of unstable Pesin manifolds for nonuniformly hyperbolic diffeomorphisms we have the following for actions of higher-rank abelian groups.

Let
\alpha\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathrm{Diff}^{1+\beta}(M)
be an action and let
\mu
be an ergodic,
\alpha
-invariant probability measure. Let
\Lambda
be as in Theorem
7.4
.

Proposition 7.5
.

For almost every
x\in\Lambda
and for every coarse Lyapunov exponent
\chi
there is a connected,
C^{1+\beta}
, injectively immersed manifold
W^{\chi}(x)
satisfying the following:

(1)

T_{x}W^{\chi}(x)=E^{\chi}(x)
;

(2)

\alpha(n)W^{\chi}(x)=W^{\chi}(\alpha(n)(x))
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
;

(3)

W^{\chi}(x)
is the set of all
y\in M
satisfying

\limsup_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{k}\log d(\alpha(-kn)(y),\alpha(-kn)(x))<0\text{ for all $n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}$ with $\chi(n)>0$}.

The manifold
W^{\chi}(x)
is called the
coarse Lyapunov manifold
through
x
associated with the coarse Lyapunov exponent
\chi
.

To construct
W^{\chi}
-manifolds, given
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with
\chi(n)>0
let
W_{\alpha(n)}^{u}(x)
denote the unstable manifold for the diffeomorphism
\alpha(n)\colon M\to M
through
x
. Then, for almost every
x\in M
the manifold
W^{\chi}(x)
is the path component of the intersection

\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d},\chi(n)>0}W_{\alpha(n)}^{u}(x)

containing
x
.

8.
Metric entropy

8.1.
Metric entropy

General references for this subsection include
[
MR02172581
,
MR1086631
]
. Throughout, we take
(X,\mu)
to be a
standard probability space
. That is,
(X,\mu)
equipped with the
\sigma
-algebra of
\mu
-measurable sets is measurably isomorphic to an interval equipped with the Lebesgue measure and a countable number of point masses; see for instance
[
MR1086631
, Chapter 2]
.

8.1.1.
Measurable partitions and conditional measures

Recall that a partition
\xi
of
(X,\mu)
is
measurable
if the quotient
(Y,\hat{\mu}):=(X,\mu)/\xi
is a standard probability space. See also Definition
B.7
in Appendix
B.3
. This is a technical but crucial condition. For more discussion and other characterizations of measurability see
[
1208.4550
]
,
[
MR0047744
]
, and Appendices
B
and
C
.

A key property of measurable partitions is the existence and uniqueness of a family of conditional measures (or a
disintegration
) of
\mu
relative to this partition. Given a partition
\xi
of
X
, for
x\in X
we write
\xi(x)
for the element of
\xi
containing
x
.

Definition 8.1
.

Let
\xi
be a measurable partition of
(X,\mu)
. Then there is family of Borel probability measure
\{\mu^{\xi}_{x}\}_{x\in X}
, called a
family of conditional measures
of
\mu
relative to
\xi
, with the following properties: For almost every
x

(1)

\mu_{x}^{\xi}
is a Borel probability measure on
X
with
\mu^{\xi}_{x}(\xi(x))=1
;

(2)

if
y\in\xi(x)
then
\mu_{y}^{\xi}=\mu_{x}^{\xi}
.

Moreover, if
D\subset X
is a Borel subset then

(3)

x\mapsto\mu_{x}^{\xi}(D)
is measurable and

(4)

\mu(D)=\int\mu_{x}^{\xi}(D)\ d\mu(x)
.

Such a family is unique modulo
\mu
-null sets.

For construction and properties of
\{\mu^{\xi}_{x}\}
see for instance
[
MR0047744
]
. See also Appendix
B.2
for further discussion.

8.1.2.
Conditional information and conditional entropy

Given a measurable partition
\xi
of a standard probability space
(X,\mu)
, write
\{\mu_{x}^{\xi}\}
for a family of conditional measures of
\mu
with respect to the partition
\xi
. Given two measurable partitions
\eta,\xi
of
(X,\mu)

the
conditional information
of
\eta
relative to
\xi
is

I_{\mu}(\eta\mid\xi)(x)=-\log(\mu^{\xi}_{x}(\eta(x)))

and
the
conditional entropy
of
\eta
relative to
\xi
is

H_{\mu}(\eta\mid\xi)=\int I_{\mu}(\eta\mid\xi)(x)\ d\mu(x).

The
join

\eta\vee\xi
of two partitions
\eta
and
\xi
is

\eta\vee\xi=\{A\cap B\mid A\in\eta,B\in\xi\}.

The
entropy
of
\eta
is
H_{\mu}(\eta)=H_{\mu}(\eta\mid\{\emptyset,X\})
. Note that if
H_{\mu}(\eta)<\infty
then
\eta
is necessarily countable (mod zero) and
H_{\mu}(\eta)=-\sum_{C\in\eta}\log(\mu(C))\mu(C).

8.1.3.
Metric entropy of a transformation

Let
f\colon(X,\mu)\to(X,\mu)
be an invertible, measurable, measure-preserving transformation. Let
\eta
be an arbitrary measurable partition of
(X,\mu)
.
We define

\eta^{+}:=\bigvee_{i=0}^{\infty}f^{i}\eta,\quad\quad\quad\eta^{f}:=\bigvee_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}^{\infty}f^{i}\eta.

We define the
entropy of
f
given the partition
\eta
to be

h_{\mu}(f,\eta):=H_{\mu}(\eta\mid f\eta^{+})=H_{\mu}(\eta^{+}\mid f\eta^{+})=H_{\mu}(f^{-1}\eta^{+}\mid\eta^{+}).

We define the
\mu
-metric entropy of
f
to be
h_{\mu}(f)=\sup\{h_{\mu}(f,\eta)\}

where the supremum is taken over all measurable partitions of
(X,\mu)
.
If

\mu=\alpha\mu_{1}+\beta\mu_{2}

where
\alpha,\beta\in[0,1]
satisfy
\alpha+\beta=1
and
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
are
f
-invariant Borel probability measures then

(8.1)

h_{\mu}(f)=\alpha h_{\mu_{1}}(f)+\beta h_{\mu_{2}}(f).

8.2.
Entropy under factor maps

Let
(X,\mu)
and
(Y,\nu)
be standard probability spaces. Let
f\colon X\to X
and
g\colon Y\to Y
be measure-preserving transformations. Suppose there is a measurable map
\psi\colon X\to Y
with

\psi_{*}\mu=\nu

and

\psi\circ f=g\circ\psi.

In this case, we say that
g\colon(Y,\nu)\to(Y,\nu)
is a
measurable factor
of
f\colon(X,\mu)\to(X,\mu)
.

We note that entropy only decreases under measurable factors: if
g\colon(Y,\nu)\to(Y,\nu)
is a measurable factor of
f\colon(X,\mu)\to(X,\mu)
then

h_{\nu}(g)\leq h_{\mu}(f).

The difference between the entropies
h_{\nu}(g)
and
h_{\mu}(f)
is captured by the Abramov–Rokhlin theorem. Let
\zeta
be the measurable partition of
(X,\mu)
into level sets of
\psi\colon X\to Y
. Note that
\zeta
is an
f
-invariant partition:
\zeta=\zeta^{f}
. Define the conditional entropy
h_{\mu}(f\mid\zeta)
of
f
relative to
\zeta
to be

h_{\mu}(f\mid\zeta)=\sup_{\xi}h_{\mu}(f,\xi\vee\zeta)

where, as usual, the supremum is over all measurable partitions
\xi
of
(X,\mu)
.
We call
h_{\mu}(f\mid\zeta)
the
fiberwise entropy
of
f
. The Abramov–Rokhlin theorem (see
[
MR0140660
,
MR1179170
,
MR0476995
]
) states the following:

(8.2)

h_{\mu}(f)=h_{\nu}(g)+h_{\mu}(f\mid\zeta).

8.3.
Unstable entropy of a diffeomorphism

Let
f\colon M\to M
be a
C^{1+\beta}
diffeomorphism and let
\mu
be an ergodic,
f
-invariant measure.

8.3.1.
Partitions subordinate to a foliation

For the following discussion and in most applications considered in this text, we may take
\mathcal{F}
to be an
f
-invariant foliation of
M
with
C^{1+\beta}
leaves. More generally, we may take
\mathcal{F}
to be, in the terminology introduced in
[
AWB-GLY-P1
]
, an
f
-invariant,
tame measurable foliation
; that is,
\mathcal{F}
a partition of a full measure set by
C^{1+\beta}
manifolds with the property that locally, restricting to sets of large measure,
\mathcal{F}
has the structure of a continuous family of
C^{1+\beta}
discs. The primary examples of such measurable foliations include the partition into global
j
th unstable Pesin manifolds and the partition into global coarse Lyapunov manifolds in the setting of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-actions. Note that the partition into global leaves of a measurable foliation is not necessarily a measurable partition; rather locally the partition looks like a measurable family of
C^{1+\beta}
discs.

Write
\mathcal{F}(x)
for the leaf of
\mathcal{F}
through
x
. We say
\mathcal{F}
is
expanding
(for
f
) if
\mathcal{F}(x)\subset W^{u}(x)
, i.e. if
\mathcal{F}(x)
is a subset of the global unstable manifold through
x
for
f
discussed in Section
7.4
.
As a key example, one should consider
\mathcal{F}^{u}
, the partition of
M
into full global unstable manifolds.

Definition 8.2
.

We say a measurable partition
\xi
is
subordinate to
\mathcal{F}
if

(1)

\xi(x)\subset\mathcal{F}(x)
for
\mu
-a.e.
x
;

(2)

\xi(x)
contains an open (in the immersed topology) neighborhood of
x
in
\mathcal{F}(x)
for
\mu
-a.e.
x
;

(3)

\xi(x)
is precompact in (the immersed topology of)
\mathcal{F}(x)
for
\mu
-a.e.
x
;

8.3.2.
Partial ordering on the set of partitions

We recall the partial order on partitions of
(M,\mu)
. Let
\xi
and
\eta
be partitions of the probability space
(M,\mu)
. We write

\eta\prec\xi

and say that
\xi
is
finer
than
\eta
(or that
\eta
is
coarser
than
\xi
) if there is a subset
X\subset M
with
\mu(X)=1
such that for almost every
x
,

\xi(x)\cap X\subset\eta(x)\cap X.

We say
\eta=\xi
if
\eta\prec\xi
and
\xi\prec\eta
.

8.3.3.
Entropy conditioned on a foliation

We say that a partition
\xi
is
increasing
if
f\xi\prec\xi
where
f\xi
denotes the partition
f\xi=\{f(C)\mid C\in\xi\}
.

Definition 8.3
.

Given an expanding,
f
-invariant foliation
\mathcal{F}
we define the
entropy of
f
conditioned on
\mathcal{F}
to be

h_{\mu}(f\mid\mathcal{F})=h_{\mu}(f,\xi)

where
\xi
is any increasing, measurable partition subordinate to
\mathcal{F}
.

There are two small claim in Definition
8.3
: First we have that
h_{\mu}(f,\xi_{1})=h_{\mu}(f,\xi_{2})
for any two increasing partitions
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
subordinate to
\mathcal{F}
; see for example
[
MR819556
, Lemma 3.1.2]
. Second, such a partition
\xi
always exists. This was shown when
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}^{u}
is the partition into global unstable Pesin manifolds for a
C^{1+\beta}
diffeomorphism in
[
MR693976
]
(see also discussion in
[
MR819556
, (3.1)]
) extending a construction due to Sinai for uniformly hyperbolic dynamics
[
MR0197684
,
Sinai1968
]
; the proof in
[
MR693976
]
can be adapted for general invariant expanding
\mathcal{F}
.

When
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}^{u}
is the partition into full unstable manifolds, define the
unstable metric entropy of
f
to be

h^{u}_{\mu}(f):=h_{\mu}(f\mid\mathcal{F}^{u}).

The principal result (Corollary 5.3) of
[
MR819556
]
shows that for
C^{2}
diffeomorphisms we have equality of the metric entropy of
f
and the unstable metric entropy of
f
:

(8.3)

h_{\mu}(f)=h_{\mu}^{u}(f).

For
C^{1+\beta}
-diffeomorphism without zero Lyapunov exponents equality (
8.3
) was shown by Ledrappier in
[
MR743818
]
; for the general case of
C^{1+\beta}
-diffeomorphisms, (
8.3
) holds from
[
1608.05886
]
.

8.4.
Entropy, exponents, and geometry of conditional measures.

(See Appendix
D
for further details).
In this section, we consider the relationships between metric entropy
h_{\mu}(f)
, Lyapunov exponents, and the geometry of conditional measures along unstable manifolds.

Let
f\colon M\to M
be a
C^{1+\beta}
diffeomorphism and let
\mu
be an ergodic,
f
-invariant measure.
At one extreme we have the following generalization of Lemma
3.6
characterizing invariant measures with zero entropy.

Lemma 8.4
.

Let
\mu
be an ergodic,
f
-invariant measure on
M
and let
\xi
be a measurable partition of
(M,\mu)
subordinate to the partition into unstable manifolds.
The following are equivalent:

(1)

h_{\mu}(f)=0
;

(2)

for
\mu
-a.e.
x
, the conditional measure
\mu^{\xi}_{x}
has at least one atom;

(3)

for
\mu
-a.e.
x
, the conditional measure
\mu^{\xi}_{x}
is a single atom supported at
x
;

(4)

the partition of
(M,\mu)
into full
W^{u}
-manifolds is a measurable partition.

Proof sketch.

The implications
(1)

\implies

(4)
and
(1)

\implies

(3)
are a consequence of
[
MR819556
]
*Theorem B (see also
[
1608.05886
]
for
C^{1+\beta}
setting.) Indeed, if
h_{\mu}(f)=0
, then the Pinsker partition (see Section
8.6.2
below) is the point partition. From
[
MR819556
, Theorem B]
we have that the Pinsker partition is the measurable hull of (and in particular is coarser than) the partition into full unstable manifolds. As the point partition is the finest partition, it follows that the partition into full unstable manifolds is measurably equivalent to the point partition and
(3)
and
(4)
follow.

The implications
(4)

\implies

(3)
and
(2)

\implies

(3)
follow from the dynamics on unstable manifolds and ergodicity of the measure. For instance, to see
(4)

\implies

(3)
, assume the partition of
(M,\mu)
into full
W^{u}
-manifolds is measurable and let
\{\mu^{u}_{x}\}
denote a family of conditional probability measures for this partition. As
\mu
is
f
-invariant and as the partition into full unstable leaves is
f
-invariant, we have
f_{*}\mu^{u}_{x}=\mu^{u}_{f(x)}
for almost every
x
.

Given
x\in M
, let
W^{u}(x,R)
denote the metric ball of radius
R
centered at
x
in the internal metric of
W^{u}(x)
.
Given
\delta>0
and
R>0
, define the set
G_{\delta,R}
of
(\delta,R)
-good points to be

G_{\delta,R}:=\{x\in M\mid\mu^{u}_{x}(W^{u}(x,R))\geq 1-\delta\}.

Fix
R>0
such that
\mu(G_{\delta,R})>0
. Take a subset
G^{\prime}\subset G_{\delta,R}
with
\mu(G^{\prime})>0
such that the function

x^{\prime}\mapsto\mathrm{diam}^{u}_{f^{-n}(x^{\prime})}(f^{-n}(W^{u}(x^{\prime},R)))

converges to
0
uniformly on
G^{\prime}
as
n\to\infty
where
\mathrm{diam}^{u}_{x}(B)
denotes the diameter of
B\subset W^{u}(x)
with respect to the internal metric on
W^{u}(x)
.
For almost every
x
, we have
f^{n}(x)\in G^{\prime}
for infinitely many
n\in\mathbb{N}
. For such
x
and any
\epsilon>0
, there is
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
n\geq n_{0}
with
f^{n}(x)\in G^{\prime}
we have

f^{-n}(W^{u}(f^{n}(x),R))\subset W^{u}(x,\epsilon)

whence

\mu^{u}_{x}(W^{u}(x,\epsilon))\geq\mu^{u}_{f^{n}(x)}(W^{u}(f^{n}(x),R))\geq 1-\delta.

Taking
\epsilon\to 0
we have
\mu^{u}_{x}(\{x\})\geq 1-\delta
and, as
\delta
was arbitrary,
(3)
follows.

Finally, the implication
(3)

\implies

(1)
follows from Corollary 5.3 of
[
MR819556
]
(see (
8.3
) below) and the computation of unstable entropy in
Definition

8.3
.
∎

At the other extreme, we have the following definition.

Definition 8.5
.

We say
\mu
is an
SRB
measure (or satisfies the
SRB property
) if, for any measurable partition
\xi
of
(M,\mu)
subordinate to the partition into unstable manifolds, for almost every
x
the conditional measure
\mu^{\xi}_{x}
is absolutely continuous with respect to Riemannian volume on
W^{u}(x)
.

We have the following summary of a number of important results.

Theorem 8.6
.

Let
f\colon M\to M
be a
C^{1+\beta}
diffeomorphism and let
\mu
be an ergodic,
f
-invariant measure. Then

(1)

h_{\mu}(f)\leq\sum_{\lambda^{i}>0}m^{i}\lambda^{i}
;

(2)

if
\mu
is absolutely continuous with respect to volume then

h_{\mu}(f)=\sum_{\lambda^{i}>0}m^{i}\lambda^{i};

(3)

if
\mu
is SRB then
h_{\mu}(f)=\sum_{\lambda^{i}>0}m^{i}\lambda^{i}
.

Theorem
8.6
(1)
, known as the
Margulis–Ruelle inequality
, is proven in
[
MR516310
]
.
Theorem
8.6
(2)
, known as the
Pesin entropy formula
, is shown in
[
MR0466791
]
.
Theorem
8.6
(3)
was established by Ledrappier and Strelcyn in
[
MR693976
]
.
In the next section, we will complete Theorem
8.6
with Ledrappier’s Theorem, Theorem
9.3
, which provides a converse to Theorem
8.6
(3)
.

For general measures invariant under a
C^{2}
-diffeomorphism (for the case of
C^{1+\beta}
-diffeomorphisms, see
[
1608.05886
]
), Ledrappier and Young explain explicitly the defect from equality in Theorem
8.6
(1)
. This captures the intermediate geometry of measures with positive entropy (and hence non-atomic unstable conditional measures) but entropy strictly smaller than the sum of positive Lyapunov exponents.

Let
\delta^{i}
denote the (almost-surely constant value of the) pointwise dimension of
\mu
along the
i
th unstable manifolds; see Section
D.3
in Appendix
D
for definition. With
\delta^{0}=0
, let

\gamma^{i}=\delta^{i}-\delta^{i-1}.

The coefficients
\gamma^{i}
reflect the transverse geometry (in particular the transverse dimension) of the measure
\mu
inside of the
i
th unstable manifold transverse to the collection of
(i-1)
th unstable manifolds. In particular, we have
\gamma^{i}\leq m^{i}
(see
[
MR819557
, Proposition 7.3.2]
.)

Theorem 8.7

(
[
MR819557
]
)
.

Let
f\colon M\to M
be a
C^{1+\beta}
diffeomorphism and let
\mu
be an ergodic,
f
-invariant measure. Then

h_{\mu}(f)=\sum_{\lambda^{i}>0}\gamma^{i}\lambda^{i}.

(Note that the proof in
[
MR819557
]
required
f
to be
C^{2}
; following
[
1608.05886
]
and
[
MR1709302
]
*Appendix, the theorem holds when
f\in C^{1+\beta}.)

\bigast
8.5.
Coarse-Lyapunov entropy and entropy product structure.

Consider now
\alpha\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathrm{Diff}^{1+\beta}(M)
a smooth
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-action on a compact manifold
M
. Let
\mu
be an ergodic,
\alpha
-invariant measure. Recall that a coarse Lyapunov exponent
\chi
is a positive-proportionality class of Lyapunov exponents of
\alpha
. For almost every
x\in M
there is a coarse Lyapunov subspace
E^{\chi}(x)\subset T_{x}M
and a coarse Lyapunov manifold
W^{\chi}(x)
tangent to
E^{\chi}(x)
at
x
.

Let
\mathcal{F}^{\chi}
denote the partition of
M
into full
W^{\chi}
-manifolds. Given
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with
\chi(n)>0
, following the construction from
[
MR693976
]
we can find a measurable partition
\xi
of
(M,\mu)
that is subordinate to
\mathcal{F}^{\chi}
and increasing for
\alpha(n)
. We then define the
\chi
-entropy of
\alpha(n)
to be

h_{\mu}^{\chi}(\alpha(n))=h_{\mu}(\alpha(n)\mid\chi):=h_{\mu}(\alpha(n)\mid\mathcal{F}^{\chi})=h_{\mu}(\alpha(n),\xi).

The main result of
[
AWB-GLY-P3
]
is the following “product structure of entropy” for
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-actions.

Theorem 8.8

(
[
AWB-GLY-P3
, Corollary 13.2]
)
.

Let
\alpha\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathrm{Diff}^{1+\beta}(M)
be a smooth
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-action on a compact manifold
M
and let
\mu
be an ergodic,
\alpha
-invariant measure. Then for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}

h_{\mu}(\alpha(n))=\sum_{\chi(n)>0}h_{\mu}(\alpha(n)\mid\chi).

Fix
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and let
f=\alpha(n)
.
The formulas in
Theorem

8.7
and
Theorem

8.8
then look quite similar. However, the contribution of each Lyapunov exponent
\lambda^{i}
to the total entropy in
Theorem

8.7
is a “transverse entropy” (the coefficient
\gamma^{i}
is a measure of “transverse dimension”). In
Theorem

8.8
, the entropy of each coarse Lyapunov exponent
\chi
is a “tangential entropy”
h_{\mu}(\alpha(n)\mid\chi)
obtained by conditioning along
W^{\chi}
-manifolds. Thus,
Theorem

8.7
does not immediately imply
Theorem

8.8
. To show
Theorem

8.8
, one first shows that the total “transverse entropy” in
Theorem

8.7
contributed by all
\lambda^{i}\in\chi
is equal to the total conditional entropy
h_{\mu}(\alpha(n)\mid\chi)
. This is done in
[
AWB-GLY-P3
]
. The idea is to first establish an analogue of
Theorem

8.7
for the conditional entropy
h_{\mu}(f\mid\chi)
; this is done in
[
AWB-GLY-P2
]
where a formula of the form

h_{\mu}(f\mid\chi)=h_{\mu}(\alpha(n)\mid\chi)=\sum_{\lambda^{i}\in\chi}\gamma^{\chi,i}_{n}\lambda^{i}(n)

is shown. Then (following
[
MR1213080
]
) one uses that
n\mapsto h_{\mu}(\alpha(n)\mid\chi)
is linear on any half-cone where no coarse Lyapunov exponent
\chi^{\prime}
changes sign to show that the transverse dimensions
\gamma^{\chi,i}_{n}
of each
\lambda^{i}\in\chi
are independent of
n
and coincide with the transverse dimensions
\gamma^{i}
appearing in
Theorem

8.7
for
f=\alpha(n).

8.6.
Abstract ergodic theoretic constructions in smooth dynamics

(See Appendices
B
and
C
for further details.)
Let
f\colon M\to M
be a
C^{1+\beta}
diffeomorphism and let
\mu
be an
f
-invariant probability measure. We do not assume
\mu
to be ergodic.
We introduce here a number of measurable partitions of the measure space
(M,\mu)
associated with the dynamics
f
:

(1)

\mathcal{E}
, the ergodic decomposition (see
Definition

6.5
and
Theorem
B.11
in Appendix
B.4
);

(2)

\pi
, the Pinsker partition;

(3)

\Xi^{u}
, the measurable hull of the partition into unstable manifolds;

(4)

\Xi^{s}
, the measurable hull of the partition into stable manifolds.

In this section, we will define objects (
2
)–(
4
) above and explain the following two assertions:

(1)

\mathcal{E}\prec\Xi^{s}

(2)

\Xi^{s}=\pi=\Xi^{u}.

The first assertion is a standard fact in hyperbolic dynamics (which forms the first step in the Hopf argument for ergodicity) and is discussed in detail in Theorem
C.4
in Appendix
C
. The second is
[
MR819556
, Theorem B]
.

8.6.1.
Measurable hull of a partition

Given a (possibly nonmeasurable) partition
\xi
of
(M,\mu)
we write
\Xi(\xi)
for the
measurable hull
of
\xi
; that is,
\Xi(\xi)
is the finest measurable partition with
\Xi(\xi)\prec\xi
. If
\xi
is measurable, then we have
\Xi(\xi)=\xi
but in general
\Xi(\xi)
is strictly coarser than
\xi
.
We illustrate this concept with a few examples.

Example 8.9
.

Suppose that
\mu
is
f
-invariant and ergodic. Let
\mathcal{O}
be the partition into orbits of
f
. Then
\mathcal{O}
is not measurable (see
Example

B.6
in
Appendix

B
). The measurable hull of
\mathcal{O}
is the trivial partition
\Xi(\mathcal{O})=\{M,\emptyset\}
. For example, given a totally irrational flow on the torus
\mathbb{T}^{2}
, the partition into flow lines is not measurable and the measurable hull is the trivial partition.

More generally, if
\mu
is not ergodic then the measurable hull of
\mathcal{O}
is
\Xi(\mathcal{O})=\mathcal{E}
, the ergodic decomposition
(M,\mu)
. (See
Definition

6.5
and
Example

C.2
in Appendix
C
.)

For the following two examples, recall
Lemma

3.6
and
Lemma

8.4
.

Example 8.10
.

Let
f
be a
C^{1+\beta}
volume-preserving Anosov diffeomorphism of a connected manifold
M
. Let
\xi^{u}
denote the partition of
M
into unstable manifolds. Then
\xi^{u}
is not measurable (for the invariant volume). In fact, it is known that the measurable hull of
\xi^{u}
is again the trivial partition
\Xi(\xi^{u})=\{M,\emptyset\}
.

Example 8.11
.

Let
f\colon M\to M
be a
C^{1+\beta}
diffeomorphism and let
\mu
be any ergodic,
f
-invariant probability measure. Let
\xi^{u}
denote the partition of
M
into (full) unstable manifolds. Then
\xi^{u}
is measurable if and only if
h_{\mu}(f)=0
. In particular, if
h_{\mu}(f)>0
then the measurable hull of
\xi^{u}
is strictly coarser than
\xi^{u}
.

In general, given a
C^{1+\beta}
diffeomorphism
f\colon M\to M
and an ergodic,
f
-invariant probability measure
\mu

we let
\Xi^{u}
and
\Xi^{s}
denote, respectively, the measurable hulls of the partition of
(M,\mu)
into full unstable and stable manifolds.

We state the first relationship between the above objects in the following proposition whose proof follows immediately from the pointwise ergodic theorem. (See Theorem
C.4
, Appendix
C
.)

Proposition 8.12
.

Let
f\colon M\to M
be a
C^{1+\beta}
diffeomorphism and let
\mu
be any
f
-invariant probability measure. Then
\mathcal{E}\prec\Xi^{s}
and
\mathcal{E}\prec\Xi^{u}
.

Proof.

Let
\sigma(\mathcal{E})
and
\sigma(\Xi^{s})
denote the
\sigma
-algebras of
\mathcal{E}
-saturated and
\Xi^{s}
-saturated sets, respectively.

Consider any continuous function
\phi\colon M\to\mathbb{R}
. Then
\phi^{+}\colon M\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

\phi^{+}(x):=\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\phi(f^{k}(x))

is an
f
-invariant function that is constant along
W^{s}
-leaves.
In particular, the function
\phi^{+}
is measurable with respect to
\sigma(\mathcal{E})
and
\sigma(\Xi^{s})
.
Moreover, using that
C^{0}(M)
is separable and dense in
L^{1}(\mu)
and applying the pointwise ergodic theorem, it follows that the
\sigma
-algebra
\sigma(\mathcal{E})
is the minimal
\sigma
-algebra for which
\phi^{+}
is measurable for all continuous
\phi\colon M\to\mathbb{R}
. It follows that
\sigma(\mathcal{E})\subset\sigma(\Xi^{s})
whence
\mathcal{E}\prec\Xi^{s}
.
∎

8.6.2.
The Pinsker partition

Let
f\colon(X,\mu)\to(X,\mu)
be a measure-preserving transformation of a standard probability space
(X,\mu)
. The
Pinsker partition

\pi
of
f\colon(X,\mu)\to(X,\mu)
is the finest measurable partition
(X,\mu)
with the following property: for any measurable partition
\xi\prec\pi
, we have

h_{\mu}(f,\xi)=0.

Another characterization of
\pi
is the following:
\pi
is the unique
f
-invariant partition such that, if
(g,Y,\nu)
is a measurable factor of
(f,X,\mu)
with zero entropy, then
(g,Y,\nu)
is also a factor of the factor system
(f,X,\mu)/\pi
.

Our second relationship, stated as
[
MR819556
, Theorem B]
, characterizes the Pinsker partition in smooth dynamics.

Proposition 8.13

(
[
MR819556
, Theorem B]
)
.

Let
f\colon M\to M
be a
C^{1+\beta}
diffeomorphism and let
\mu
be any
f
-invariant Borel probability measure. Then we have equality of partitions

\Xi^{u}=\pi=\Xi^{s}.

Remark 8.14
.

We say that a measure-preserving transformation
f\colon(X,\mu)\to(X,\mu)
has the
K-property
(or the
Kolmogorov property
) if the Pinsker partition
\pi
is the trivial partition
\pi=\{\emptyset,X\}
. For such systems, every non-trivial factor has positive entropy.

Let
f\colon M\to M
be a
C^{1+\beta}
volume-preserving Anosov diffeomorphism. Anosov first showed that such maps are ergodic with respect to the invariant volume in
[
MR0224110
]
. In this setting, the analogue of
Proposition

8.13
is established in
[
MR0197684
]
; that is any set
A\in\pi
is equal modulo
0
to a set fully saturated by stable manifolds and also equal modulo
0
to a (possibly different set) that is fully saturated by unstable manifolds.
Using the absolute continuity of the stable and unstable foliations established by Anosov in his proof of ergodicity, one may show that any
A\in\pi
is equal modulo
0
to a set that is both fully saturated by stable manifolds and unstable manifolds. It follows that any
A\in\pi
is null or conull. In particular, this shows that volume-preserving Anosov diffeomorphisms have the K-property. This explains the conclusion in
Example

8.10
that
\Xi^{u}
is the trivial partition. See, for example,
[
MR2630044
]
for a modern discussion of absolute continuity and the
K
-property in uniformly (partially) hyperbolic settings.

9.
Entropy, invariance, and the SRB property

In dissipative (i.e. non-volume-preserving) dynamical systems, ergodic SRB measures
\mu
without zero Lyapunov exponents provide examples of
physical measures
: there is a set
B
of positive Lebesgue measure such that for any continuous function
\phi
, the forward time average of
\phi
along the orbit of points in
B
converges to
\int\phi\ d\mu
.
In applications and specific examples, a recurring problem is to establish the existence of physical and SRB measures. We pose a related question that arises naturally in the settings considered in this text:

Question 9.1
.

Given a diffeomorphism
f\colon M\to M

and an
f
-invariant measure
\mu
, how do you verify that
\mu
is an SRB measure?

Seemingly unrelated, consider a group
G
acting smoothly on a manifold
M
. We pose the following:

Question 9.2
.

Given a Borel probability measure
\mu
on
M
and a subgroup
H\subset G
, how do you verify that
\mu
is
H
-invariant?

One method to answer both of these questions is given in
Theorem

9.3
and
Theorem

9.5
below.

9.1.
Ledrappier’s theorem

(See Appendix
D
for further details.)
We outline one approach that solves both Question
9.1
and
9.2
in a number of settings. We discuss other approaches towards verifying the existence of SRB measures below.

We recall
8.3
where the notion of unstable entropy was introduced. The main result (Corollary 5.3) of
[
MR819556
]
shows for a
C^{2}
(see
[
1608.05886
]
for the
C^{1+\beta}
case) diffeomorphism
f\colon M\to M
preserving an ergodic probability measure
\mu
that the metric entropy of
f
and the unstable metric entropy of
f
coincide:

h_{\mu}(f)=h_{\mu}^{u}(f).
