Proposition

15.5
,
\mu
is automatically
G
-invariant.

\bigast
15.3.
Coarse-Lyapunov Abramov–Rokhlin Theorem and Proof of Proposition
15.5
.

The proof of Proposition
15.5
follows from a version of the Abramov–Rokhlin theorem (see equation (
8.2
), page
8.2
) for entropies subordinated to coarse-Lyapunov foliations. We outline these ideas and the proof of Proposition
15.5
in this section.

Each root
\beta^{i,j}
of
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
is a Lyapunov exponent for the
A
-action on
(M^{\alpha},\mu)
(corresponding to vectors tangent to
U^{i,j}
orbits in
M^{\alpha}
.) Let
\chi^{i,j}
denote the coarse Lyapunov exponent for the
A
-action on
(M^{\alpha},\mu)
containing
\beta^{i,j}
; that is,
\chi^{i,j}
is the equivalence class of all Lyapunov exponents for the
A
-action on
(M^{\alpha},\mu)
that are positively proportional to
\beta^{i,j}
.
Let
\{\lambda^{F}_{i,\mu},1\leq i\leq p\}
denote the collection of fiberwise Lyapunov exponents.
We have that

\text{$\chi^{i,j}=\{\beta^{i,j}\}$ if $\beta^{i,j}$ is not resonant with any $\lambda^{F}_{i,\mu}$}.

Otherwise,
\chi^{i,j}
contains
\beta^{i,j}
and all fiberwise Lyapunov exponents
\lambda^{F}_{i,\mu}\colon A\to\mathbb{R}
that are positively proportional to
\beta^{i,j}
.

For
\mu
-a.e.
x\in M^{\alpha}
there is a coarse Lyapunov manifold
W^{\chi^{i,j}}(x)
through
x
(see
Section

7.4.3
). If
\chi^{i,j}=\{\beta^{i,j}\}
then for
x\in M^{\alpha}
,
W^{\chi^{i,j}}(x)
is simply the
U^{i,j}
-orbit of
x
.
Otherwise,
W^{\chi^{i,j}}(x)
is a higher-dimensional manifold which intersects the fibers of
M^{\alpha}
nontrivially.
The partition of
(M^{\alpha},\mu)
into
W^{\chi^{i,j}}
-manifolds forms an
A
-invariant partition
\mathcal{F}^{\chi^{i,j}}
with
C^{1+\beta}
-leaves.

If
\beta^{i,j}
is resonant with some fiberwise Lyapunov exponent, let
\chi^{i,j,F}
denote the corresponding coarse fiberwise Lyapunov exponent; that is,
\chi^{i,j,F}
is the equivalence class of fiberwise Lyapunov exponents that are positively proportional to
\beta^{i,j}
. If
\beta^{i,j}
is not resonant with any fiberwise Lyapunov exponent, let
\chi^{i,j,F}
denote the zero functional. If
\chi^{i,j,F}
is nonzero, for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in M^{\alpha}
there is a
coarse fiberwise Lyapunov manifold

W^{\chi^{i,j,F}}(x)
through
x
.
(To construct fiberwise coarse Lyapunov manifolds
W^{\chi^{i,j,F}}(x)
, recall that the fibers of
M^{\alpha}
are permuted by the dynamics of
A
; all constructions in
Section

7
may be carried out fiberwise in the setting of a skew-product of diffeomorphisms over a measurable base if the
C^{1+\beta}
norms of the fibers are uniformly bounded.)
If
\chi^{i,j,F}
is zero, we simply define
W^{\chi^{i,j,F}}(x)=\{x\}
.
We have that
W^{\chi^{i,j,F}}(x)
is contained in the fiber through
x
and that
W^{\chi^{i,j}}(x)
is the
U^{i,j}
-orbit of
W^{\chi^{i,j,F}}(x)
.

For each
\chi^{i,j}
and
a\in A
with
\beta^{i,j}(a)>0
we define a conditional entropy of
a
conditioned on
\chi^{i,j}
-manifolds, denoted by
h_{\mu}(a\mid\chi^{i,j})
as in Section
8.4
.
Similarly, we can define a conditional entropy of
a
conditioned on the fiberwise coarse Lyapunov manifolds associated to
\chi^{i,j,F}
, denoted by
h_{\mu}(a\mid\chi^{i,j,F})
. In this setting, we have the following “coarse-Lyapunov Abramov–Rokhlin formula.”

Theorem 15.7
.

Let
\mu
be an ergodic,
A
-invariant measure on
M^{\alpha}
that projects to the Haar measure on
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})/\Gamma
. For any
a\in A
with
\beta^{i,j}(a)>0
,

(15.2)

h_{\mu}(a\mid\chi^{i,j})=h_{\mathrm{Haar}}(a\mid\beta^{i,j})+h_{{\mu}}(a\mid\chi^{i,j,F}).

Above,

h_{\mathrm{Haar}}(a\mid\beta^{i,j})

denotes the conditional entropy of translation by
a
in
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})/\Gamma
conditioned along
U^{i,j}
-orbits in
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})/\Gamma
.

Proof of Theorem
15.7
.

We first show the upper bound

(15.3)

h_{\mu}(a\mid\chi^{i,j})\leq h_{\mathrm{Haar}}(a\mid\beta^{i,j})+h_{{\mu}}(a\mid\chi^{i,j,F}).

This is a standard estimate in abstract ergodic theory whose proof we include for completeness.

Fix
a\in A
with
\beta^{i,j}(a)>0
. Let
\hat{\eta}
be an increasing measurable partition of
G/\Gamma
subordinate to the partition into
U^{i,j}
-orbits. Let
\pi\colon M^{\alpha}\to G/\Gamma
be the natural projection and let
\eta=\pi^{-1}\hat{\eta}
. Let
\xi\succ\eta
be an increasing measurable partition of
(M^{\alpha},\mu)
subordinate to the partition into
W^{\chi^{i,j}}
-manifolds. Let

\zeta
be the partition of
(M^{\alpha},\mu)
into the level sets of
\pi\colon M^{\alpha}\to G/\Gamma
; that is,
\zeta
is the partition of
M^{\alpha}
into fibers of the fibration
\pi\colon M^{\alpha}\to G/\Gamma
. Let
\xi^{F}:=\xi\vee\zeta
be the join of
\xi
and
\zeta
.
The partitions
\hat{\eta}
,
\xi
, and
\xi^{F}
satisfy

(1)

h_{\mu}(a,\eta)=h_{\mathrm{Haar}}(a,\hat{\eta})=h_{\mathrm{Haar}}(a\mid\beta^{i,j})
,

(2)

h_{\mu}(a,\xi)=h_{\mu}(a\mid\chi^{i,j}),
and

(3)

h_{\mu}(a,\xi^{F})=h_{\mu}(a\mid\chi^{i,j,F}).

We have the following computation (see for example
[
MR2729332
, Lemma 6.1]
):

\displaystyle h_{\mu}(a\mid\chi^{i,j})

\displaystyle:=h_{\mu}(a,\xi)

\displaystyle=h_{\mu}(a,\eta\vee\xi)

\displaystyle\leq h_{\mu}(a,\eta)+h_{\mu}\left(a,\xi\vee\bigvee_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}a^{n}(\eta)\right)

\displaystyle=h_{\mathrm{Haar}}(a,\hat{\eta})+h_{\mu}(a,\xi\vee\zeta)

\displaystyle=h_{\mathrm{Haar}}(a\mid\beta^{i,j})+h_{\mu}(a\mid\chi^{i,j,F})

and (
15.3
) follows.

On the other hand, summing over all roots
\beta
with
\beta(a)>0
we have from the classical Abramov–Rokhlin theorem (
8.2
), the product structure of entropy in Theorem
8.8
, and an analogous version of Theorem
8.8
for the fiberwise entropy
h_{\mu}(a\mid\zeta)
appearing in (
8.2
) that

\displaystyle h_{\mu}(a)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\chi(a)>0}h_{\mu}(a\mid\chi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta^{i,j}(a)>0}h_{\mu}(a\mid\chi^{i,j})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$\chi^{F}$ nonres.}\\
\chi^{F}(a)>0\end{subarray}}h_{\mu}(a\mid\chi^{F})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\beta^{i,j}(a)>0}\left(h_{\mathrm{Haar}}(a\mid\beta^{i,j})+h_{\mu}(a\mid\chi^{i,j,F})\right)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$\chi^{F}$ nonres.}\\
\chi^{F}(a)>0\end{subarray}}h_{\mu}(a\mid\chi^{F})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta^{i,j}(a)>0}h_{\mathrm{Haar}}(a\mid\beta^{i,j})+\sum_{\chi^{F}(a)>0}h_{\mu}(a\mid\chi^{F})

\displaystyle=h_{\mathrm{Haar}}(a)+h_{\mu}(a\mid\zeta)

\displaystyle=h_{\mu}(a).

In the second and third lines, the second sum is over all fiberwise coarse Lyapunov exponents that are not resonant with any root
\beta
of
G
.
Since entropies are non-negative quantities, it follows that

h_{\mu}(a\mid\chi^{i,j})=h_{\mathrm{Haar}}(a\mid\beta^{i,j})+h_{\mu}(a\mid\chi^{i,j,F})

for all
\beta^{i,j}
with
\beta^{i,j}(a)>0
.
∎

Remark 15.8
.

A more general version of Theorem
15.7
appears in
[
AWB-GLY-P3
, Theorem 13.6]
where the factor map
\pi
is allowed to be measurable and the measure
\pi_{*}(\mu)
on the factor system is an arbitrary ergodic,
A
-invariant measure.

The proof of Proposition
15.5
is a straightforward consequence of Theorem
15.7
.

Proof of Proposition
15.5
.

Given a root
\beta^{i,j}
and
a\in A
such that
\beta^{i,j}(a)>0
we have defined the conditional entropy
h_{\mu}(a\mid\beta^{i,j})
for the translation by
a
conditioned on
U^{i,j}
-orbits in
M^{\alpha}
.
From an appropriate version of the Margulis–Ruelle inequality (see Theorem
8.6
(1)
and (
9.3
)), for
a\in A
with
\beta^{i,j}(a)>0
we have that

(15.4)

h_{\mu}(a\mid\beta^{i,j})\leq\beta^{i,j}(a).

On the other hand, if
\beta^{i,j}
is nonresonant then
\chi^{i,j,F}
is the zero functional whence the coarse Lyapunov manifold
W^{\chi^{i,j}}(x)
associated to
\chi^{i,j}
is simply the
U^{i,j}
-orbit of
x
for every
x\in M^{\alpha}
and the term
h_{\mathrm{\mu}}(a\mid\chi^{i,j,F})
in (
15.2
) of Theorem
15.7
vanishes.
Hence, by Theorem
15.7
,

(15.5)

h_{\mu}(a\mid\beta^{i,j})=h_{\mu}(a\mid\chi^{i,j})=h_{\mathrm{Haar}}(a\mid\beta^{i,j})+0=\beta^{i,j}(a).

From (
15.4
) and (
15.5
), we have that the conditional entropy
h_{\mu}(a\mid\beta^{i,j})
attains its maximal possible value.
In particular, from the invariance principle in Theorem
9.5
(3)
, it follows that
\mu
is
U^{i,j}
-invariant.
∎

\bigast
15.4.
Proof of Theorem
11.4
using the advanced invariance principle.

We outline another proof of Theorem
11.4
based on Proposition
15.5
.
This more closely mimics the arguments in
[
1608.04995
]
.

Proof of Theorem
11.4
using
Proposition

15.5
.

From Claim
15.2
, it is sufficient to construct a
G
-invariant probability measure on
M^{\alpha}
.
Note that
A\simeq\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
is abelian (and in particular amenable, see
Remark

18.2
) and that the space of probability measures on
M^{\alpha}
projecting to the Haar measure on
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})/\Gamma
is nonempty,
A
-invariant, and weak-
*
compact. The Krylov-Bogolyubov theorem thus gives an
A
-invariant probability measure
\mu
on
M^{\alpha}
projecting to the Haar measure on
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})/\Gamma
. Moreover, since the Haar measure on
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})/\Gamma
is
A
-ergodic, we may assume
\mu
is
A
-ergodic.

Let
\dim(M)=d\leq n-2
. The fiberwise tangent bundle
F
of
M^{\alpha}
is
d
-dimensional and therefore there are at most
d
fiberwise Lyapunov exponents

\lambda^{F}_{1,\mu},\cdots,\lambda^{F}_{k,\mu},\quad k\leq d.

As no pair of roots of
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
is positively proportional, there are at most
d
roots that are resonant with the fiberwise Lyapunov exponent
\lambda^{F}_{j,\mu}
. All other roots
\beta^{i,j}
are nonresonant. By Proposition
15.5
, if
\beta^{i,j}
is not resonant with any
\lambda^{F}_{j,\mu}
, then
\mu
is
U^{i,j}
-invariant.

Let
H\subset\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
be the subgroup that preserves
\mu
. We claim
H=G
completing the proof.
As
d\leq n-2
,
\mu
is invariant under
A
and all-but-at-most-
(n-2)
root subgroups
U^{i,j}
. Then
H
has codimension at most
(n-2)
.
From
[
1608.04995
, Lemma 2.5]
, we have that
H
is parabolic; that is,
H
is conjugate to a group of block-upper-triangular matrices (see
Remark

10.8
). However, the proper closed parabolic subgroups
of
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
of maximal codimension are conjugate to the codimension
(n-1)
subgroup

(15.6)

\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}*&*&\cdots&*\\
0&*&\cdots&*\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&*&\cdots&*\end{array}\right)\right\}.

(See Section VII.7, especially Proposition 7.76 of
[
MR1920389
]
for discussion on the structure of parabolic subgroups.)
As
H
has codimension at most
n-2
, it thus follows that
H=G
as there are no proper parabolic subgroups of
G
with codimension less than
(n-1)
. ∎

Remark 15.9
.

The above proof has the advantage that it generalizes to give invariance of measures in the optimal critical dimension for actions by lattices in other Lie groups including
\mathrm{Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R}),

\mathrm{SO}(n,n),
or
\mathrm{SO}(n,n+1)
on manifolds of the optimal dimension.
As discussed in
15.1
for a lattice
\Gamma
in a group such as
G=\mathrm{Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
, the proof in
15.1
yields that any
C^{1+\beta}
action of
\Gamma
on a manifold of dimension at most
\mathrm{rank}(G)-1
, any
A
-invariant measure on
M^{\alpha}
that projects to Haar on
G/\Gamma
is
G
-invariant. However, the above proof establishes this result for manifolds
M
where the critical dimension is
r(G)
, the number in the last column of
Table

1
(page
1
) defined in
[
AWBFRHZW-latticemeasure
,
1608.04995
]
(see also
Footnote

4
.) For
\mathbb{R}
-split groups
G
we have
r(G)=d_{0}(G)
.
In particular, the above proof can be adapted to show the following:

Theorem 15.10
.

Let
G
be a higher-rank simple Lie group
G
with finite center, let
\Gamma
be a lattice in
G
, let
M
be a closed manifold, and let
\alpha\colon\Gamma\to\mathrm{Diff}^{1+\beta}(M)
be an action. Then

(1)

if
\dim(M)\leq r(G)-1
, every
A
-invariant probability measure on
M^{\alpha}
that projects to the Haar measure on
G/\Gamma
is
G
-invariant;

(2)

if
\dim(M)\leq r(G)
and
\alpha
is volume-preserving, every
A
-invariant probability measure on
M^{\alpha}
that projects to the Haar measure on
G/\Gamma
is
G
-invariant.

In particular, if
\dim(M)\leq r(G)-1
, every action
\alpha\colon\Gamma\to\mathrm{Diff}^{1+\beta}(M)
preserves a Borel probability measure.

16.
Proof outline of Theorem
11.6

We outline the proof of Theorem
11.6
for the case of
C^{\infty}
actions of cocompact lattices in
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
.
That is, for
n\geq 3
, we consider a cocompact lattice
\Gamma
in
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
and show that every homomorphism
\alpha\colon\Gamma\to\mathrm{Diff}^{\infty}(M)
has finite image when

(1)

M
is a compact manifold of dimension at most
(n-2)
, or

(2)

M
is a compact manifold of dimension at most
(n-1)
and
\alpha
preserves a volume form
\mathrm{vol}
.

The broad outline of the proof consists of 3 steps.

16.1.
Step 1: Subexponential growth

In the case that
\Gamma\subset\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
is cocompact, using its action on
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
and that
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
is a proper length space one may show that
\Gamma
is finitely generated (see for example
[
MR2850125
, Theorem 8.2]
). More generally, it is a classical fact that all lattices
\Gamma
in semisimple Lie groups are finitely generated.

Fix a finite symmetric generating set
S
for
\Gamma
. Given
\gamma\in\Gamma
, let
|\gamma|=|\gamma|_{S}
denote the word-length of
\gamma
relative to this generating set; that is,

|\gamma|=\min\{k:\gamma=s_{k}\cdots s_{1},s_{i}\in S\}.

Note that if we replace the finite generating set
S
with another finite generating set
S^{\prime}
, there is a uniform constant
C
such that the word-lengths are uniformly distorted:

|\gamma|_{S^{\prime}}\leq C|\gamma|_{S}.

Thus all definitions below will be independent of the choice of
S
.

Equip
TM
with a Riemannian metric and corresponding norm.

Definition 16.1
.

We say that an action
\alpha\colon\Gamma\to\mathrm{Diff}^{1}(M)
has
uniform subexponential growth of derivatives
if for every
\epsilon>0
there is a
C=C_{\epsilon}
such that for every
\gamma\in\Gamma
,

\sup_{x\in M}\|D_{x}\alpha(\gamma)\|\leq Ce^{\epsilon|\gamma|}.

Note that if
\alpha\colon\Gamma\to\mathrm{Diff}^{1}(M)
has uniform subexponential growth of derivatives it follows for every
\epsilon>0
that there is a
C=C_{\epsilon}
such that for every
\gamma\in\Gamma
,

\sup_{x\in M}\|D_{x}\alpha(\gamma)\|\geq Ce^{-\epsilon|\gamma|}.

The following is the main result of
[
1608.04995
]
in the case of cocompact lattices in
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
.

Theorem 16.2

(
[
1608.04995
, Theorem 2.8]
)
.

For
n\geq 3
, let
\Gamma\subset\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
be a cocompact lattice. Let
\alpha\colon\Gamma\to\mathrm{Diff}^{2}(M)
be an action. Suppose that either

(1)

\dim(M)\leq n-2
, or

(2)

\dim(M)=n-1
and
\alpha
preserves a smooth volume.

Then
\alpha
has uniform subexponential growth of derivatives.

Remark 16.3
.

The proof of Theorem
16.2
is the only place in the proof of Theorem
11.6
where cocompactness of
\Gamma
is used. It is not required for Steps 2 or 3 below. For
\Gamma=\mathrm{SL}(m,\mathbb{Z})
, the analogue of Theorem
16.2
is established in
[
1710.02735
]
and has been announced for general lattices in
[
BFHWM
]
.

16.2.
Step 2: Strong property (T) and averaging Riemannian metrics

Assume
\alpha\colon\Gamma\to\mathrm{Diff}^{\infty}(M)
is an action by
C^{\infty}
diffeomorphisms.
7
7

7

For
C^{2}
actions, one replaces the Hilbert Sobolev spaces
W^{2,k}(S^{2}(T^{*}M)))
below with appropriate Banach Sobolev spaces
W^{p,1}(S^{2}(T^{*}M)))
and verifies such spaces are of the type
\mathcal{E}_{10}
considered in
[
MR3407190
]
.
The action
\alpha
of
\Gamma
on
M
induces an action
\alpha_{\#}
of
\Gamma
on tensor powers of the cotangent bundle of
M
by pull-back:
Given
\omega\in(T^{*}M)^{\otimes k}
write

\alpha_{\#}(\gamma)\omega=\alpha(\gamma^{-1})^{*}\omega;

that is,
if
v_{1},\dots v_{k}\in T_{x}M
then

\alpha_{\#}(\gamma)\omega(x)(v_{1},\dots,v_{k})=\omega(x)(D_{x}\alpha(\gamma^{-1})v_{1},\dots,D_{x}\alpha(\gamma^{-1})v_{k}).

In particular, we obtain an action of
\Gamma
on the set of Riemannian metrics which naturally sits as a half-cone inside
S^{2}(T^{*}M)
, the vector space of all symmetric 2-forms on
M
.

Note that
\alpha_{\#}
preserves
C^{\ell}(S^{2}(T^{*}(M)))
, the subspace of all
C^{\ell}
sections of
S^{2}(T^{*}M)
for any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
.

Fix a volume form
\mathrm{vol}
on
M
. The norm on
TM
induced by the background Riemannian metric induces a norm on each fiber of
S^{2}(T^{*}M)
. We then obtain a natural notion of measurable and integrable sections of
S^{2}(T^{*}M)
with respect to
\mathrm{vol}
. Let
\mathcal{H}^{k}=W^{2,k}(S^{2}(T^{*}M))
be the Sobolev space of symmetric 2-forms whose weak derivatives of order
\ell
are bounded with respect to the
L^{2}(\mathrm{vol})
-norm for
0\leq\ell\leq k
. Then
\mathcal{H}^{k}
is a Hilbert space. Let
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}}
denote the corresponding Sobolev norm on
\mathcal{H}^{k}
as well as the induced operator norm on the space
B(\mathcal{H}^{k})
of bounded operators on
\mathcal{H}^{k}
. Working in local coordinates, the Sobolev embedding theorem implies that

\mathcal{H}^{k}\subset C^{\ell}(S^{2}(T^{*}(M)))

as long as

\ell<k-\dim(M)/2.

In particular, for
k
sufficiently large, an element
\omega
of
\mathcal{H}^{k}
is a
C^{\ell}
section of
S^{2}(T^{*}M)
which will be a
C^{\ell}

Riemannian metric on
M
if it is positive definite.

The action
\alpha_{\#}
is a representation of
\Gamma
by bounded operators on
\mathcal{H}^{k}
.
From Theorem
16.2
, we obtain strong control on the norm growth of the induced representation
\alpha_{\#}
. In particular, we obtain that the representation
\alpha_{\#}\colon\Gamma\to B(\mathcal{H}^{k})
has
subexponential norm growth:

Lemma 16.4
.

Let
\alpha\colon\Gamma\to\mathrm{Diff}^{\infty}(M)
have uniform subexponential growth of derivatives. Then,
for all
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
there is
C>0
such that

\|\alpha_{\#}(\gamma)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}}\leq Ce^{\epsilon^{\prime}|\gamma|}

for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
.

The proof of Lemma
16.4
follows from the chain rule, Leibniz rule, and computations that bound the growth of higher-order derivatives by polynomial functions in the growth of the first derivative. See
[
MR2198325
, Lemma 6.4]
and discussion in
[
1608.04995
, Section 6.3]
.

We use the main result from
[
MR3407190
,
MR2423763
]
: cocompact lattices
\Gamma
in higher-rank simple Lie groups (such as
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
for
n\geq 3
) satisfy Lafforgue’s
strong Banach property (T)
first introduced in
[
MR2423763
]
. The result for
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
and its cocompact lattices (as well as other higher-rank simple Lie groups containing a subgroup isogenous to
\mathrm{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})
) is established by Lafforgue in Corollary 4.1 and Proposition 4.3 of
[
MR2423763
]
; for cocompact lattices in certain other higher-rank Lie groups (containing a subgroup isogenous to
\mathrm{Sp}(4,\mathbb{R})
), the results of
[
MR3407190
]
are needed.
See also
[
delaSallenonuniform
]
for the case of nonuniform lattices. Strong Banach property (T) considers representations
\pi
of
\Gamma
by bounded operators on certain Banach spaces
E
(of type
\mathcal{E}_{10}
). If such representations have sufficiently slow exponential norm growth, then there exists a sequence of averaging operators
p_{n}
converging to a projection
p_{\infty}
such that for any vector
v\in E
, the limit
p_{\infty}(v)
is
\pi
-invariant.
In the case that
E
is a Hilbert space (which we may assume when
\alpha
is an action by
C^{\infty}
diffeomorphisms)
we have the following formulation. Note that Lemma
16.4
(which follows from Theorem
16.2
) ensures our representation
\alpha_{\#}
satisfies the hypotheses of the theorem.

Theorem 16.5

(
[
MR3407190
,
delaSallenonuniform
,
MR2423763
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space and for
n\geq 3
, let
\Gamma
be a lattice in
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
.

There exists
\epsilon>0
such that for any representation
\pi\colon\Gamma\to B(\mathcal{H})
, if there exists
C_{\epsilon}>0
such that

\displaystyle\|\pi(\gamma)\|\leq C_{\epsilon}e^{\epsilon|\gamma|}

for all
\gamma\in\Gamma

then there exists a sequence of averaging operators
p_{n}=\sum w_{i}\pi(\gamma_{i})
in
B(\mathcal{H})
—where
w_{i}\geq 0
,
\sum w_{i}=1
, and
w_{i}=0
for every
\gamma_{i}\in\Gamma
of word-length larger than
n
—such that for any vector
v\in\mathcal{H}
, the sequence
v_{n}=p_{n}(v)\in\mathcal{H}
converges to an invariant vector
v^{*}=p_{\infty}(v)
.

Moreover the convergence is exponentially fast: there exist
0<\lambda<1
and
C=C_{\lambda}
such that
\|v_{n}-v_{*}\|\leq C\lambda^{n}\|v\|
.

Theorem
16.5
as stated in
[
MR3407190
,
MR2423763
]
requires that
\Gamma
be cocompact. The extension to nonuniform lattices is announced in
[
delaSallenonuniform
]
. The exponential convergence in Theorem
16.5
is often not explicitly stated in the definition of strong property (T) or in statements of theorems establishing that the property holds for lattices in higher-rank simple Lie groups; however, the exponential convergence follows from the proofs.

We complete Step 2 with the following computation.

Proposition 16.6
.

For
n\geq 3
, let
\Gamma\subset\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
be a lattice and let
\alpha\colon\Gamma\to\mathrm{Diff}^{\infty}(M)
be an action with uniform subexponential growth of derivatives. Then for any
\ell
, there is a
C^{\ell}
Riemannian metric
g
on
M
such that

\alpha(\Gamma)\subset\mathrm{Isom}_{g}(M).

Proof.

Consider an arbitrary
C^{\infty}
Riemannian metric
g
. For any
k
, we have
g\in\mathcal{H}^{k}
. We apply
Theorem

16.5
and its notation to the representation
\alpha_{\#}\colon\Gamma\to B(\mathcal{H}^{k})
with the
g
the initial vector
v
. As averages of finitely many Riemannian metrics are still Riemannian metrics we have that
g_{n}:=p_{n}(g)
is positive definite for every
n
. In particular, the limit
g_{\infty}=p_{\infty}(g)
is in the closed cone of positive (possibly indefinite) symmetric 2-tensors in
\mathcal{H}^{k}
. Having taken
k
sufficiently large we have that
g_{\infty}
is
C^{\ell}
; in particular,
g_{\infty}
is continuous, everywhere defined, and positive everywhere. We need only confirm that
g_{\infty}
is non-degenerate, i.e. is positive definite on
T_{x}M
for every
x\in M
.

Given any
x\in M
and unit vector
\xi\in T_{x}M
, for any
\epsilon>0
we have from Definition
16.1
that there is a
C_{\epsilon}>0
such that

\displaystyle p_{n}(g)(\xi,\xi)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum w_{i}\alpha_{\#}(\gamma_{i})g\right)(\xi,\xi)

\displaystyle=\sum w_{i}g(D_{x}\alpha(\gamma_{i}^{-1})\xi,D_{x}\alpha(\gamma_{i}^{-1})\xi)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{C_{\epsilon}^{2}}e^{-2\epsilon n}

where we use that
w_{i}>0
only when
\gamma_{i}
has word-length at most
n
.

On the other hand, from the exponential convergence in Theorem
16.5
we have

\displaystyle|p_{n}(g)(\xi,\xi)-p_{\infty}(g)(\xi,\xi)|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\lambda}\lambda^{n}.

Thus

p_{\infty}(g)(\xi,\xi)\geq\frac{1}{C_{\epsilon}^{2}}e^{-2\epsilon n}-C_{\lambda}\lambda^{n}

for all
n\geq 0
. Taking
\epsilon>0
sufficiently small we can ensure that

{C_{\epsilon}^{2}}e^{2\epsilon n}<\frac{1}{C_{\lambda}}\lambda^{-n}

for all sufficiently large
n
. Then, for all sufficiently large
n
we have

\frac{1}{C_{\epsilon}^{2}}e^{-2\epsilon n}>C_{\lambda}\lambda^{n}

and thus
p_{\infty}(g)(\xi,\xi)>0.

∎

16.3.
Step 3: Margulis superrigidity with compact codomain

From Steps 1 and 2 we have that any action
\alpha\colon\Gamma\to\mathrm{Diff}^{\infty}(M)
as in Theorem
11.6
preserves a
C^{\ell}
Riemannian metric
g
.
In the general case of
C^{2}
-actions (or even
C^{1+\beta}
-actions), we have that any action
\alpha\colon\Gamma\to\mathrm{Diff}^{2}(M)
preserves a continuous Riemannian metric
g
. See
[
1608.04995
, Theorem 2.7]
.
We thus have

\alpha\colon\Gamma\to\mathrm{Isom}_{g}(M).

The group
\mathrm{Isom}_{g}(M)
is compact. When
g
is at least
C^{1}
, it follows from the classical result of Myers and Steenrod
[
MR1503467
]
that
\mathrm{Isom}_{g}(M)
is a Lie group; for continuous
g
we use the solution to the Hilbert–Smith conjecture for actions by bi-Lipschitz maps
[
MR1464908
]
to conclude that
\mathrm{Isom}_{g}(M)
is a Lie group.
Let
\dim(M)=m
. Then

(16.1)

\dim(\mathrm{Isom}_{g}(M))\leq\dfrac{m(m+1)}{2}.

Indeed, the orbit of any point
p\in M
under
\mathrm{Isom}_{g}(M)
has dimension at most
m
and the dimension of the stabilizer of a point is at most
\frac{m(m-1)}{2}
, the dimension of
\mathrm{SO}(m)
; thus

\dim(\mathrm{Isom}_{g}(M))\leq m+\frac{m(m-1)}{2}.

With
K=\mathrm{Isom}_{g}(M)
we thus obtain a compact-valued representation
\alpha\colon\Gamma\to K
. By equation
(
16.1
), if
m<\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{8n^{2}-7}-\frac{1}{2}

then
\dim(\mathfrak{su}(n))=n^{2}-1>\dim(K)
; by conclusion
(2)
of Theorem
12.4
,
\alpha(\Gamma)
is thus contained in a 0-dimensional subgroup of
K
.
This holds in particular if
m\leq n-1
. We thus conclude that the image

\alpha(\Gamma)\subset K=\mathrm{Isom}_{g}(M)

is finite.

Summarizing the arguments from Steps 2 and 3, we obtain the following.

Theorem 16.7
.

For
n\geq 3
, let
\Gamma\subset\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
be a lattice. Let
\alpha\colon\Gamma\to\mathrm{Diff}^{2}(M)
be an action with uniform subexponential growth of derivatives.

Then, if

\dim(M)<\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{8n^{2}-7}-\frac{1}{2},

the image
\alpha(\Gamma)
is finite.

17.
Proof outline of Theorem
16.2

To establish Theorem
11.6
, from the discussion in Section
16
it is enough to establish
Theorem
16.2
: the action
\alpha
has uniform subexponential growth of derivatives. We outline the proof of Theorem
16.2
.

17.1.
Setup for proof

For
n\geq 3
, let
\Gamma\subset\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
be a cocompact lattice. Let
M
be a compact manifold and let
\alpha\colon\Gamma\to\mathrm{Diff}^{2}(M)
be an action. Assume either that
\dim(M)\leq n-2
or that
\dim(M)\leq n-1
and that
\alpha
preserves a volume form.
We recall the following constructions from the proof of Theorem
11.4
:

(1)

The manifold
M^{\alpha}=(\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})\times M)/\Gamma
is the suspension space introduced in Section
14.1
.
M^{\alpha}
is a fiber bundle over
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})/\Gamma
with fibers diffeomorphic to
M
. Moreover,
M^{\alpha}
and
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})/\Gamma
have natural (left)
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
-actions and the projection
\pi\colon M^{\alpha}\to\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})/\Gamma
intertwines these
G
-actions.

(2)

A\subset\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
denotes the subgroup of diagonal matrices with positive entries. We have
A\simeq\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
which is a higher-rank, free abelian group if
n\geq 3
.

(3)

Given an ergodic,
A
-invariant Borel probability measure
\mu
on
M^{\alpha}
we have
fiberwise Lyapunov exponents
.

\lambda^{F}_{1,\mu},\dots,\lambda^{F}_{p,\mu}\colon A\to\mathbb{R}

for the restriction of the derivative of the
A
-action on
M^{\alpha}
to the fibers of
M^{\alpha}
introduced in Section
14.2
.

(4)

\beta^{i,j}\colon A\to\mathbb{R}
are the
roots
of
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
and
U^{i,j}
are the corresponding
root subgroups
introduced in Section
13.1
.

17.2.
Two key propositions

The proof of Theorem
16.2
is by contradiction and follows from the following two propositions. Our first key proposition is an analogue of Proposition
7.2
.

Proposition 17.1
.

Suppose that
\alpha\colon\Gamma\to\mathrm{Diff}^{1}(M)
fails to have uniform subexponential growth of derivatives. Then there exists a Borel probability measure
\mu^{\prime}
on
M^{\alpha}
such that

(1)

\mu^{\prime}
is
A
-invariant and ergodic;

(2)

there exists a nonzero fiberwise Lyapunov exponent
\lambda^{F}_{j,\mu^{\prime}}\colon A\to\mathbb{R}
.

The proof of Proposition
17.1
is very similar to the proof of Proposition
7.2
with some minor modifications and notational differences. We include an outline of the proof in
18.3
; see also
[
1608.04995
, Section 4]
for complete details. Obtaining uniform control on the growth of a cocycle from bounds on the top Lyapunov exponent over all invariant measures is a standard technique in dynamical systems; see for instance
[
MR1986306
,
MR1643183
,
Hurtado_Burnside
,
MR2776369
]
.

The measure
\mu^{\prime}
in Proposition
17.1
projects to an ergodic,
A
-invariant measure on
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})/\Gamma
. If
\mu^{\prime}
projected to the Haar measure on
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})/\Gamma
then, from Theorem
15.1
and the bounds on the dimension
M
, the measure
\mu^{\prime}
would be
G
-invariant and, as explained below, the proof of Theorem
16.2
would be complete. However, there may exist ergodic
A
-invariant measures on

\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})/\Gamma
that are not the Haar measure.
8
8

8

In fact, for certain lattices
\Gamma
there exist ergodic
A
-invariant measures on
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})/\Gamma
that have positive entropy for some element of
A
as shown by M. Rees; see
[
MR1989231
, Section 8]
.

By carefully averaging the measure
\mu^{\prime}
along root subgroups
U^{i,j}
and applying Ratner’s measure classification theorem
[
MR1262705
]
to the projected measure on
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})/\Gamma
we obtain the following.

Proposition 17.2
.

Let
\alpha\colon\Gamma\to\mathrm{Diff}^{1}(M)
be an action.
Suppose there exists an ergodic,
A
-invariant measure
\mu^{\prime}
on the suspension space
M^{\alpha}
with a nonzero fiberwise Lyapunov exponent
\lambda^{F}_{j^{\prime},\mu^{\prime}}\colon A\to\mathbb{R}
. Then there exists a Borel probability measure
\mu
on
M^{\alpha}
such that

(1)

\mu
is
A
-invariant and ergodic;

(2)

there exists a nonzero fiberwise Lyapunov exponent
\lambda^{F}_{j,\mu}\colon A\to\mathbb{R}
;

(3)

\mu
projects to the Haar measure on
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})/\Gamma
.

Remark 17.3
.

(1)

Propositions
17.1
and
17.2
hold in full generality; they do not depend on the comparison between the dimension of
M
and the rank of
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
. The constraint on the dimension of
M
is used to obtain a contradiction in the proof of Theorem
16.2
by applying Theorem
15.1
and Zimmer’s cocycle superrigidity to the fiberwise derivative cocycle.

(2)

Propositions
17.1
and
17.2
heavily use the fact that
\Gamma
is cocompact in
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
so that the manifold
M^{\alpha}
is compact. For instance, if
M^{\alpha}
is not compact then the proof of Proposition
17.1
(compare with proof of Proposition
7.2
) fails as there may be escape of mass into the cusp of
G/\Gamma
. Thus, more subtle arguments are required to establish the analogue of
Theorem

16.2
in the case that
\Gamma
is nonuniform. In the case that
\Gamma=\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{Z})
, such arguments appear in
[
1710.02735
]
.

(3)

Both
Proposition

17.1
and
Proposition

17.2
hold for
C^{1}
actions. The
C^{1+\beta}
hypotheses is later used (along with the dimension bounds) to conclude that the
A
-invariant measure
\mu
obtained in
Proposition

17.2
is, in fact,
G
-invariant by applying
Theorem

15.1
.

17.3.
Proof of Theorem
16.2

We deduce Theorem
16.2
from Proposition
17.1
, Proposition
17.2
, Theorem
15.1
, and Theorem
12.2
.

Proof of Theorem
16.2
.

Let
\alpha\colon\Gamma\to\mathrm{Diff}^{2}(M)
be as in Theorem
16.2
. For the sake of contradiction, assume that

\alpha\colon\Gamma\to\mathrm{Diff}^{2}(M)

fails to have uniform subexponential growth of derivatives.
Let
\mu^{\prime}
be the measure guaranteed by Proposition
17.1
. We then apply Proposition
17.2
to obtain an ergodic,
A
-invariant Borel probability measure
\mu
on
M^{\alpha}
that projects to the Haar measure on
G/\Gamma
and has a non-zero fiberwise Lyapunov exponent.
In either case considered in
Theorem

16.2
, it follows from Theorem
15.1
that
\mu
is
G
-invariant.

Recall that we write
\pi\colon M^{\alpha}\to\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})/\Gamma
for the natural projection and let
F
be the fiberwise tangent bundle; that is,
F
is sub-vector-bundle of
TM^{\alpha}
given by
F=\ker D\pi
.
As
F
is
G
-invariant, we may apply Zimmer’s cocycle superrigidity theorem, Theorem
12.2
, to the fiberwise derivative cocycle
\mathcal{A}(g,x)={D_{x}g}{\restriction_{{F(x)}}}
of the
\mu
-preserving
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
-action on
M^{\alpha}
.
Since the fibers have dimension at most
n-1
and since there are no non-trivial representations
\rho\colon\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})\to\mathrm{SL}(d,\mathbb{R})
for
d<n
, it follows from Theorem
12.2
that the fiberwise derivative cocycle
\mathcal{A}(g,x)={D_{x}g}{\restriction_{{F(x)}}}
is cohomologous to a compact-valued cocycle: there is a compact group
K\subset\mathrm{SL}(d,\mathbb{R})
and a measurable map
\Phi\colon M^{\alpha}\to\mathrm{GL}(d,\mathbb{R})
such that

\Phi(g\cdot x){D_{x}g}{\restriction_{{F(x)}}}\Phi(x)^{-1}\in K.

By Poincaré recurrence to sets on which the norm and conorm of
\Phi
are bounded, it follows for any
g\in G
and
\epsilon>0
that the set of
x\in M^{\alpha}
such that

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\|{D_{x}g^{n}}{\restriction_{{F(x)}}}\|\geq{\epsilon}

has
\mu
-measure zero. This contradicts the existence of nonzero fiberwise Lyapunov exponent for
\mu
. This contradiction completes the proof of Theorem
16.2
.
∎

18.
Discussion of the proof of Propositions
17.1
and
17.2

We outline the main steps in the proof of Propositions
17.1
and
17.2
.

18.1.
Averaging measures on
M^{\alpha}

Let
H=\{h^{t}:t\in\mathbb{R}\}
be a 1-parameter subgroup of
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
. Given a measure
\mu
on
M^{\alpha}
and
T\geq 0
we define

H^{T}\ast\mu:=\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}(h^{t})_{*}\mu\ dt

to be the measure obtained by averaging the translates of
\mu
over the interval
[0,T]
.

Let
s\in A
. Given any
s
-invariant measure
\mu
on
M^{\alpha}
, the
average top fiberwise Lyapunov exponent
of
s
with respect to
\mu
is

(18.1)

\lambda^{F}_{\mathrm{top}}(s,\mu)=\inf_{n\geq 1}\frac{1}{n}\int\log\|{D_{x}(s^{n})}{\restriction_{{F}}}\|\ d\mu(x).

Note that if
\mu
is moreover
A
-invariant and
A
-ergodic with fiberwise Lyapunov exponents
\lambda^{F}_{1,\mu},\dots,\lambda^{F}_{p,\mu}\colon A\to\mathbb{R}
then

\lambda^{F}_{\mathrm{top}}(s,\mu)=\max_{1\leq i\leq p}\lambda^{F}_{i,\mu}(s).

We have the following facts which we invoke throughout our averaging procedures.

Claim 18.1
.

Let
s\in A
and let
\mu
be an
s
-invariant measure on
M^{\alpha}
.
Let
H=\{h^{t},t\in\mathbb{R}\}
be a one-parameter group contained in the centralizer of
s
in
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
.

(1)

The measure
H^{T}\ast\mu

is
s
-invariant for every
T\geq 0
.

(2)

Any weak-
*
limit point of

\{H^{T}\ast\mu\}
as
T\to\infty

is
s
-invariant.

(3)

Any weak-
*
limit point of

\{H^{T}\ast\mu\}
as
T\to\infty

is
H
-invariant.

(4)

\lambda^{F}_{\mathrm{top}}(s,H^{T}\ast\mu)=\lambda^{F}_{\mathrm{top}}(s,\mu)
for every
T\geq 0
.

(5)

If
\mu^{\prime}
is a weak-
*
limit point of

\{H^{T}\ast\mu\}
as
T\to\infty

then

\lambda^{F}_{\mathrm{top}}(s,\mu^{\prime})\geq\lambda^{F}_{\mathrm{top}}(s,\mu).

(1)
is clear from definition and
(2)
follows since the set of
s
-invariant measures is closed.

(3)
follows from (the proof of) the Krylov-Bogolyubov theorem (see
7.3
).

(4)
is a standard computation which follows from the compactness of
M^{\alpha}
and hence boundedness of the cocycle.
Indeed we have

\displaystyle\lambda^{F}_{\mathrm{top}}

\displaystyle(s,H^{T}\ast\mu)=\inf_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\int\log\|{D_{x}(s^{n})}{\restriction_{{F}}}\|\ d(H^{T}\ast\mu)(x)

\displaystyle=\inf_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\frac{1}{T}\int\int_{t=0}^{T}\log\|{D_{h^{t}\cdot x}(s^{n})}{\restriction_{{F}}}\|\ dt\,d\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\inf_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\frac{1}{T}\int\int_{t=0}^{T}\log\|{D_{h^{t}\cdot x}(h^{t}s^{n}h^{-t})}{\restriction_{{F}}}\|\ dt\,d\mu(x)

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\frac{1}{T}\int\int_{t=0}^{T}\log\|{D_{h^{t}\cdot x}(h^{-t})}{\restriction_{{F}}}\|

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+\log\|{D_{x}(s^{n})}{\restriction_{{F}}}\|+\log\|{D_{s\cdot x}(h^{t})}{\restriction_{{F}}}\|\ dt\,d\mu(x)

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\left(\int\log\|{D_{x}(s^{n})}{\restriction_{{F}}}\|\ d\mu(x)+2K\right)

where

K=\sup\left\{\log\|{D_{x}(h^{t})}{\restriction_{{F}}}\|:x\in M,t\in[-T,T]\right\}.

(5)
follows from the well-known fact that the average top Lyapunov exponent is upper-semicontinuous on the set of
s
-invariant measures (see for example
[
MR3289050
]
or
[
1608.04995
, Lemma 3.2(b)]
). Indeed, in the weak-
*
topology, for each
n
the function

\mu\mapsto\frac{1}{n}\int\log\|{D_{x}(s^{n})}{\restriction_{{F}}}\|\ d\mu(x)

is continuous. The pointwise infimum of a family of continuous functions is upper-semicontinuous.

Remark 18.2
.

Recall that a
Følner sequence
in a Lie group
H
equipped with a left-Haar measure
m_{H}
is a sequence
\{F_{n}\}
of Borel subsets
F_{n}\subset H
, with
0<m_{H}(F_{n})<\infty
, such that for every compact subset
Q\subset H
one has

\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{h\in Q}\dfrac{m_{H}\big((h\cdot F_{n})\triangle F_{n}\big)}{m_{H}(F_{n})}=0.

If
H
admits a Følner sequence then
H
is said to be
amenable
.
When
H=\mathbb{R}
, a Følner sequence is given by
F_{n}=[0,n]
. Examples of amenable groups include abelian groups, nilpotent groups, solvable groups, and compact groups. See
[
BdHV
]
for more details.

Consider
H
to be an amenable Lie subgroup of
G=\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
.
Given a Borel probability measure
\mu
on
M^{\alpha}
and a Følner sequence
\{F_{n}\}
in
H
we define

F_{n}\ast\mu:=\dfrac{1}{m_{H}(F_{n})}\int_{F_{n}}h_{*}\mu\ dm_{H}(h).

By a computation analogous to (
7.3
) in the proof of
7.3
, any weak-
*
limit point
\hat{\mu}
of the sequence
\{F_{n}\ast\mu\}
as
n\to\infty
is an
H
-invariant measure on
M^{\alpha}
. Moreover, properties analogous to those in Claim
18.1
hold when averaging an
s
-invariant measure
\mu
against a Følner sequence
\{F_{n}\}
in an amenable subgroup
H
contained in the centralizer
C_{G}(s)
of
s
. See
[
1608.04995
, Lemma 3.2]
for precise formulations.

18.2.
Averaging measures on
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})/\Gamma

When averaging probability measures on
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})/\Gamma
along 1-parameter unipotent subgroups we obtain additional properties of the limiting measures. The results stated in the following proposition are consequences of
Ratner’s measure classification and equidistribution theorems for unipotent flows
[
MR1262705
,
MR1054166
,
MR1075042
]
. See also
[
MR2158954
]
. We do not formulate Ratner’s theorems here but only the consequences we use in the remainder.

Proposition 18.3
.

Let
\hat{\mu}
be a Borel probability measure on
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})/\Gamma
.
For each 1-parameter root subgroup
U^{i,j}

(1)

the weak-
*
limit

U^{i,j}\ast\hat{\mu}:=\lim_{T\to\infty}\{(U^{i,j})^{T}\ast\hat{\mu}:T\geq 0\}

exists;

(2)

if
\hat{\mu}
is
A
-invariant, so is
U^{i,j}\ast\hat{\mu}
;

(3)

if
\hat{\mu}
is
A
-invariant and
A
-ergodic, the measure
U^{i,j}\ast\hat{\mu}
is
A
-ergodic;

(4)

if
\hat{\mu}
is
A
-invariant and
U^{i,j}
-invariant then
\hat{\mu}
is
U^{j,i}
-invariant.

Proposition

18.3
(1)
follows from Ratner’s measure classification and equidistribution theorems for unipotent flows. When
U
is higher-dimensional, we use an analogue of
Proposition

18.3
(1)
due to Shah
[
MR1291701
, Corollary 1.3]
.

Proposition

18.3
(2)
follows from the fact that
A
normalizes
U^{i,j}
and that the limit in
Proposition

18.3
(1)
exists and is hence unique.

Proposition

18.3
(4)
is a consequence of Theorem 9 in
[
MR1262705
]
or Proposition 2.1 in
[
MR1054166
]
.

Proposition

18.3
(3)
is a short argument that uses the
A
-invariance of
\hat{\mu}
and the pointwise ergodic theorem: Since there is
s\in A
such that
U^{i,j}
-orbits are contracted by
s
, by the pointwise ergodic theorem, the measurable hull of the partition into
U^{i,j}
-orbits refines the ergodic decomposition for
A
. (See
Proposition

8.12
and Theorem
C.4
in Appendix
C
.) Let
\eta
be the measurable hull of the partition into
U^{i,j}
-orbits and let
\{\hat{\mu}^{\eta}_{x}\}
be a family of conditional measures of
\hat{\mu}
for this partition. (Note that from Ratner’s equidistribution theorem, we have that
\hat{\mu}^{\eta}_{x}
is a homogeneous measure on a closed homogeneous submanifold.) If
\phi
is a bounded,
A
-invariant measurable function then for
\hat{\mu}
-a.e.
x
,
\phi
is constant
\hat{\mu}^{\eta}_{x}
-almost surely; in particular,

\phi(x)=\int\phi\ d\hat{\mu}^{\eta}_{x}

for
\hat{\mu}
-a.e.
x
. Then
x\mapsto\int\phi\ d\hat{\mu}^{\eta}_{x}
is a
\mu
-almost everywhere defined,
A
-invariant function. In particular,
x\mapsto\int\phi\ d\hat{\mu}^{\eta}_{x}
is constant
\mu
a.s. by ergodicity of
\mu
. It follows that
\phi
is constant
\hat{\mu}
-a.s. and ergodicity follows.

18.3.
Proof of Proposition
17.1

We outline the proof of
Proposition

17.1
.
Recall the notation introduced in Section
14.1
.
In particular,
\pi\colon M^{\alpha}\to G/\Gamma
is the canonical projection and
F=\ker(D\pi)
is the fiberwise tangent bundle of
M^{\alpha}
.
We write the derivative of translation by
g
in
M^{\alpha}
as
Dg
and the restriction to the fiber of
F
through
x\in M^{\alpha}
by
{D_{x}g}{\restriction_{{F(x)}}}.
Equip
M^{\alpha}
with any Riemannian metric and write

\|{Dg}{\restriction_{{F}}}\|=\sup_{x\in M^{\alpha}}\|{D_{x}g}{\restriction_{{F(x)}}}\|.

Let
K=\mathrm{SO}(n)
. We equip
G
with a right-invariant, left-
K
-invariant Riemannian metric and induced distance function
d(\cdot,\cdot)
. We note that relative to such a metric, all
A
-orbits are geodesically embedded in
G
. We have the following elementary claim which allows us to transfer exponential growth properties between the
\Gamma
-action on
M
and the
G
-action on the fibers of
M^{\alpha}
.

Claim 18.4
.

If
\Gamma\subset\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
is cocompact and if
M
is compact, then any action

\alpha\colon\Gamma\to\mathrm{Diff}^{1}(M)

has uniform subexponential growth of derivatives if and only if for every
\epsilon>0
there is a
C
such that for all
g\in\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
,

\|{Dg}{\restriction_{{F}}}\|\leq Ce^{\epsilon d(e,g)}.

With the above claim, we outline the main steps in the proof of
Proposition

17.1
.

Proof of
Proposition

17.1
.

We assume
\alpha\colon\Gamma\to\mathrm{Diff}^{1}(M)
fails to have uniform subexponential growth of derivatives. Then, by
18.4
, there exist
\epsilon>0
, integers
m_{n}\in\mathbb{N}
with
{m_{n}}\to\infty
, elements
g_{m_{n}}\in G
with
d(g_{m_{n}},e)={m_{n}}
, points
x_{m_{n}}\in M_{\alpha},
and unit vectors
v_{m_{n}}\in T_{x_{m_{n}}}M_{\alpha}
such that

\|D_{x_{m_{n}}}g_{m_{n}}(v_{m_{n}})\|\geq e^{\epsilon{m_{n}}}.

Let
UF
denote the unit sphere bundle in
F
and, given
g\in G
, let
UDg
denote the induced action on
UF
: given
x\in M^{\alpha}
and
v\in UF(x)
write

UD_{x}g(v)=\frac{D_{x}g(v)}{\|D_{x}g(v)\|}

and

UDg(x,v)=\left(g\cdot x,UD_{x}g(v)\right).

By the singular value decomposition of matrices, the group
G=\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
can be written as
G=KAK
where
K=\mathrm{SO}(n)
. (For general simple Lie groups
G
we use the Cartan decomposition). We can thus write each
g_{m_{n}}\in G
as

g_{m_{n}}=k_{n}a_{n}k^{\prime}_{n}

where
k_{n},k^{\prime}_{n}\in K
and
a_{n}\in A
. Write

x^{\prime}_{n}=k^{\prime}_{n}\cdot x_{m_{n}},\quad x^{\prime\prime}_{n}=a_{n}k^{\prime}_{n}\cdot x_{m_{n}},

v^{\prime}_{n}=UD_{x_{m_{n}}}k^{\prime}_{n}(v_{m_{n}}),\quad v^{\prime\prime}_{n}=UD_{x_{m_{n}}}(a_{n}k^{\prime}_{n})(v_{m_{n}}).

Then

\displaystyle\|

\displaystyle D_{x_{m_{n}}}g_{m_{n}}(v_{m_{n}})\|=\|D_{x_{n}^{\prime\prime}}k_{n}(v_{n}^{\prime\prime})\|\cdot\|D_{x_{n}^{\prime}}a_{n}(v_{n}^{\prime})\|\cdot\|D_{x_{m_{n}}}k^{\prime}_{n}(v_{m_{n}})\|

and so

\epsilon\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{{m_{n}}}\log\|D_{x_{m_{n}}}g_{m_{n}}(v_{m_{n}})\|=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{{m_{n}}}\log\|D_{x^{\prime}_{n}}a_{n}(v^{\prime}_{n})\|

as
\|{D_{x}k}{\restriction_{{F}}}\|
is uniformly bounded over all
k\in K
and
x\in M^{\alpha}
.

Note that

|{m_{n}}-d(a_{n},e)|=|d(g_{m_{n}},e)-d(a_{n},e)|\leq d(k_{n},e)+d(k^{\prime}_{n},e)

is uniformly bounded in
n
. Thus
{m_{n}}^{-1}d(a_{n},e)\to 1
. As
A\simeq\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
, for each
n
there is a unique
\tilde{a}_{n}
with
a_{n}=(\tilde{a}_{n})^{m_{n}}
; moreover, as
A
is geodesically embedded in
G
, we have
d(\tilde{a}_{n},e)\to 1
.

For each
n
, let
\nu_{n}
be the empirical measure on
UF
given by

\nu_{n}=\frac{1}{{m_{n}}}\sum_{j=0}^{{m_{n}}-1}(\tilde{a}_{n})^{j}_{*}\delta_{(x_{n}^{\prime},v_{n}^{\prime})}.

Taking a subsequence
\{n_{j}\}
, we may assume that
\nu_{n_{j}}
converges to some
\nu_{\infty}
and that
\tilde{a}_{n_{j}}
converges to some
s\in A
. Note that
d(s,e)=1
. Let
\overline{\mu}
denote the image of
\nu_{\infty}
under the natural projection
UF\to M^{\alpha}
. Adapting the proofs of
7.3
and
Proposition

7.2
one can show that

(1)

\nu_{\infty}
is
UD{s}
-invariant whence
\overline{\mu}
is
s
-invariant;

(2)

\lambda^{F}_{\mathrm{top}}(s,\overline{\mu})\geq\epsilon>0
.

Take a Følner sequence
\{F_{n}\}
in
A
and let
\tilde{\mu}
be any weak-
*
limit point of
\{F_{n}\ast\overline{\mu}\}
as
n\to\infty
. Then, from analogues of the properties in
18.1
for averaging over Følner sequences, we have that

(1)

\tilde{\mu}
is
A
-invariant;

(2)

\lambda^{F}_{\mathrm{top}}(s,\tilde{\mu})\geq\epsilon>0
.

We take
\mu^{\prime}
to be an
A
-ergodic component of
\tilde{\mu}
with
\lambda^{F}_{\mathrm{top}}(s,\mu^{\prime})\geq\epsilon>0
.
∎

18.4.
Proof of Proposition
17.2
for
\mathrm{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})

To simplify ideas, we outline the proof of Proposition
17.2
assuming that
\Gamma
is a cocompact lattice in
\mathrm{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})
. We perform two averaging procedures on the measure
\mu^{\prime}
from the hypotheses of Proposition
17.2
to obtain the measure
\mu
in the conclusion of Proposition
17.2
.

Proof of Proposition
17.2
for
\Gamma\subset\mathrm{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})
.

Take
\mu_{0}=\mu^{\prime}
to be the ergodic,
A
-invariant probability measure in the hypotheses of Proposition
17.2
with nonzero fiberwise exponent

\lambda^{F}_{j,\mu_{0}}\colon A\to\mathbb{R}
,
\lambda^{F}_{j,\mu_{0}}\ \neq 0
.

First averaging.

Consider the elements

s=\mathrm{diag}(\tfrac{1}{4},2,2)
and
\overline{s}=\mathrm{diag}(2,2,\tfrac{1}{4})

of
A\subset\mathrm{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})
. Note that
s
and
\overline{s}
are linearly independent and hence form a basis for
A\simeq\mathbb{R}^{2}
. As the linear functional
\lambda^{F}_{j,\mu_{0}}
is nonzero, either

\lambda^{F}_{j,\mu_{0}}(s)\neq 0
or
\lambda^{F}_{j,\mu_{0}}(\overline{s})\neq 0
.

Without loss of generality we may assume that

\lambda^{F}_{j,\mu_{0}}(s)\neq 0.

Take
s_{0}
to be either
s
or
s^{-1}
so that
\lambda^{F}_{j,\mu_{0}}(s_{0})>0
.

Consider the 1-parameter subgroup

U^{2,3}=\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
0&1&t\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right):t\in\mathbb{R}\right\}.

Note that
U^{2,3}
commutes with
s_{0}
.
Let
\mu_{1}
be any weak-
*
limit point of
\{(U^{2,3})^{T}\ast\mu\}
as
T\to\infty
. From Claim
18.1
,
\mu_{1}
is
s_{0}
-invariant and
\lambda^{F}_{\mathrm{top}}(s_{0},\mu_{1})\geq\lambda^{F}_{\mathrm{top}}(s_{0},\mu_{0}).

We now average
\mu_{1}
over a Følner sequence in
A
: identifying
A
with
\mathbb{R}^{2}
let
A^{T}=[0,T]\times[0,T]
define a Følner sequence
\{A^{T}\}
in
A
. Then

A^{T}\ast\mu_{1}:=\frac{1}{T^{2}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{T}(t_{1},t_{2})_{*}\mu_{1}\ d(t_{1},t_{2}).

Let
\mu_{2}
be any weak-
*
limit point of
\{A^{T}\ast\mu_{1}\}
as
T\to\infty
. Then, from facts analogous to those in Claim
18.1
,
\mu_{2}
is
A
-invariant and

\lambda^{F}_{\mathrm{top}}(s_{0},\mu_{2})\geq\lambda^{F}_{\mathrm{top}}(s_{0},\mu_{1})>0.

Note that
\mu_{2}
might no longer be
U^{2,3}
-invariant.

We investigate properties of the projection of each measure
\mu_{0},\mu_{1},
and
\mu_{2}
to
\mathrm{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})/\Gamma
. For each
j
, we denote by
\hat{\mu}_{j}=\pi_{*}(\mu_{j})
the image of
\mu_{j}
under the projection
\pi\colon M^{\alpha}\to\mathrm{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})/\Gamma
.

Observe that
\hat{\mu}_{1}=U^{2,3}\ast\hat{\mu}_{0}
is
U^{2,3}
-invariant. Since
\hat{\mu}_{0}
was
A
-invariant, from
Proposition

18.3
(2)
we have that
\hat{\mu}_{1}
is
A
-invariant and it follows that
\hat{\mu}_{1}=\hat{\mu}_{2}
so
\hat{\mu}_{2}
is
U^{2,3}
-invariant and
A
-invariant.
From
Proposition

18.3
(4)
,

\hat{\mu}_{2}
is invariant under the subgroup

\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\ast&0&0\\
0&\ast&\ast\\
0&\ast&\ast\end{array}\right)\right\}\subset\mathrm{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})

generated by
A
,
U^{2,3}
and
U^{3,2}
in
\mathrm{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})
.
Moreover, since
\hat{\mu}_{0}
was
A
-ergodic, from
Proposition

18.3
(3)
the measure
\hat{\mu}_{1}=\hat{\mu}_{2}
is
A
-ergodic.

Returning to
M^{\alpha}
, as
\lambda^{F}_{\mathrm{top}}(s_{0},\mu_{2})>0
and as
\hat{\mu}_{2}
is
A
-ergodic, we may replace
\mu_{2}
with an
A
-ergodic component
\mu_{2}^{\prime}
of
\mu_{2}
such that

(1)

\lambda^{F}_{\mathrm{top}}(s_{0},\mu_{2}^{\prime})>0
, and

(2)

the projection of
\mu_{2}^{\prime}
to
\mathrm{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})/\Gamma
is
\hat{\mu}_{2}
.

Let
\lambda^{F}_{1,\mu_{2}^{\prime}},\dots,\lambda^{F}_{p^{\prime},\mu_{2}^{\prime}}\colon A\to\mathbb{R}
denote the fiberwise Lyapunov exponents for the
A
-invariant,
A
-ergodic measure
\mu_{2}^{\prime}
.
Then
0<\lambda^{F}_{j^{\prime},\mu_{2}^{\prime}}(s_{0})=\lambda^{F}_{\mathrm{top}}(s_{0},\mu_{2}^{\prime})
for some
1\leq j^{\prime}\leq p^{\prime}
whence some fiberwise Lyapunov exponent
\lambda^{F}_{j^{\prime},\mu_{2}^{\prime}}\colon A\to\mathbb{R}
is a nonzero linear functional.

Second averaging.

Consider now the elements
s=(2,2,\frac{1}{4})
and
\overline{s}=(2,\frac{1}{4},2)
in
A
.
Again, either

\lambda^{F}_{j^{\prime},\mu_{2}^{\prime}}(s)\neq 0
or
\lambda^{F}_{j^{\prime},\mu_{2}^{\prime}}(\overline{s})\neq 0
.

Case 1:
\lambda^{F}_{j^{\prime},\mu_{2}^{\prime}}(s)\neq 0
.

Take
s_{1}=s
or
s_{1}=s^{-1}
so that
\lambda^{F}_{j^{\prime},\mu_{2}^{\prime}}(s_{1})>0
.
Consider the one-parameter group
U^{1,2}
which commutes with
s_{1}
. As above, any weak-
*
limit point
\mu_{3}
of

\{(U^{1,2})^{T}\ast\mu_{2}^{\prime}\}
as
T\to\infty
is
s_{1}
-invariant, with

\lambda^{F}_{\mathrm{top}}(s_{1},\mu_{3})\geq\lambda^{F}_{\mathrm{top}}(s_{1},\mu_{2}^{\prime})>0.

Let
\mu_{4}
be any weak-
*
limit point of
\{A^{T}\ast\mu_{3}\}
as
T\to\infty
(where
A^{T}\ast\mu_{3}
is as in the first averaging). Then
\mu_{4}
is
A
-invariant and

\lambda^{F}_{\mathrm{top}}(s_{1},\mu_{4})\geq\lambda^{F}_{\mathrm{top}}(s_{1},\mu_{3})>0.

We claim that the projection
\hat{\mu}_{4}
of
\mu_{4}
to
\mathrm{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})/\Gamma
is the Haar measure.
Since the groups
U^{1,2}
and
U^{3,2}
commute and since
\hat{\mu}_{2}
was
U^{3,2}
-invariant, it follows that
\hat{\mu}_{3}=U^{1,2}\ast\hat{\mu}_{2}
is
U^{3,2}
-invariant. Also, since
\hat{\mu}_{2}
was
A
-invariant,
Proposition

18.3
(2)
shows that
\hat{\mu}_{3}
is
A
-invariant. Thus
\hat{\mu}_{3}=\hat{\mu}_{4}
and
\hat{\mu}_{4}
is also invariant under the actions of
A
,
U^{1,2}
, and
U^{3,2}.

By
Proposition

18.3
(4)
it follows that

\hat{\mu}_{4}
is invariant under the groups
U^{2,1}
and
U^{2,3}
; in particular
\hat{\mu}_{4}
is invariant under the following subgroups of
\mathrm{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})
:

\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\ast&0&0\\
0&\ast&\ast\\
0&\ast&\ast\end{array}\right)\right\},\quad\quad\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\ast&\ast&0\\
\ast&\ast&0\\
0&0&\ast\end{array}\right)\right\}.

These two groups generate all of
\mathrm{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})
, and hence
\hat{\mu}_{4}
is the Haar measure.

Case 2:
\lambda^{F}_{j^{\prime},\mu_{2}^{\prime}}(\overline{s})\neq 0
.

Take
s_{1}=\overline{s}
or
s_{1}=\overline{s}^{-1}
so that
\lambda^{F}_{j^{\prime},\mu_{2}^{\prime}}(s_{1})>0
.
Consider the one-parameter group
U^{1,3}
which commutes with
s_{1}
. As above, any weak-
*
limit point
\mu_{3}
of

\{(U^{1,3})^{T}\ast\mu_{2}^{\prime}\}
as
T\to\infty
is
s_{1}
-invariant, with

\lambda^{F}_{\mathrm{top}}(s_{1},\mu_{3})\geq\lambda^{F}_{\mathrm{top}}(s_{1},\mu_{2}^{\prime})>0.

Let
\mu_{4}
be any weak-
*
limit point of
\{A^{T}\ast\mu_{3}\}
as
T\to\infty
. Then
\mu_{4}
is
A
-invariant and

\lambda^{F}_{\mathrm{top}}(s_{1},\mu_{4})\geq\lambda^{F}_{\mathrm{top}}(s_{1},\mu_{3})>0.

Again, we claim that
\hat{\mu}_{4}=U^{1,3}\ast\hat{\mu}_{2}
is the Haar measure.
Since the groups
U^{1,3}
and
U^{2,3}
commute, it follows that
\hat{\mu}_{3}
is
U^{2,3}
-invariant.
Also, since
\hat{\mu}_{2}
was
A
-invariant,
Proposition

18.3
(2)
shows that
\hat{\mu}_{3}
is
A
-invariant. Thus
\hat{\mu}_{3}=\hat{\mu}_{4}
and
\hat{\mu}_{4}
is also invariant under the actions of
A
,

U^{1,3}
and
U^{2,3}
.
By
Proposition

18.3
(4)
it follows that
\hat{\mu}_{4}
is invariant under the following subgroups of
\mathrm{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})
:

\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\ast&0&0\\
0&\ast&\ast\\
0&\ast&\ast\end{array}\right)\right\},\quad\quad\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\ast&0&\ast\\
0&\ast&0\\
\ast&0&\ast\end{array}\right)\right\}.

Again, these two groups generate all of
\mathrm{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})
, and hence
\hat{\mu}_{4}
is the Haar measure.

Completion of proof.

In either Case 1 or Case 2, since the Haar measure
\hat{\mu}_{4}
is
A
-ergodic, we may take an
A
-ergodic component
\mu_{4}^{\prime}
of
\mu_{4}
projecting to the Haar measure with

\lambda^{F}_{\mathrm{top}}(s_{1},\mu_{4}^{\prime})>0.

If
\lambda^{F}_{1,\mu_{4}^{\prime}},\dots,\lambda^{F}_{p^{\prime\prime},\mu_{4}^{\prime}}\colon A\to\mathbb{R}
denote the fiberwise Lyapunov exponents for the
A
-invariant,
A
-ergodic measure
\mu_{4}^{\prime}
then
0<\lambda^{F}_{j^{\prime\prime},\mu_{4}^{\prime}}(s_{1})=\lambda^{F}_{\mathrm{top}}(s_{1},\mu_{4}^{\prime})
for some
1\leq j^{\prime\prime}\leq p^{\prime\prime}
whence some fiberwise Lyapunov exponent
\lambda^{F}_{j^{\prime\prime},\mu_{4}^{\prime}}\colon A\to\mathbb{R}
is a nonzero linear functional.

This completes the proof of Proposition
17.2
.
∎

18.5.
Modifications of the proof of Proposition
17.2
in
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})

When
\Gamma
is a cocompact lattice in
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
we replace the first averaging step with a more complicated averaging.

First averaging.

We again take
\mu_{0}=\mu^{\prime}
to be the
A
-invariant measure in
Proposition

17.2
with nonzero fiberwise exponent

\lambda^{F}_{j,\mu_{0}}\colon A\to\mathbb{R}
,
\lambda^{F}_{j,\mu_{0}}\ \neq 0
.

Without loss of generality (by conjugating by a permutation matrix) we may assume that for the element

s=\mathrm{diag}(\tfrac{1}{2^{n-1}},2,\dots,2)

of
A\subset\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
, we have

\lambda^{F}_{j,\mu_{0}}(s)\neq 0.

Take
s_{0}
to be either
s,
or
s^{-1}
so that
\lambda^{F}_{j,\mu_{0}}(s_{0})>0
.

Consider the unipotent subgroup
U\subset\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
of matrices of the form

U=\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}1&0&0&\cdots&0\\
0&1&\ast&\cdots&\ast\\
\vdots&&\ddots&&\vdots\\
0&0&\cdots&1&\ast\\
0&0&\cdots&0&1\\
\end{array}\right)\right\}.

Note that
U
commutes with
s_{0}
.

Let
\{F_{n}\}
be a Følner sequence in
U
and let
\mu_{1}
be any weak-
*
limit point of
\{F_{n}\ast\mu_{0}\}
as
n\to\infty
where

F_{n}\ast\mu_{0}=\frac{1}{m_{U}(F_{n})}\int_{F_{n}}u_{*}\mu_{0}\ du.

From facts analogous to those in Claim
18.1
, we have that
\mu_{1}
is
s_{0}
-invariant and
\lambda^{F}_{\mathrm{top}}(s_{0},\mu_{1})\geq\lambda^{F}_{\mathrm{top}}(s_{0},\mu_{0})>0.

Moreover, as
U
is higher-dimensional, we use
[
MR1291701
, Corollary 1.3]
rather than
Proposition

18.3
(1)
to conclude (at least for certain Følner sequences
\{F_{n}\}
in
U
with nice geometry) that the projection
\hat{\mu}_{1}
of
\mu_{1}
to
G/\Gamma
is the limit

\hat{\mu}_{1}=\lim F_{n}\ast\hat{\mu}_{0}

and is
A
-invariant, ergodic, and
U
-invariant.

We again average
\mu_{1}
over a Følner sequence of the form

A^{T}=[0,T]\times\dots\times[0,T]

in
A
(identified with
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
)
and let
\mu_{2}
be any weak-
*
limit point of
\{A^{T}\ast\mu_{1}\}
as
T\to\infty
. Then
\mu_{2}
is
A
-invariant and

\lambda^{F}_{\mathrm{top}}(s_{0},\mu_{2})\geq\lambda^{F}_{\mathrm{top}}(s_{0},\mu_{1})>0.

Again, we have equality of the projected measures
\hat{\mu}_{1}=\hat{\mu}_{2}
so
\hat{\mu}_{2}
is
U
-invariant and
A
-invariant.
From
Proposition

18.3
(4)
,

\hat{\mu}_{2}
is also invariant under the subgroup

H=\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}1&0&0&\cdots&0\\
0&\ast&\ast&&\ast\\
0&\ast&\ast&&\ast\\
\vdots&&&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&\ast&\ast&\cdots&\ast\\
\end{array}\right)\right\}.

As
\hat{\mu}_{2}
is
A
-ergodic, we may replace
\mu_{2}
with an
A
-ergodic component
\mu_{2}^{\prime}
of
\mu_{2}
such that

(1)

\lambda^{F}_{\mathrm{top}}(s_{0},\mu_{2}^{\prime})>0
, and

(2)

the projection of
\mu_{2}^{\prime}
to
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})/\Gamma
is
\hat{\mu}_{2}
.

Then, if
\lambda^{F}_{1,\mu_{2}^{\prime}},\dots,\lambda^{F}_{p^{\prime},\mu_{2}^{\prime}}\colon A\to\mathbb{R}
denote the fiberwise Lyapunov exponents for
\mu_{2}^{\prime}
, we have
0<\lambda^{F}_{j^{\prime},\mu_{2}^{\prime}}(s_{0})=\lambda^{F}_{\mathrm{top}}(s_{0},\mu_{2}^{\prime})
for some
1\leq j^{\prime}\leq p^{\prime}
.

Second averaging.

Consider now the roots
\beta^{1,2}
and
\beta^{1,n}
of
G
. Since
\beta^{1,2}
and
\beta^{1,n}
are not proportional, at most one of

\beta^{1,2}
and
\beta^{1,n}
is proportional to
\lambda^{F}_{j^{\prime},\mu_{2}^{\prime}}
. In particular, we may find either
s
or
\overline{s}
in
A
such that

(1)

\beta^{1,2}(s)=0
but
\lambda^{F}_{j^{\prime},\mu_{2}^{\prime}}(s)\neq 0
; or

(2)

\beta^{1,n}(\overline{s})=0
but
\lambda^{F}_{j^{\prime},\mu_{2}^{\prime}}(\overline{s})\neq 0
.

The two cases in the second averaging step of Section
18.4
are then identical to the above, where we either average over the 1-parameter group
U^{1,2}
in the case
\lambda^{F}_{j^{\prime},\mu_{2}^{\prime}}(s)\neq 0
or
U^{1,n}
in the case
\lambda^{F}_{j,\mu_{2}^{\prime}}(\overline{s})\neq 0
. The structure theory of
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
will then imply that the measure obtained after the second averaging projects to the Haar measure.

\bigast
19.  Zimmer’s conjecture for actions by lattices in other Lie groups

Consider a connected, simple Lie group
G
with finite center. Let
\Gamma\subset G
be a cocompact lattice. The proof of Theorem
11.6
discussed above, particularly the use of Theorem
15.1
in Section
17.3
can be adapted almost verbatim to show the following. See also
[
Cantat
]
where Theorem
19.1
is stated and given a mostly self-contained proof.

Theorem 19.1
.

Let
G
be a connected, simple Lie group
G
with finite center and rank at least
2
. Let
\Gamma\subset G
be a cocompact lattice. Let
M
be a compact manifold.

(1)

If
\dim(M)<\mathrm{rank}(G)

then any homomorphism
\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{Diff}^{2}(M)
has finite image.

(2)

In addition, if
\mathrm{vol}
is a volume form on
M

and if
\dim(M)\leq\mathrm{rank}(G)
then any homomorphism
\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{Diff}^{2}_{\mathrm{vol}}(M)
has finite image.

As mentioned in
11.4
, Theorem
19.1
holds for
C^{1}
actions; see Theorem
11.7
.

Theorem
19.1
fails to give the optimal dimension bounds for the analogue of Conjecture
11.2
given in Conjecture
11.3
for actions by lattices in Lie groups other than
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
. See Table
1
for various conjectured critical dimensions arising in Zimmer’s conjecture for other Lie groups.

To state the most general (as of 2018) result towards solving Conjecture
11.3
, to any simple Lie group
G
, we associate a non-negative integer
r(G)
. See
[
1608.04995
, Section 2.2]
or
Footnote

4
, page
4
, for equivalent definitions of
r(G)
and Table
1
for values of
r(G)
in various examples of
G
. For actions of lattices in a general Lie group
G
, the main result of
[
1608.04995
]
, as well as the announced extension, gives finiteness of the action up to the critical dimension
r(G)
.

Theorem 19.2

(
[
1608.04995
]
cocompact case;
[
BFHWM
]
nonuniform case)
.

Let
\Gamma\subset G
be a lattice in a higher-rank simple Lie group
G
with finite center. Let
M
be a compact manifold.

(1)

If
\dim(M)<r(G)

then any homomorphism
\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{Diff}^{1+\beta}(M)
has finite image.

(2)

In addition, if
\mathrm{vol}
is a volume form on
M

and if
\dim(M)=r(G)
then any homomorphism
\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{Diff}^{1+\beta}_{\mathrm{vol}}(M)
has finite image.

When
G
is exceptional or not a split real form, our number
r(G)
is lower than the conjectured critical dimension in Conjecture
11.3
(1)
and
(2)
. However, for lattices in all Lie groups that are non-exceptional, split real forms
Theorem

19.2
confirms Conjecture
11.3
(1)
and
(2)
. For instance, for actions by lattices in symplectic groups we have the following.

Theorem 19.3

(
[
1608.04995
, Theorem 1.3]
cocompact case;
[
BFHWM
]
nonuniform case)
.

For
n\geq 2
, if
M
is a compact manifold with
\dim(M)<2n-1
and if
\Gamma\subset\mathrm{Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
is a lattice then any homomorphism
\alpha\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{Diff}^{2}(M)
has finite image. In addition, if
\dim(M)=2n-1
then any homomorphism
\alpha\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{Diff}_{\mathrm{vol}}^{2}(M)
has finite image.

Similarly, for actions by lattices in split orthogonal groups we have the following.

Theorem 19.4

(
[
1608.04995
, Theorem 1.4]
cocompact case;
[
BFHWM
]
nonuniform case)
.

Let
M
be a compact manifold.

(1)

For
n\geq 4
, if
\Gamma\subset\mathrm{SO}(n,n)
is a lattice and if
\dim(M)<2n-2
then any homomorphism
\alpha\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{Diff}^{2}(M)
has finite image. If
\dim(M)=2n-2
then any homomorphism
\alpha\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{Diff}^{2}_{\mathrm{vol}}(M)
has finite image.

(2)

For
n\geq 3
, if
\Gamma\subset\mathrm{SO}(n,n+1)
is a lattice and if
\dim(M)<2n-1
then any homomorphism
\alpha\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{Diff}^{2}(M)
has finite image. If
\dim(M)=2n-1

then any homomorphism
\alpha\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{Diff}^{2}_{\mathrm{vol}}(M)
has finite image.

For actions by lattices
\Gamma
in simple Lie groups that are not split real forms such as
G=\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{C})
,
\mathrm{SO}(n,m)
for
m\geq n+2
, or
\mathrm{SU}(n,m)
, Theorem
19.2
above (the main result of
[
1608.04995
]
for cocompact case,
[
BFHWM
]
in general) gives finiteness of all actions on manifolds whose dimension is below a certain critical dimension. However, this critical dimension may be below the dimension conjectured by the analogue of Conjecture
11.3
for these groups. See Table
1
.

Part IV
A selection of other measure rigidity results

20.
Nonuniformly hyperbolic
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
-actions

Instead of considering
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-actions by automorphisms of
\mathbb{T}^{3}
as in Theorem
2.8
, we might consider
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-actions on the torus
\mathbb{T}^{3}
generated by two commuting diffeomorphisms
f,g\colon\mathbb{T}^{3}\to\mathbb{T}^{3}
.

Recall that for any homeomorphism
f\colon\mathbb{T}^{3}\to\mathbb{T}^{3}
there exists a unique
M\in\mathrm{GL}(3,\mathbb{Z})
so that any lift
\tilde{f}\colon\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
of
f
is of the form

\tilde{f}(x)=Mx+\psi(x)

where
\psi\colon\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
is
\mathbb{Z}^{3}
-periodic. The linear map
M
can also be seen as the induced action of
f
on first homology of
\mathbb{T}^{3}
. We call
M
the
linear data
of
f
.
By a theorem of Franks
[
MR0271990
]
, if
M
has no eigenvalues of modulus 1 then there is a continuous, surjective
h\colon\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{T}^{d}
, homotopic to the identity, such that

(20.1)

h\circ f=L_{M}\circ h

where
L_{M}\colon\mathbb{T}^{3}\to\mathbb{T}^{3}
is the induced automorphism of the torus; such a map
h
is called a
semiconjugacy
.

If
f,g\colon\mathbb{T}^{3}\to\mathbb{T}^{3}
are commuting homeomorphisms with linear data
A
and
B
, respectively, one can verify that
A
and
B
commute. Indeed if
\tilde{f}(x)=Ax+\psi(x)
and
\tilde{g}(x)=Bx+\phi(x)
are lifts of
f
and
g
, respectively, then

\tilde{f}\circ\tilde{g}(x)=ABx+A\phi(x)+\psi(x)

and

\tilde{g}\circ\tilde{f}(x)=BAx+B\psi(x)+\phi(x)

are both lifts of
f\circ g=g\circ f
whence
AB=BA
.

If
A
has no eigenvalues of modulus 1, we may take a map
h\colon\mathbb{T}^{3}\to\mathbb{T}^{3}
with

h\circ f=L_{A}\circ h

as in (
20.1
). Following
[
MR2643892
, Lemma 1]
(correcting
[
MR2261075
, Lemma 1.2]
) the map
h
conjugates the
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-action generated by
f
and
g
to an affine action on
\mathbb{T}^{3}
whose linear part is generated by
L_{A}
and
L_{B}
.
That is, if
\alpha\colon\mathbb{Z}^{2}\to\mathrm{Diff}(\mathbb{T}^{3})
is the non-linear action

\alpha(n_{1},n_{2})=f^{n_{1}}g^{n_{2}}

then there is an affine action
\alpha_{0}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{2}\to\mathrm{Diff}(\mathbb{T}^{3})
of the form

\alpha_{0}(n_{1},n_{2})(x)=L_{A}^{n_{1}}L_{B}^{n_{2}}(x)+v_{(n_{1},n_{2})}

for some
v_{(n_{1},n_{2})}\in\mathbb{T}^{3}
such that for all
(n_{1},n_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}

(20.2)

h\circ\alpha(n_{1},n_{2})=\alpha_{0}(n_{1},n_{2})\circ h.

We note that the translation term
(n_{1},n_{2})\mapsto v_{(n_{1},n_{2})}
is a cocycle:

v_{(n_{1},n_{2})+(m_{1},m_{2})}=L_{A}^{n_{1}}L_{B}^{n_{2}}v_{(m_{1},m_{2})}+v_{(n_{1},n_{2})}.

Moreover, the action
\alpha_{0}
has a fixed point if and only if
v_{(n_{1},n_{2})}
is a coboundary:

v_{(n_{1},n_{2})}=L_{A}^{n_{1}}L_{B}^{n_{2}}\eta-\eta

for some
\eta\in\mathbb{T}^{3}
.
The presence of the translation term
v_{(n_{1},n_{2})}
is due to the non-uniqueness of the map
h
satisfying (
20.1
). However, all maps
h
satisfying (
20.1
) differ by a translation by an element of the finite set of fixed points for
L_{A}
. Thus, the translation terms
v_{(n_{1},n_{2})}
take only finitely many possible values. See discussion in
[
MR2643892
]
for more details. We note that it is possible to construct genuinely affine Anosov actions
\alpha_{0}
without fixed points as in
Remark

10.6
. See for example
[
MR1236179
, Theorem 2]
. In particular, it may be that the action
\alpha
is not semiconjugate to any action by automorphisms. However, restricting to a subgroup
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{Z}^{2}
of finite index, one has that
{\alpha_{0}}{\restriction_{{\Sigma}}}\colon\Sigma\to\mathrm{Diff}(\mathbb{T}^{3})
is an action by automorphisms:

\alpha_{0}(n_{1},n_{2})(x)=L_{A}^{n_{1}}L_{B}^{n_{2}}(x)

for all
(n_{1},n_{2})\in\Sigma
.

If
f
is Anosov then its linear data
A
is known to have no eigenvalues of modulus 1 and the map
h
in (
20.1
) is a homeomorphism. Suppose further that
f
and
g
generate a “genuine”
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-action so that the group of matrices generated by their linear data
A
and
B
is not virtually cyclic. This implies that the linear action generated by
L_{A}
and
L_{B}
satisfies Theorem
2.8
.
Restricted to a finite-index subgroup, the map
h
conjugates the action
\alpha
to a linear action of the type of action considered in Example
2.6
. Since invertible maps preserve entropy, Theorem
2.8
classifies all positive entropy measures that are jointly
f
- and
g
-invariant. We remark also that under the above assumptions, from
[
MR2318497
]
, we know in this setting that the conjugating map
h
satisfying (
20.2
) is smooth.
Note that the assumption that the group of matrices generated by
A
and
B
is not virtually cyclic is essential; for instance, if
g
is a power of
f
we expect no rigidity of jointly invariant measures or smoothness of the conjugacy
h
.

If neither
f
nor
g
is Anosov, the map
h
in (
20.2
) may be non-invertible. In particular, there may exist ergodic,
\alpha
-invariant measures
\mu
on
\mathbb{T}^{3}
with
h_{\mu}(f)>0
such that the push-forward measure
h_{*}(\mu)
has zero entropy for
\alpha_{0}(\mathbf{n})
for every
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}.

Note however that if

h_{h_{*}(\mu)}(\alpha_{0}(\mathbf{n}))>0

for some
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
then
h_{*}(\mu)
is necessarily Haar by Theorem
2.8
.

When the map
h
in (
20.2
) is non-invertible, analysis of measures invariant under the affine action
\alpha_{0}
gives less information about measures jointly invariant under
f
and
g
. However, the method of proof of Theorem
2.8
can be adapted to study measures jointly invariant under
f
and
g
; in particular, one can show the following theorem which is a simplified version of the main results of
[
MR2261075
,
MR2285730
]
.

Theorem 20.1

(
[
MR2261075
,
MR2285730
]
)
.

Suppose
f,g\colon\mathbb{T}^{3}\to\mathbb{T}^{3}
are commuting
C^{1+\beta}
diffeomorphisms. Suppose the linear data of
f
and
g
are, respectively, the matrices
A
and
B
in Example
2.6
.
Then any ergodic probability measure
\mu
that is invariant under both
f
and
g
and such that
h_{*}(\mu)
is Haar is absolutely continuous with respect to the Riemannian volume on
\mathbb{T}^{3}
. Such a measure always exists and is, moreover, unique.

For actions on more general manifolds, there may be no a priori semiconjugacy between the nonlinear action and an affine Anosov action. However, under certain dynamical hypotheses on the action, the structure of the algebraic toral action can be reconstructed. Consider a
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-action
\alpha
on a 3-manifold
M
generated by two commuting diffeomorphisms
f,g\colon M\to M
. Given an ergodic,
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-invariant probability measure
\mu
, one can define Lyapunov exponent functionals for the
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-action as in Theorem
7.4
. These extend to linear functionals on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Note that there are at most 3 (the dimension of
M
) Lyapunov exponent functionals. Under some genericity assumptions on the Lyapunov exponent functionals, an analogue of
Theorem

2.8
and
Theorem

20.1
was obtained in
[
MR2811602
]
.

Theorem 20.2

(
[
MR2811602
]
)
.

Let
\alpha
be a
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-action by
C^{1+\beta}
-diffeomorphisms of a 3-manifold and let
\mu
be an ergodic,
\alpha
-invariant measure. Assume there are 3 nonzero, Lyapunov exponent functionals
\lambda_{\mu}^{1},\lambda_{\mu}^{2},\lambda_{\mu}^{3}
and that no pair of exponents is proportional.

If some element
\alpha(n_{1},n_{2})
has positive entropy with respect to
\mu
, then
\mu
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Riemannian volume on
M
.

In
[
MR3503686
]
, it is shown in the setting of Theorem
20.2
that one can reconstruct an action by (infra-)toral automorphism and a measurable semiconjugacy
h
between the non-linear action
\alpha
(restricted to a finite-index subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
) and the algebraic action. Moreover, the semiconjugacy
h
takes (an ergodic component of)
\mu
to the Lebesgue measure on the (infra-)torus, is differentiable along stable manifolds, and is differentiable (in the Whitney sense) off sets of arbitrarily small measure.
This, in particular, implies that the exponents
\lambda^{i}_{\mu}(n)
are logarithms of algebraic numbers for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.

21.
Invariant measures for Cartan flows

In Section
13.2
, we introduced an important example of a higher-rank, continuous-time algebraic Anosov action, namely, the diagonal action (or Cartan flow) on a higher-rank semisimple homogeneous space. We review its properties, referring back to Section
13
for details.

Example 21.1
.

Let
G=\mathrm{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})
and let
\Gamma=\mathrm{SL}(3,\mathbb{Z})
or any lattice in
G
.
Let
X
denote the coset space
X=G/\Gamma
. This is an
8
dimensional manifold (which is noncompact for
\Gamma=\mathrm{SL}(3,\mathbb{Z})
.)

G
acts on
X
by left translation.

The group
A\subset G
of diagonal matrices with positive entries is isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
The action
\alpha\colon A\times X\to X
of
A
on
X
is given by
\alpha(s)(x)=sx.

There are
6
roots
\beta^{i,j}\colon A\to\mathbb{R}
given by
\beta^{i,j}(\mathrm{diag}(e^{t_{1}},e^{t_{1}},e^{t_{3}}))=t_{i}-t_{j}
each with an associated root subgroup
U^{i,j}\subset G
. For
x\in X
,
W^{i,j}(x)
is the orbit of
x
under the 1-parameter group
U^{i,j}
:

W^{i,j}(x)=\{U^{i,j}\cdot x:t\in\mathbb{R}\}.

For
s\in A
, the action
\alpha(s)
dilates distances in
W^{i,j}(x)
by exactly
e^{\beta^{i,j}(s)}
.

One might ask whether an analogue of Theorem
2.8
holds in Example
21.1
. That is, if
\mu
is an ergodic,
A
-invariant probability measure on
X
such that there is some
s\in A
with
h_{\mu}(\alpha(s))>0
, is
\mu
necessarily the Haar measure on
X
or on a homogeneous submanifold of
X
?

The answer is no. The extension of the proof of Theorem
2.8
breaks down in this setting as the trick in Lemma
6.6
fails.
Indeed, for every root
\beta^{i,j}
, we have that
\beta^{i,j}
and
\beta^{j,i}=-\beta^{i,j}
are negatively proportional.
Moreover, explicit examples of diagonally invariant measures with positive entropy (for some element of the diagonal) on spaces of the form
\mathrm{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})/\Gamma
for certain (cocompact) lattices
\Gamma
were constructed by Mary Rees in an unpublished manuscript. See
[
MR1989231
]
for detailed constructions of such measures.

A related problem is the classification of orbit closures for the
A
-action on
X=\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})/\Gamma
. Rees’s construction yields
A
-orbit closures in
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})/\Gamma
that are non-homogeneous and of intermediate Hausdorff dimensions for certain lattices
\Gamma
. It was shown in
[
MR2630049
]
that there exist non-homogeneous
A^{\prime}
-orbit closures in
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})/\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{Z})
(for
n\geq 6
) for certain higher-rank subgroups
A^{\prime}
of the full diagonal group
A
.

Returning to the classification of invariant measures, in the case that
\Gamma=\mathrm{SL}(3,\mathbb{Z})
, the
Margulis conjecture
asserts that all ergodic
A
-invariant measures
\mu
on
X
should be algebraic. See
[
MR2247967
, Conjecture 1.1]
and discussion in
[
MR1754775
, §1.2]
. For measures with positive entropy, this conjecture was solved in
[
MR2247967
]
(see
Theorem

21.5
below). We outline the main results used in
[
MR2247967
]
, namely the
high
and
low entropy
methods.

To discuss the high and low entropy methods, first note that there are some key differences in the structure of the foliations in this setting versus the setting of Example
2.6
. First note that any two transverse foliations
W^{i},W^{j}
of the torus
\mathbb{T}^{3}
by lines are jointly integrable; that is there is a foliation of
\mathbb{T}^{3}
by planes
W^{i,j}
with
W^{i}(x)\subset W^{i,j}(x)
and
W^{j}(x)\subset W^{i,j}(x)
for all
x
. This follows as
\mathbb{T}^{3}
has an abelian group structure. In
X=\mathrm{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})/\Gamma
, Lyapunov foliations do not jointly integrate as the corresponding subgroups may not commute. For instance, the subgroups
U^{1,2}
and
U^{2,3}
do not commute and thus the foliations
W^{1,2}(x)
and
W^{2,3}(x)
do not jointly integrate. This is the key idea behind the
high entropy method
.
Moreover, translations along Lyapunov directions
E^{i}
are isometries in the torus
\mathbb{T}^{3}
.
In
X=G/\Gamma
, translation by an element of a 1-parameter subgroup
U^{i,j}
is not isometric; there is some polynomial shearing. This is a key step in the proof of Ratner’s measure classification theorem for unipotent flows (see
[
MR1262705
]
) and is also a key idea in the
low entropy method
.

We state the versions of the high entropy and low entropy methods for Example
21.1
. Given a measure
\mu
on
X
, for
i\neq j
let
\mu^{i,j}_{x}
denote the locally finite leaf-wise measures obtained by conditioning
\mu
along
W^{i,j}
-manifolds.

Theorem 21.2

(High entropy method
[
MR1989231
]
)
.

Let
\mu
be an ergodic,
A
-invariant measure on
X=\mathrm{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})/\Gamma
.
Let
i,j,
and
k
be distinct elements of
\{1,2,3\}
. If
\mu^{i,j}_{x}
and
\mu^{j,k}_{x}
are nonatomic for a positive measure set of
x
then
\mu
is
U^{i,k}
-invariant.

Note that the subgroups
U^{i,j}
and
U^{j,k}
do not commute; precisely, we have
[U^{i,j},U^{j,k}]=U^{i,k}
. Theorem
21.2
states that if both Lyapunov exponents
\beta^{i,j}
and
\beta^{j,k}
contribute entropy (so that
\mu^{i,j}_{x}
and
\mu^{j,k}_{x}
are nonatomic) then the measure
\mu
is invariant under their bracket
U^{i,k}=[U^{i,j},U^{j,k}]
. The noncommutativity of
U^{i,j}
and
U^{j,k}
is essential in the proof of the theorem.

From Theorem
21.2
one can derive the following corollary.

Theorem 21.3

(
[
MR1989231
, Theorem 4.1]
)
.

Let
\Gamma\subset\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
be a lattice and
let
\mu
be an ergodic,
A
-invariant measure on
X=\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})/\Gamma
such that for every nontrivial
s\in A
,

h_{\mu}(\alpha(s))>0.

Then
\mu
is the Haar measure on
X
.

We note that the statements of Theorems
21.2
and
21.3
are specific for the group
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
. More general high entropy methods appear in
[
MR2191228
]
.

The low entropy method is a bit more difficult to state. We state a version for
X=\mathrm{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})/\Gamma
. For each
i\neq j
, let
A^{\prime}_{i,j}
denote the kernel of
\beta^{i,j}
and let
C(A^{\prime}_{i,j})
denote the centralizer of
A^{\prime}_{i,j}
in
G=\mathrm{SL}(3,\mathbb{R}).

Theorem 21.4

(Low entropy method,
[
MR2247967
, Theorem 2.3.]
)
.

Let
\mu
be an ergodic,
A
-invariant measure on
X=\mathrm{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})/\Gamma
.
If
\mu^{i,j}_{x}
and
\mu^{j,i}_{x}
are nonatomic for some
i,j
and
\mu^{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}_{x}
is atomic for all other pairs
i^{\prime},j^{\prime}
then either

(1)

\mu
is
U^{i,j}
-invariant, or

(2)

there is
x_{0}\in X
and
s\in A^{\prime}_{i,j}
with
\alpha(s)(x_{0})=x_{0}
such that
\mu
is supported on the orbit
C(A^{\prime}_{i,j})x_{0}
.

Conclusion
(2)
is specific for the case that
X
is of the form
X=\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})/\Gamma
for
n=3
. For
n>3
, the appropriate version of
(2)
is slightly more complicated.

To show that
Theorem

21.2
and
Theorem

21.4
cover all cases it is shown
[
MR2247967
, Corollary 3.4]
for any ergodic,
A
-invariant measure
\mu
and every pair
i,j
, the measure
\mu^{i,j}_{x}
is nonatomic if and only if
\mu^{j,i}_{x}
is nonatomic. Thus every
A
-invariant measure
\mu
with positive entropy is considered in either
Theorem

21.2
and
Theorem

21.4
.

To apply the low entropy method, one typically does additional work to rule out conclusion
(2)
in
Theorem

21.4
. Note that conclusion
(2)
of
Theorem

21.4
occurs in Rees’s examples so it can not be ruled out for all lattices
\Gamma
. However, for certain lattices, it can be shown that
(2)
of Theorem
21.4
does not happen. In particular, this is verified for
\Gamma=\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{Z})
in
[
MR2247967
]
.
The high and low entropy method combine to give the following.

Theorem 21.5

(
[
MR2247967
, Theorem 1.3, Corollary 1.4]
)
.

Let
\mu
be an ergodic,
A
-invariant measure on
X=\mathrm{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})/\mathrm{SL}(3,\mathbb{Z})
. Assume
\mu
has positive entropy for some nontrivial element of
A
. Then
\mu
is the Haar measure on
X
.

Note the conclusion that
\mu
is the Haar measure above follows as we assume
n=3
which is prime. For the general result on
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})/\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{Z})
, the conclusion is that
\mu
is algebraic.

The study of invariant measures and orbit closures for various subgroups
H\subset\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
acting on
X=\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})/\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{Z})
is related to several important problems in number theory. See for instance the proof of Margulis’s proof
[
MR882782
,
MR993328
]
of the Oppenheim conjecture which reduces to the study of
H=\mathrm{SO}(2,1)
-orbit closures in
\mathrm{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})/\mathrm{SL}(3,\mathbb{Z})
. See
[
MR2158954
, Section 1.2]
for further discussion. One motivation for studying
A
-invariant measures on
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})/\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{Z})
is its relationship to Littlewood’s conjecture. An important consequence of
Theorem

21.5
is that the set of values for which Littlewood’s conjecture fails has Hausdorff dimension zero. See
[
MR2247967
]
as well as
[
MR1754775
, §1.2]
and
[
MR2358379
]
for details.
